Independent English · B1 Teacher's Guide

Independent English (B1 Intermediate)

The full teaching manual for a 13-week adult-education B1 class (26 classes × 90 minutes): lesson stages and timing, board plans, drilling and concept-check questions, pair-work, error correction, differentiation, and L1 watch-outs.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 1 — Time, Experience & Past Habits

Three classes that open the B1 level. Learners already know the present perfect, the past simple and used to from A2. The B1 skill is choosing: present perfect or past simple? Present perfect simple or continuous? used to or would? Class 1 also welcomes a new group and gives you a quick A2 diagnostic. By the end of the module learners can open a topic with an experience, give the details, talk about what they have been doing lately, and discuss how life has changed.

3 lessons Lessons 1.1–1.3 Classes C1–C3 Weeks 1–2 90 min per class CEFR B1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups, discussion) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-1-time-experience.html. Print materials/module-1/classroom-materials.html before C1. It holds the flashcards, the welcome question cards and the A2 diagnostic grid, the new identity cards, the timeline interview cards, the "Have you ever…?" Find Someone Who, the "What have you been doing?" clue game, the "How life has changed" discussion cards and roles, and all the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences and dialogues again at home.
  • This is extension, not a first meeting. A2 taught each form on its own (present perfect for experiences in A2 Module 4, used to in A2 Module 5). B1 puts the forms side by side and asks learners to choose. Spend less time on form and more on the choice, the reason, and fluent use.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use some British forms (film, flat, cinema, football, biscuits, neighbourhood, kilometres, practising, mobile phones, I've just got home). Teach movie, apartment, movie theater, soccer, cookies, neighborhood, kilometers / miles, practicing, cell phones, I've just gotten home, and accept the British forms when learners use them. Americans also often use the past simple with just / already / yet ("Did you eat yet?"). Teach the present perfect version (it is correct everywhere) and accept both.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

What I've done → what I've been doing → how things used to be. Every lesson in this module links the past to now. In C1 learners open a topic with an experience or some news (I've started a new job) and move into the finished details (I started two weeks ago). In C2 they talk about activities that are still going on, or that explain what we can see now (I've been painting. Look at my hands!). In C3 they compare then and now (People used to write letters. Every Sunday we would…) and give an opinion: was it better before? Each class ends with a longer speaking task than the last: a pair interview, then a group game and a catch-up chat, then a group discussion with roles.

Trauma-informed: the past is personal

This whole module is about the past, and for some adults the past holds hard things: a home they had to leave, a job they lost, people far away. Never ask "How was life in your country?", "Why did you come here?", "How long have you lived here?" or "What did you use to do before you came?" in a whole-class task. Keep personal questions to small, safe topics: food, hobbies, phones, work routines, free time. For every personal task offer three choices out loud: your real life, a new identity card (in the classroom materials), or people in general / a place you know ("People used to write letters"). In C3 the discussion is about technology, work and daily life, not about any one country. Anyone can say "Pass." Say it every time, so choosing a card is normal and not a sign that something is wrong.

B1 speaking: fluency first, then accuracy

B1 learners need long turns: more than one sentence, with a reason or an example. In every freer task, do not interrupt to correct. Walk around, listen, and write down 4–6 sentences you hear (good ones and ones with errors, no names). Give feedback in two steps: content first ("Lina's story about her first job was great: who else heard a surprising story?"), language second (the sentences on the board; learners fix them together). Correct on the spot only when the meaning breaks down or during controlled practice and drills.

Dominant speakers. Mixed-level groups often have one or two confident learners who fill every silence. Use talking tokens (3 per person; put one down when you take a long turn; when yours are gone, wait until everyone has used theirs), roles (the confident learner is the chair whose job is to make others talk, or the reporter who must repeat what others said), and think time (1 minute of silent notes before any discussion). Say it kindly in private: "You have great ideas. Today your job is to help Maria and Kofi talk more."

Before the Module

  • Placement. Ideally, learners took the toolkit placement test (../../instructor-toolkit/placement-test.html) before C1. If not, the C1 welcome mingle doubles as a quick A2 diagnostic (see Lesson 1.1). It will not replace a placement test, but it will show you who needs extra support and who is close to B2.
  • Print the welcome question cards (Set B, one set per 12 learners) and one copy of the A2 diagnostic grid for you, on a clipboard. Print name tents (folded paper is fine).
  • Print the picture flashcards (Set A, one set for the board), the time-word sort cards (one set per pair for C1) and the new identity cards (Set C). Keep the identity cards on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • For C1: one timeline interview card per learner (Set E). For C2: one Find Someone Who sheet per learner (Set H) and one clue game deck per group of 3–4 (Set F). For C3: one set of discussion topic cards and role cards per group of 4 (Set G) and talking tokens (3 per learner: paper clips, beans or small squares of paper).
  • Bring a photo or an object for each lead-in: something new in your life for C1 (a place you've been, a new skill), a paintbrush or a flour-covered cloth for C2 (acting it out is fun), and an old object for C3 (a paper map, a phone book, an old phone, a cassette, a photo of a pay phone).

Module at a Glance

LessonClassFocusLearners can…
1.1C1 · wk 1Welcome + A2 diagnostic; present perfect vs past simple (finished time vs connected to now); for / since; just, already, yet, still; open with the present perfect, then give details in the past simple; been vs gonetell a partner about an experience or some news: open with the present perfect and give 3+ details in the past simple
1.2C2 · wk 1Present perfect continuous (have / has been + -ing) for duration up to now and for a recent activity with a present result; simple (how many?) vs continuous (how long?); stative verbsanswer "What have you been doing lately?" with 3+ ongoing activities, how long, and one counted result
1.3C3 · wk 2used to (+ / − / ?) for past habits and states; didn't use to / Did … use to…?; would for repeated past actions only; used to vs past simpletake part in a group discussion about how life has changed (6+ sentences with used to / would) and give an opinion with a reason

Lesson Plans

1.1 · C1

Welcome to B1 · Present Perfect vs Past Simple

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • meet classmates and use A2 language fluently: past simple, present perfect for experiences, future forms
  • choose the past simple for a finished action at a finished time (yesterday, in 2019, two weeks ago) and the present perfect for a past action connected to now (experience, news, result, unfinished time)
  • use for + a period and since + a start point to say how long something has been true
  • use just, already, yet and still in the right position
  • open a topic with the present perfect and switch to the past simple for the details: I've been to Japan. I went in 2019…
Materials
  • Workbook 1.1 (Welcome box, New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My timeline")
  • Slides 1–9 · name tents · Welcome question cards (Set B) · A2 diagnostic grid (your copy) · experience flashcards + time-word sort cards (Set A, 1 set per pair) · Timeline interview cards (Set E) · New identity cards (Set C)
  • Listening scripts 1.1-A (Sam's news) and 1.1-B (dictation)

Left: two boxes. FINISHED TIME (yesterday · last week · in 2019 · two days ago · when I was a child) → past simple: I saw it last Friday. · CONNECTED TO NOW (ever · never · today · this week · so far · no time word) → present perfect: I've seen it three times.

Center: a time line: past ——————→ ● NOW. An arrow from 2021 to NOW: "I've worked here since 2021 / for five years." A single dot at "last Friday": "I saw it last Friday."

Right: the shape of a story: OPEN (present perfect, no time) "I've been to Japan." → DETAILS (past simple) "I went in 2019. We visited Kyoto. It was amazing." Below: I've just arrived. · She's already left. · Have you eaten yet? · I still haven't finished.

StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Welcome & name tents7T–SGreet each person at the door. Learners write the name they want to be called on a name tent (a new-identity name is fine). Go around the room and learn to say every name correctly. Introduce yourself in three tenses: "I'm ___. I've taught English for six years. Last summer I learned to make bread. This year I'm going to run a 5K."
Welcome mingle = A2 diagnostic15S–S (mingle)Slide 3. Each learner takes three welcome question cards, one of each color (PAST · PERFECT · FUTURE). They stand, find a partner, ask one question and one follow-up, answer, then swap cards and find a new partner (4 rounds). You walk with the diagnostic grid and listen (see "Running the diagnostic" below). Last 3 minutes: 3–4 learners tell the class something interesting about a partner: "Nadia has cooked for fifty people! She did it at her cousin's wedding." ICQs first.
About this course3T–SSlide 2. 26 classes, 2 a week, 13 weeks; the workbook; the online course is homework (show it on a phone and tap a 🔊 button). B1 = choosing the right form, longer answers, and discussion. Module 1 = the past, connected to now.
Lead-in: my news5T–STell a short true story in the "open → details" shape (board, right): "I've been to the new market downtown. I went on Saturday with my neighbor. We bought fresh bread and it was cheap!" Ask: "Which sentence has no time? Which sentences have a time?" Write the first and second sentences on the board. No rules yet: let learners notice. Then slide 4: learners ask you "Have you ever…?" about two pictures.
Grammar: the choice12T–S → S–SSlides 5–6. The key idea: finished time → past simple; connected to now → present perfect (board, left). CCQs (below). Then the time-word sort: pairs sort 14 cards into FINISHED and CONNECTED TO NOW (3 min); check with the class. Drill (below).
Listening: Sam's news6T–S → SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 1.1-A once for gist ("What's Sam's news?"), then again: learners answer the questions. Check: "Which tense opens the news? Which tense gives the details?"
for / since + just, already, yet, still9T–SSlide 7. for = a length, since = a start point: quick-fire (T: "2020" → S: "since 2020"; T: "three weeks" → S: "for three weeks"). Then the four adverbs and their positions, with one mime each (just = snap fingers; still = shake head, impatient). CCQs.
Controlled practice9S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2, 3 and 4 alone, then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on. Early finishers start Ex 5.
Freer: Timeline interview14S → S–S → T–SSlides 8–9. Read the model dialogue with a strong learner. (1) Each learner draws their own timeline on the card with 4–5 small events as pictures or one word (real, a new identity card, or imagined), and one thing that is still true now (3 min). (2) Swap timelines. The partner interviews: open with the present perfect ("Have you ever…? / How long have you…?"), then ask for details in the past simple ("When did you…? What happened? How was it?") (4 min each way). (3) 3–4 learners tell the class one story about their partner (3 min). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SContent first: "Who heard a surprising story?" Then language: 4 sentences you heard (2 good, 2 with errors, no names), e.g. "I have seen him last week." · "I work here since three years." Learners fix them.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 1.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total907 + 15 + 3 + 5 + 12 + 6 + 9 + 9 + 14 + 5 + 5
If you run long

The welcome mingle is the most important part of C1. Do not cut it: the group needs it, and you need the diagnostic. Cut instead: Ex 4 goes to homework, and the dictation (Script 1.1-B) moves to the C2 warmer. Keep at least 10 minutes for the timeline interview, because it is the first real B1 speaking task.

Running the diagnostic — what to listen for

The mingle is a friendly first conversation, not a test. Learners must never feel watched. Hold the grid on a clipboard, stand near a pair for 30–60 seconds, and join in sometimes ("Oh, have you really cooked for fifty people? I haven't!"). Mark each learner ✓ (easy and accurate) · ~ (understands, some errors) · ✗ (can't produce it yet) in four columns:

  • PAST SIMPLE: irregular verbs (went, bought, saw), Did you…? and didn't + base verb. Listen for "I goed," "Did you went?" and "I no went."
  • PRESENT PERFECT: Have you ever…? + past participle, I've never…, short answers. Listen for "Have you ever went?", "I have see," and present perfect with a finished time.
  • FUTURE FORMS: going to for plans, will / might for guesses, present continuous for arrangements. Listen for "I going to," "I will to," and the present simple for a plan ("Tomorrow I visit…").
  • FLUENCY: Does the learner give more than one sentence? Ask a question back? Keep going when they don't know a word? This column matters most at B1.

What to do with the results: Mostly ✓ and ~ = ready for B1. Many ~ in the PRESENT PERFECT column is normal: that is exactly what C1–C2 teach. ✗ in two or three columns = check the placement privately (the toolkit's oral placement interview takes 5 minutes); the learner may do better in the A2 course, or may need extra support from Day 1. Lots of ✓ and strong fluency = give stretch tasks and consider the B2 course later. Never share the grid with learners. Keep it; compare it with Progress Test 1 in C13.

ICQs before the welcome mingle
How many cards do you take? (Three, one of each color.) · How many questions do you ask each person? (One, and one follow-up question.) · After you talk, what do you do with the cards? (Swap them.) · Then? (Find a new partner.) · Do you have to answer about your real life? (No. You can use a new identity card, or say "Pass.")
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning. The choice is not about how long ago. It is about whether the time is finished (past simple) or the action is connected to now (present perfect: an experience in my life up to now, news with a result now, or a situation that is still true).

"I've lost my keys." Do I have my keys now? (No.) Do we know when I lost them? (No.) Is that important? (No, the result is important: I can't open the door!)
"I lost my keys yesterday." When? (Yesterday.) Is yesterday finished? (Yes.) Can I say "I've lost my keys yesterday"? (No! A finished time word needs the past simple.)
"She has lived here for ten years." Does she live here now? (Yes.) · "She lived there for ten years." Does she live there now? (No.)
"I've been to Japan." Am I in Japan now? (No, I went and came back.) · "She's gone to Japan." Is she here now? (No, she's in Japan.)
"I've just finished." A long time ago or a short time ago? (Very short.) · "I still haven't finished." Did I finish? (No.) Am I surprised or a bit unhappy? (Yes.)

Form. Learners know both forms. Put them side by side and focus on the time words:

Past simplesubject + past form (+ finished time)I saw that movie last Friday. · Did you see it? — Yes, I did.
Present perfectsubject + have / has + past participleI've seen it three times. · Have you ever been to Canada?
for / sincefor + a period · since + a start pointfor ten years, for a long time · since 2014, since Monday, since I was a child
just / alreadybetween have and the participleI've just finished lunch. · She's already left.
yet / stillyet at the end (? and −) · still before haven'tHave you eaten yet? · I haven't eaten yet. · I still haven't packed.
Open → detailspresent perfect (no time) → past simple (time, place, how)I've run a marathon. I ran it last year in Boston. It was really hard.

Pronunciation. Use the contractions: I've /aɪv/, she's /ʃiz/, haven't /ˈhævənt/. The 've is small but it carries the meaning: "I've seen it" vs "I seen it" (non-standard). Have learners say /v/ with teeth on the lip. for is weak: /fər/ ("fer ten years"). been is usually weak: /bɪn/. Have you ever…? links: /həv jə ˈɛvər/. Questions with yet rise at the end ("Have you eaten yet? ↗").

Drill script
  1. Time-word switch: T: "I've seen that movie." + "last Friday" → S: "I saw that movie last Friday." T: "She's called me." + "yesterday" → S: "She called me yesterday." T: "We visited Chicago." + "three times" → S: "We've visited Chicago three times." Choral, then individuals.
  2. for or since? T: "2021" → S: "since 2021." "six months" → "for six months." "last summer" → "since last summer." "ages" → "for ages." Then full sentences: "I've known her… (2014)" → "I've known her since 2014."
  3. Open → details chain: T: "I've been to a big concert." → S1: "When did you go?" → T: "I went in May." → S2: "Who did you go with?"… Then S1 opens with a new experience and the class asks.
  4. just / already / yet / still with mimes: T snaps fingers + "finish" → S: "I've just finished." T shakes head impatiently + "not / call me" → S: "She still hasn't called me." T: "you / eat?" + rising hand → S: "Have you eaten yet?"
Timeline interview — how to run it
  • Model first with your timeline on the board: 4 small pictures or words (🍞 bread class, 🚲 bike, 🎸 guitar, ✈️ Mexico) and one arrow to NOW (📚 "teach English"). Invite learners to interview you.
  • Learners draw pictures or one word only, no sentences. Years are optional. Events can be small and happy: a first cake, a new phone, a concert, a good book, a new skill. Nobody has to put their real life on paper. A new identity card has a ready-made timeline.
  • The interviewer opens with the present perfect: "I see a guitar. Have you ever played in a band?" / "How long have you had your bike?" Then asks for details in the past simple: "When did you start? Who taught you? How was it?"
  • The speaker answers in full, long turns: 2–3 sentences for every question. Stronger pairs add just / already / yet / still.
  • Report: "Kofi has played the guitar since 2020. He learned from videos, and he played at a wedding last year!"
ICQs before the timeline interview
Do you write sentences on your timeline? (No, pictures or one word.) · Must it be your real life? (No. A new identity card or an imagined life is fine.) · What tense is the first question? (Present perfect: "Have you ever…?") · And the next questions? (Past simple: "When did you…?") · How long are your answers? (Two or three sentences.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Draw the timeline for the learner if needed, from what they say. Give a phrase strip: "Have you ever…?" / "Yes, I have. / No, I've never…" / "When did you…?" / "I… in / last / ago." In the sort, use the picture side of the time cards (📅 🔚 for finished, ➡️● for connected to now). Target: two experiences, each with one past-simple detail. Workbook: Ex 1 (listening) and Ex 2, items 1–5 only.

Stretch

Use still and so far ("I've visited four states so far"). Explain why each tense fits, in their own words (the online journal task). In the interview, ask a surprise follow-up the speaker didn't plan. Early finishers write 3 pairs of sentences about the same thing: one present perfect, one past simple ("I've broken my phone!" / "I broke it this morning.").

L1 watch-outs
  • "I have seen him yesterday." French (passé composé), German (Perfekt), Italian (passato prossimo), Dutch and Spanish (in Spain) use a "have" tense for finished past time. Point at the FINISHED box on the board every time.
  • "I live here since five years" / "I am here since Monday." Spanish desde hace, French depuis, German seit, Portuguese há, Russian and Polish all use the present. It's understood; recast: "Oh, you've lived here for five years."
  • "since three years" / "for 2019." The same languages use one word for both (depuis, desde, seit). Drill: length → for; start → since.
  • Past simple everywhere ("Did you ever eat sushi?", "I already ate"): Chinese, Vietnamese, Arabic, Russian, Tagalog and Korean have no present perfect. Also normal in American English, so don't mark it wrong in speech; practice the present perfect as the safe choice.
  • Portuguese tenho feito means "I've been doing (again and again)," so Brazilian learners may avoid "I've done." Give lots of "I've + participle" for single experiences.
  • Dropped endings: Vietnamese, Cantonese and some Spanish speakers drop the final /v/ in I've and the /t/ or /d/ in worked, lived. Say it slowly: "I've — lived — here."
Homework

Online lesson 1.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_01.html. Listen to the model sentences and the marathon dialogue with 🔊 and say them out loud. Do the exercises, the Quick Quiz and the 2-minute speaking project (record it on a phone if possible). Workbook 1.1: finish Ex 5, "My new words" and the journal (two sentences about the same event, one present perfect and one past simple, and why).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B I called him yesterday. · Q2 A for · Q3 (new) B gone (she's there now). Sam's news (Ex 1): 1 he's started a new job (at a hotel) · 2 two weeks ago · 3 no, never · 4 at a bank, for three years · 5 yes, on his first day · 6 learned everyone's name · 7 finished her exams.

1.2 · C2

Present Perfect Continuous — "What Have You Been Doing?"

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • form the present perfect continuous: I've / she's been + -ing, haven't been, Have you been…?, How long have you been…?
  • use it for an activity that started in the past and is still going on, with for / since: I've been studying English for three years.
  • use it for a recent activity with a result we can see now: His hands are dirty. He's been fixing the car.
  • choose the simple for a result or a number (how many?) and the continuous for the activity (how long?)
  • keep stative verbs (know, like, want, have = own…) in the simple form
Materials
  • Workbook 1.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "What have I been doing lately?" table)
  • Slides 10–13 · Find Someone Who: Have you ever…? (Set H, 1 per learner) · clue-picture flashcards (Set A) · "What have you been doing?" clue game (Set F, 1 deck per group of 3–4) · New identity cards · a paintbrush, a dish towel with flour, or a gym bag for the lead-in
  • Listening scripts 1.2-A (Catching up) and 1.2-B (dictation)

Left: subject + have / has + been + -ing. I've been working. · She's been studying. · They haven't been sleeping well. · How long have you been waiting?

Center: a time line with a wavy line from "9 a.m." to ● NOW: "It's been raining all day." Below: two uses. ⏳ How long? (still going) · 👀 Evidence now (paint on hands → "I've been painting").

Right: two columns. HOW MANY? → simple: I've written three emails. · HOW LONG? → continuous: I've been writing emails all morning. Below, a red box: know · like · want · need · believe · understand · have (= own) → no -ing!

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Find Someone Who8S–S (mingle)Review C1. Learners mingle with the "Have you ever…?" sheet. When someone says "Yes, I have," they ask one past-simple follow-up ("When did you…?"). 5 min of mingling, then 3 learners report: "Omar has met a famous person. He met a soccer player at the airport!"
Lead-in: "Sorry I'm late!"5T–SWalk in (or turn around) out of breath, with flour on your hands or a paintbrush. "Look at me! What have I been doing?" Accept any guess, then say it: "I've been painting my kitchen! I've been painting since eight o'clock!" Write it on the board. "Did I stop painting? (Yes, just now.) Can you see it? (Yes, my hands!)"
Form + Use 1: how long?12T–SSlide 11. Form (board, left), then the main use: an activity that is still going on, with for / since / all day. CCQs and drill (below). Pairs ask each other two real questions: "How long have you been learning English? / coming to this school?"
Use 2: evidence now5T–SSlide 10. Show 4 clue-picture flashcards (mechanic, swim, garden, bus stop). "His hands are dirty. What has he been doing?" → "He's been fixing the car." CCQs. Keep it fast: the clue game later practices it.
Listening: Catching up7T–S → SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 1.2-A twice (both voices, or ask a confident learner to read Marco). Learners complete the table: what, how long, and the result. Pairs compare.
Simple vs continuous + stative verbs12T–SSlide 12. Two sentences from the listening: "I've been practicing every evening." / "I've learned about ten songs." "Which one answers how many? Which one answers how long?" Board, right. Then the stative-verb box: "I've been knowing her ❌ → I've known her ✅." CCQs. Quick-fire: T says a verb + time; learners choose ("know / ten years" → "I've known her for ten years").
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2, 3 and 4 alone, then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on (usually Ex 3). Early finishers: Ex 5 (stative trap).
Freer 1: clue game13S–S (groups)Slide 13. Groups of 3–4 with a clue-card deck. Player A takes a card and acts it or reads the clue in the first person ("Look at my hands! There's paint everywhere!"). The others guess: "Have you been painting?" A: "Yes, I have! / No, I haven't." First correct guess wins the card; the winner asks one "How many / How much…?" question and A answers in the simple ("I've painted two rooms!"). ICQs first.
Freer 2: catching up8S–SNew partners, standing, like old friends in the street. Each person prepares 3 lines of the "lately" table (real, a new identity card, or imagined) (2 min). Then chat: "Hi! What have you been doing lately?" → 2–3 sentences → "How long have you been…?" → one result in the simple (5 min). Two pairs perform 30 seconds (1 min).
Feedback5T–SContent first (the funniest clue, the most interesting "lately" news). Then 4 heard sentences: "I have working all day." · "I've been knowing him for years." · "I've been reading three books." · "He have been running." Learners fix them.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 1.2; workbook "My new words" and the journal. Preview C3: bring or photograph something old (a photo, a map, an old phone) if you want.
Total908 + 5 + 12 + 5 + 7 + 12 + 10 + 13 + 8 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Shorten the Find Someone Who to 5 minutes (no report) and set Ex 4 as homework. Keep both freer tasks, but you can make "catching up" a single round of 5 minutes. Don't cut the stative-verb box: it is the most common B1 error with this tense.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning 1: duration up to now. An activity started in the past and is still going on. We want the listener to feel the time.

"I've been studying English for three years." When did I start? (Three years ago.) Am I still studying? (Yes.) Is it finished? (No.) Which question does it answer: how long or how many? (How long.)
"It's been raining all day." Is it raining now? (Probably yes.) When did it start? (This morning.)

Meaning 2: a recent activity, a result now. The activity maybe stopped a moment ago, but we can see or feel the result.

"I'm tired. I've been running." Am I running now? (No, I just stopped.) How do you know I was running? (You're tired, red, out of breath.) · "Your eyes are red. Have you been swimming?" Is this a question about the eyes or the activity? (The activity.)

Meaning 3: simple or continuous?

"I've painted the kitchen." Is the job finished? (Yes.) · "I've been painting the kitchen." Is it finished? (Maybe not.) Why is the kitchen messy? (Because I've been painting.) · "I've written three emails." How many? (Three.) Simple or continuous? (Simple: we count it.)
"I've known her for ten years." Can I say "I've been knowing her"? (No.) Why? (Know is a state, not an activity.) · "I've been having problems with my laptop." Is this OK? (Yes: having problems = experiencing, an activity.)

Form. Three pieces that never change order: have / has + been + verb-ing. Only have / has changes.

Positivesubject + have / has been + -ingI've been working. · She's been studying.
Negativehaven't / hasn't been + -ingThey haven't been sleeping well lately.
QuestionHave / Has + subject + been + -ing?Have you been waiting long? — Yes, I have. / No, I haven't.
How long?How long have / has + subject + been + -ing?How long have you been learning to drive?
Simple vs continuoushow many / result → simple · how long / activity → continuousI've read 50 pages. · I've been reading for two hours.
Stative verbsknow, like, love, hate, want, need, believe, understand, be, have (= own), belongsimple only: I've had this car since 2019.

Pronunciation. been is weak and short, like "bin" /bɪn/: "I've been waiting" = /aɪv bɪn ˈweɪtɪŋ/. Say 've been as one quick piece. The stress goes on the main verb and the time: "I've been WORKing here since JUNE." In How long have you been…? only long and the main verb are strong: "How LONG have you been WAITing?" Some learners over-pronounce the -ing as /ɪŋɡ/ (Spanish, Arabic, Cantonese): keep the /ŋ/ soft, no hard /g/.

Drill script
  1. Back-chain: "for three years" → "studying English for three years" → "been studying English for three years" → "I've been studying English for three years." Choral ×3, then individuals.
  2. Person substitution: T: "She… work here… since June" → S: "She's been working here since June." T: "They… wait… for an hour" → S: "They've been waiting for an hour." T: "It… rain… all day" → S: "It's been raining all day."
  3. How long? question, open pairs: S1: "How long have you been learning English?" S2: "For two years / Since 2023." S2 → S3 with a new verb (living in this apartment, working at…, driving, cooking…). Remind: a new identity answer is fine.
  4. Evidence prompts (flashcards or mime): wet hair → "You've been swimming!" · black hands → "He's been fixing the car." · yawning → "You haven't been sleeping well." · flour on hands → "She's been baking."
  5. How many or how long? T: "emails, three" → S: "I've written three emails." T: "emails, all morning" → S: "I've been writing emails all morning." T: "know her, ten years" → S: "I've known her for ten years." (the stative trap)
Clue game — how to run it
  • Groups of 3–4, one deck face down. Player A takes a card, looks at the picture and the clue, and acts it out or reads the clue in the first person: "Oh, my back hurts, and I have mud on my knees!" A must not say the activity.
  • The others guess with a full question: "Have you been gardening?" A answers "Yes, I have!" or "No, I haven't. Try again!" The small gray hint on the card is for A only.
  • The first correct guesser wins the card and asks one "how many / how much" question: "How many flowers have you planted?" A answers in the simple: "I've planted twenty!" This is the simple vs continuous switch.
  • Stronger groups: A answers "No, I haven't" to the first correct guess and gives a second clue, to make more questions. Most cards wins.
  • Some cards have more than one good answer ("Your eyes are red": swimming, chopping onions, reading all night…). Any logical guess with the correct form wins.
ICQs before the clue game
Do you say the activity on your card? (No! You act it or read the clue.) · What's the first word of the guess? ("Have.") · Is "Have you painting?" correct? (No. "Have you been painting?") · After a correct guess, what question does the winner ask? (How many / how much.) · And the answer is simple or continuous? (Simple: "I've painted two rooms.")
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on I've been / you've been + -ing and one use (evidence). In the clue game, the learner only guesses from the picture side; a partner reads the clue aloud. Phrase strip: "Have you been …ing?" / "Yes, I have. / No, I haven't." / "I've been …ing for … ." Target: 3 correct guesses and 2 true sentences about "lately." Workbook: Ex 1, Ex 2 and Ex 4 only.

Stretch

Use lately / recently / all week / for ages naturally. In "catching up," give a reason and a feeling ("I've been getting up at five, so I've been really tired"). Explain the difference between "I've painted the kitchen" and "I've been painting the kitchen" to a partner. Early finishers write 3 clue cards for the game.

L1 watch-outs
  • "I am working here since 2020" / "I work here for two years." Spanish, Portuguese, French, Italian, German, Russian and Polish use the present (simple or continuous) for duration up to now. Point at the wavy line on the board: it started in the past → have been.
  • Dropping been: "I have working all day" (Arabic, Chinese, Vietnamese, Tagalog, where there is no helper verb chain). Clap three beats: have — been — working.
  • Stative verbs in -ing: "I've been knowing him," "I'm having a car." Common for speakers of Hindi, Urdu, Bengali and other South Asian languages, and heard in Indian and Nigerian English. Accept that it is normal in those varieties, but teach the standard form for tests and work.
  • "He have been": learners who drop the he/she -s (Chinese, Vietnamese, Haitian Creole speakers) also drop the has. Color has on the board.
  • Counted results with -ing: "I've been reading three books." Common for everybody who has just learned the new form. Ask: "How many or how long?"
Homework

Online lesson 1.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_02.html. Listen to the model sentences and the "catching up" dialogue with 🔊 and read them aloud. Do the three exercises, the Quick Quiz and the 2-minute project "What have you been up to?". Workbook 1.2: finish Ex 5, the "lately" table, "My new words" and the journal (one continuous + one simple sentence about the same activity, and one corrected stative-verb mistake).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B She has been working here since May. · Q2 A written · Q3 (new) B I've known her since we were at school. Catching up (Ex 1): Lena: taking a driving class · since January · 8 lessons, drove on the highway twice. Marco: working at the new supermarket · three months · always tired (gets up at five). Marco: practicing the guitar · every evening · learned about ten songs. Marco: fixing his bike · just now · dirty hands, fixed the chain (not the light).

1.3 · C3

Used to & Would — How Life Has Changed

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use used to + base verb for past habits and past states that are not true now: People used to write letters. There used to be a movie theater here.
  • make negatives and questions with did and the correct spelling: didn't use to, Did you use to…?
  • use would + base verb for repeated past actions once the past setting is clear: Every Sunday we would…
  • know that would does not work for states (live, like, have, be, know)
  • discuss how technology, work and daily life have changed, and say whether life was better before, with a reason
Materials
  • Workbook 1.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, discussion notes)
  • Slides 14–18 · "then and now" flashcards (Set A) · How life has changed topic cards + role cards (Set G, 1 set per group of 4) · talking tokens (3 per learner) · an old object or photo (a paper map, an old phone, a phone book, a photo of a pay phone) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 1.3-A (Then and now: three voices) and 1.3-B (dictation)

Left: THEN | NOW. "People used to write letters. | Now we send messages." "There used to be a video store here. | Now it's a phone store." Below: I didn't use to like coffee. · Did you use to have a bike? → Yes, I did. / No, I didn't.

Center: a red circle around the d: used to ✅ → didn't used to · Did you used to…? Pronunciation: /ˈjustə/ (an s sound, not a z).

Right: two columns. ACTIONS (visit, walk, cook, watch): used to ✅ would ✅. STATES (live, like, have, be, know): used to ✅ would ❌. Below: "Every summer we would drive to the lake." (the time frame first!)

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: catching up8S–S → T–SReview C2. Pairs: "What have you been doing since our last class?" (2 sentences each + one "how long?" question). Then 3 fast clue cards with the whole class: you read the clue, learners guess "Have you been…?" Two volunteers read their journal pair (continuous + simple).
Lead-in: then and now8T–S → S–SHold up your old object (a paper map). "What's this? Do we use it now?" Elicit: "People used to use paper maps. Now we use our phones." Slide 14: then-and-now pictures. Pairs make one "used to… now…" sentence for 3 pictures. Take 4–5 answers. Write two on the board.
Grammar: used to (+ − ?)13T–SSlide 15. Meaning (not true now), form, and the spelling after did (board, center). CCQs and drill (below), including the /ˈjustə/ pronunciation. Quick-fire negatives and questions: T: "like coffee" → S: "I didn't use to like coffee." / "Did you use to like coffee?"
Listening: Then and now7T–S → SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 1.3-A (three short voices) twice. Learners write True or False, then in pairs: "Whose job has changed the most?"
would + the state rule12T–SSlide 16. Read the grandmother's farm paragraph from the online lesson (or tell your own memory): "Every morning my grandmother would wake me at dawn…" "Is would about the future here? (No! Past habits.)" Then the key rule (board, right): actions → both; states → used to only. CCQs. Quick sort: T says a verb; learners show one finger (only used to) or two (both).
Controlled practice12S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2, 3 and 4 alone, then check in pairs. Board-check Ex 3 (used to or would?) together: this is the key B1 judgment. Early finishers start Ex 5.
Freer: How life has changed20S → S–S (groups of 4) → T–SSlide 17. (1) Groups of 4 get the role cards (chair, timekeeper, questioner, reporter) and 3 talking tokens each; ICQs (2 min). (2) Each group chooses two topic cards (phones, shopping, work, getting around, free time, food, money, learning). Think time: 2 minutes of silent notes in Workbook Speaking (2 × used to, 1 × would, 1 opinion) (2 min). (3) Discussion: 5 minutes per topic (10 min). Everyone talks about people in general, a place they know, their own life, or a new identity card. (4) Reporters give the group's answer to "Was it better before, or is it better now?" with one reason; quick class vote (6 min).
Feedback + module check5T–SContent first: the most interesting change, the best reason. Then 4 heard sentences: "I would live in a small town." · "Did you used to walk to work?" · "I used to went to the market." · "Before, I use to go by bus." Learners fix them. Then learners tick the Module 1 "I can…" lists.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 1.3; workbook Ex 5 and the journal; preview C4: "Next class: If it rains, we'll… What will you do if it rains this weekend?"
Total908 + 8 + 13 + 7 + 12 + 12 + 20 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Never cut the discussion: it is the first real B1 group discussion and the goal of the whole module. Instead, do one topic card instead of two (5 minutes less), and set Ex 4 as homework. If the class is small (6 or fewer), do the discussion as one group with you as a quiet timekeeper.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: used to. A habit or a state in the past that is not true now. Everyone hears the unspoken "…but not anymore."

"I used to smoke." Did I smoke in the past? (Yes.) Once or many times? (Many times, a habit.) Do I smoke now? (No.) · "There used to be a movie theater here." Is it here now? (No, it's gone.)
"Did you use to have a bike?" Is this about now or the past? (The past.) What's the short answer? ("Yes, I did. / No, I didn't.")

Meaning: would. Repeated past actions in a story or a memory, after the past setting is clear.

"When I was a child, every Sunday my father would cook a big breakfast." Is this about the future? (No.) An imagined situation? (No.) One time or many times? (Many times, every Sunday.) · "I would live in Rome." Is this OK for a past state? (No. Live is a state: "I used to live in Rome.")
"I lived in Rome for two years." / "I used to live in Rome." Do I live in Rome now? (No, in both.) Which one feels like a general memory? (used to.) Which one gives the exact length? (The past simple.)

Form.

Positivesubject + used to + base verbI used to walk to school. · There used to be a bakery here.
Negativesubject + didn't use to + base verbI didn't use to like coffee.
QuestionDid + subject + use to + base verb?Did you use to play an instrument? — Yes, I did. / No, I didn't.
wouldsubject + would (+ always / often) + base verbEvery summer we would visit my grandparents. · He would always tell jokes.
The ruleactions: used to or would · states: used to onlyWe would go to the beach. ✅ · We would have a dog. ❌ → We used to have a dog. ✅
Not the same!be used to + -ing = be comfortable with (a present feeling)I'm used to getting up early. (Not today's lesson: just recognize it.)

Pronunciation. used to is one quick word: /ˈjustə/ ("YOO-stuh"), with an s sound, not a z, and a weak to. use to (after did) sounds the same, so the spelling rule only matters in writing. would is usually 'd after a pronoun: "we'd go," "she'd always sing." Stress the contrast words: "We USED to write LETTERS. NOW we send MESSAGES."

Drill script
  1. Then → now (flashcards): T shows the post office + phone: "letters / messages" → S: "People used to write letters. Now they send messages." Next: "cash / cards," "paper maps / GPS," "video store / streaming."
  2. Negative and question: T: "I liked vegetables." (shake head) → S: "I didn't use to like vegetables." T: "you / have a nickname?" → S: "Did you use to have a nickname?" → another S: "Yes, I did. / No, I didn't."
  3. /ˈjustə/ chorally: "used to — used to be — there used to be — there used to be a bakery here." Check nobody says "use-ed to."
  4. One finger or two? T says: "live near the sea" (1: only used to) · "play soccer every Saturday" (2: both) · "have long hair" (1) · "visit my aunt every summer" (2) · "know everyone" (1) · "tell stories at night" (2). Then S says the sentence with would for every "2."
  5. Memory chain: T: "When I was younger, every Saturday I would…" → S1 continues with would + an action → S2 adds another… (real or imagined).
How life has changed — how to run it
  • Topics, not biographies. Every card is about the world: phones, shopping, work, getting around, free time, food, money, learning. Learners can talk about people in general ("People used to…"), a place they know (any place: this city, a TV show, their workplace), their own life, or a new identity card. Never ask a learner to say which country they are talking about.
  • Roles (on the role cards): the chair asks each person in turn and invites quiet people ("Kofi, what do you think?"); the timekeeper says "One minute left!"; the questioner asks one follow-up per person ("Did people use to…?"); the reporter takes notes and gives the group's answer at the end. Give the chair role to a confident speaker, and the reporter role to someone who talks a lot but listens little.
  • Talking tokens: 3 per person. Put one in the middle when you take a long turn. When your tokens are gone, you can ask questions but not give opinions, until everyone has used theirs.
  • Discussion shape on each card: How did people use to…? → What would people do every day / week? → What's different now? → Was it better before, or is it better now? Why? Model an opinion phrase: "I think it was better before, because…" / "In my opinion, it's better now, because…"
  • Listen and note 6 sentences for feedback. Don't correct during the discussion unless the meaning breaks down.
ICQs before the discussion
How many topics does your group talk about? (Two.) · What do you do before you talk? (Two minutes of silent notes.) · Chair, what's your job? (Ask everyone. Help quiet people talk.) · What do you do with a token? (Put it down when I take a long turn.) · Do you have to talk about your own life? (No. People in general, a place, or a new identity card.) · Can we use would with "live" or "have"? (No! Only used to.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Use only used to (positive) and "Now…" with the then-and-now picture cards. Sentence frame: "People used to ___. Now they ___." Give the learner the timekeeper role (it needs a clock, not reading) and let a partner read the topic card aloud. Target: 3 then-and-now sentences and one opinion ("It was better before / It's better now"). Workbook: Ex 1, Ex 2 items 1 and 4, and Ex 5 with pictures.

Stretch

Use would for a whole memory of 4–5 sentences after one past-simple scene-setter. Add anymore / any longer / these days / nowadays. In the discussion, disagree politely with a reason ("I see your point, but…"). Early finishers: write the paragraph for Ex 5 with one negative and one question, and explain the difference between "I used to get up early" and "I'm used to getting up early."

L1 watch-outs
  • "I use to go to the gym" for a present habit. Spanish suelo, Portuguese costumo and Italian di solito work in the present, so many learners say "I use to drink coffee every morning" (meaning usually). Very common! Teach: present habit → usually; past habit → used to.
  • "Before, I live in a village" / "Before I am shy." Arabic, Chinese, Tagalog (dati), Vietnamese and Korean use a time word with no past form. It is understood; recast: "Oh, you used to live in a village."
  • "I used to went" / "I didn't used to" / "Did you used to…?" Everyone. Point at the red circle on the board.
  • "I would live in Rome" for a past state: learners who have just met would for past habits over-use it. Ask: "Action or state?"
  • "I was used to walk to school" (Russian, Polish and French speakers mixing used to with be used to). Keep be used to for recognition only today.
  • "use-ed to" with three syllables or a /z/ sound (Spanish, Portuguese, Japanese, Korean speakers read the spelling). Drill /ˈjustə/.
Homework

Online lesson 1.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_03.html. Listen to the model sentences and the "old neighborhood" conversation with 🔊 and say them. Do the exercises, the Quick Quiz and the project "How things used to be" (6+ sentences, spoken or recorded; any topic from class is fine). Workbook 1.3: finish Ex 5, "My new words" and the journal (one used to state, one would action, one negative or question, and why).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B Did you use to live here? · Q2 A used to · Q3 (new) B There used to be a movie theater on Main Street. Then and now (Ex 1): 1 T · 2 F (they didn't use to shop online) · 3 T · 4 F (she works on the phone and online now) · 5 T · 6 F (he liked it better before).

Closing the Module

End of C3: Module 1 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, the "past to now" line: I've been to… / I went… (experience → details) → I've been …ing for… (still going) → I used to… / We would… (not true now). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C4 warmer and again in the C13 progress-test catch-up.

Watch for in Module 2: conditionals bring back would, but with a different meaning (If I had more time, I would…). Learners who just learned would for past habits may be confused. Say it clearly in C5: "Same word, different job. Last week: past habits. Now: imagined situations." Keep a 2-minute "then and now" or "lately" warmer in C4–C6.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: present perfect vs past simple with time words, for / since, just / already / yet / still, been vs gone, the form of the present perfect continuous, simple vs continuous (how many / how long), stative verbs, used to / didn't use to / Did … use to…?, and used to vs would for states and actions. The speaking task may ask learners to describe how something has changed.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 2 — If & Imagining (Conditionals)

Three classes that move from the real world to the imagined one. First, the zero conditional for facts, habits and life hacks and the first conditional for real, likely futures (with when and unless). Then the second conditional for dreams, dilemmas and advice (If I were you, I'd…). Last, wish / if only for small, everyday wishes about the present, and wish + would for mild annoyances.

3 lessons Lessons 2.1–2.3 Classes C4–C6 Weeks 2–3 90 min per class CEFR B1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups, mingle) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-2-conditionals.html. Print materials/module-2/classroom-materials.html before C4. It holds the flashcards, the "imagine a person" identity cards, the listening scripts, the Life Hacks & Superstitions card sort, the conditional-plans role-play, the dilemma cards, the advice role-play, the Small Wishes, Real Fixes cards and the Wish Mingle (Find Someone Who).
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences and dialogues again at home.
  • This is extension, not a first meeting. A2 Lesson 6.1 taught the first conditional (If it rains, I'll stay home). Most B1 learners will recognize it, and many will still say "If it will rain." Check fast in the lead-in and spend your time on the new B1 work: zero vs first, when vs if, unless, the second conditional, were, and wish. Keep models inside B1: no third or mixed conditionals and no wish + had done (past regrets are B2). If a strong learner asks, give one example and say "That's for the next level."
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons sometimes use British forms (flat, flatmate, holiday, football, films, grey, neighbour, practise, organised, apologise, 100°C). Teach apartment, roommate, vacation, soccer, movies, gray, neighbor, practice, organized, apologize, and give temperatures in °F with °C in brackets. Accept the British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Real → imagined → wished for. In C4 learners talk about what is always true (life hacks, habits, facts) and what will really happen (If I finish early on Friday, I'll cook something nice). In C5 they step out of reality: If I found a wallet, I'd…, If I were you, I'd…. In C6 they use the same "unreal past" to say what they want to be different right now: I wish it were sunny. I wish I could sing. I wish the bus would come on time. The one big idea across the module: the verb shows how real the situation is. Present = real. Past = unreal (not past time!). Build the "reality line" on the board in C4 and add to it in C5 and C6.

Trauma-informed: keep the "what ifs" and wishes small

Conditionals and wishes can open painful doors for adult newcomers: "What would you do if you could go home?", "I wish my family were here," "If only I had stayed…". Never ask about home countries, family far away, migration, papers, war, illness or money worries, and never use them as examples. Every task in this module uses light, everyday topics: life hacks, superstitions, a found wallet, a lottery win, superpowers, the weather, skills, the commute, the phone, a noisy neighbor. For every personal task offer three choices out loud: your real answer, an "imagine a person" card (in the classroom materials), or an invented answer. Anyone can say "Pass" at any time. Say it every time, so choosing a card is normal and not a sign that something is wrong. If a learner shares something heavy anyway, thank them simply ("Thank you for telling us"), don't dig, and move the group on to the next card.

B1 speaking: fluency first, and room for everyone

Fluency before accuracy. In every freer stage, don't interrupt to correct. Walk around with a notepad, write down 4–6 sentences you hear (good ones and wrong ones), and use them in the Feedback stage with no names. First give feedback on content ("Which dilemma got the most different answers?"), then on language. Correct on the spot only in the drill and controlled stages. Dominant speakers: give each learner three talking tokens (paper clips, coins) per discussion; each turn costs one, and when yours are gone you listen and ask questions. Give roles: asker (reads the card), timekeeper (1 minute per person), reporter (tells the class). Always go around the circle once before open discussion, and say "Let's hear from someone who hasn't spoken yet." Quiet learners can write their answer first and then read it.

Before the Module

  • Print the picture flashcards (one set for the board): life hacks & superstitions, dreams & dilemmas, small wishes (weather, skills, commute). Card stock helps. Cut them before C4.
  • For C4: one Life Hacks & Superstitions card sort set per group of 3 (32 half-cards + 3 column headers). Cut and put each set in an envelope. Bring sticky notes (or strips of paper and tape) for the class "Life Hack Wall."
  • For C5: one set of conditional-plans role-play cards per pair (warmer), one set of dilemma cards per group of 3–4 + a reaction-phrase strip for each learner, and one set of advice role-play A/B cards per pair.
  • For C6: one set of Small Wishes, Real Fixes cards per pair and one Wish Mingle (Find Someone Who) sheet per learner.
  • Print the "imagine a person" cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Talking tokens for the discussions: 3 paper clips or coins per learner. For the C4 lead-in, a wooden spoon (or a photo of a spoon across a pot) is a fun prop.

Module at a Glance

LessonClassFocusLearners can…
2.1C4 · wk 2Zero conditional (If / When + present, present) for facts, habits, life hacks · first conditional (If + present, will / won't) for real futures · never will after if · the comma · when vs if · unless = if … not · results with can / might / imperativeshare life hacks and superstitions, say which are "always true" and which are predictions, and make 6+ real plans and warnings with If I…, I'll…, including unless and when
2.2C5 · wk 3Second conditional (If + past simple, would / 'd + base verb) for imaginary, unlikely and impossible situations · the past = unreal, not past time · first vs second (how likely?) · were for all persons · If I were you, I'd… for advice · could / might in the resultdiscuss everyday dilemmas and dreams ("What would you do if…?"), give a reason, agree or disagree politely, and give friendly advice with If I were you, I'd…
2.3C6 · wk 3wish / if only + past simple (present wishes) · the "flip" (wish = opposite of reality) · wish + were · wish + could (ability) · wish + would (someone else's annoying behavior, never yourself) · hope vs wishsay 6+ small, true or invented wishes about the weather, skills and the commute, complain kindly with I wish … would…, and suggest a real fix with a conditional

Lesson Plans

2.1 · C4

Zero & First Conditional — Life Hacks, Superstitions & Real Plans

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use the zero conditional (If / When + present simple, present simple) for facts, habits and life hacks: If you freeze grapes, they make great ice cubes.
  • use the first conditional (If + present simple, will / won't + base verb) for one real, likely future: If it rains this afternoon, I'll take the bus.
  • keep will out of the if-clause and the when-clause (when I get home, not "when I will get home")
  • choose when (sure) or if (possible), and use unless (= if … not) with a positive verb
  • put a comma after the if-clause when it comes first, and use can / might or an imperative in the result
  • share and discuss life hacks and superstitions, and say 6+ real plans and warnings about their own week
Materials
  • Workbook 2.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My six predictions")
  • Slides 3–8 · Picture flashcards (life hacks & superstitions) · Life Hacks & Superstitions card sort (1 envelope per group of 3) · sticky notes · talking tokens · "imagine a person" cards
  • Listening scripts 2.1-A (Tip Time radio show) and 2.1-B (dictation)

Left: the reality line (keep it all module). A long arrow: ALWAYS TRUE ─── REAL, LIKELY ─── IMAGINED (leave this end empty today). Under "always true": If / When + present, present. Under "real, likely": If + present, will + verb.

Center: 🔧 If you freeze grapes, they make great ice cubes. (every time) · 📅 If it rains this afternoon, I'll take the bus. (one future) · a big red ❌ over "If it will rain" · comma rule: If …, … / … if … (no comma)

Right: when = I'm sure (When I get home, I'll call you.) · if = maybe (If I get the job, I'll celebrate.) · unless = if … not (I'll be late unless I leave now.) · Result can be: will / can / might / an order (tell him!)

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: then and now8S–S → T–SReview C3 (1.3, used to / would). Pairs tell each other 2 things they used to do on weekends as a teenager and 1 thing they would always do (real, an "imagine a person" card, or invented). Two pairs report one surprising habit. Listen for "I use to" and "I would live" (state verb) for the feedback stage.
Lead-in: does it work?6T–SHold up a wooden spoon (or slide 3's life-hack picture). "My grandmother had a trick. If you put a wooden spoon across a pot of boiling water, the water doesn't boil over. Do you believe it?" Learners vote. Then: "Some people say, if you open an umbrella indoors, you'll have bad luck. Is that a fact or a belief?" Teach life hack and superstition. Ask: "Do you know the first conditional from A2?" Many will. No grammar talk yet.
Grammar: zero conditional10T–SSlide 4. Meaning (always true: facts, habits, how things work), form (present + present; if ≈ when). CCQs and drill (below). Quick-fire: T says a half ("If you heat ice…") → learners finish ("…it melts.").
Grammar: first conditional + the one rule12T–SSlide 5. Contrast on the board: "If you drop a glass, it breaks." (always) / "Careful! If you drop that glass, it'll break." (this glass, now). Form: if + present, will + base verb. Then the rule: no will after if. Put "If it will rain" on the board and cross it out dramatically. Results with can / might / imperative. CCQs and drill.
when / unless / the comma7T–S → SSlide 6. When = sure, if = maybe (CCQs). Unless = if … not, with a positive verb. The comma: learners copy two sentences from the board and add the comma only where the if-clause is first.
Listening: Tip Time7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 2.1-A twice. Learners complete the results and write H (life hack / fact), S (superstition) or F (a real future plan). Pairs compare. Point out: the superstitions use will — they sound like predictions.
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2, 3 and 4 alone, then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on (usually Ex 4, when / if / unless). Early finishers start Ex 5.
Freer: Life Hacks & Superstitions card sort20S–S (groups of 3) → T–SSlide 8. (1) Match the 32 half-cards into 16 sentences (5 min). (2) Sort them under the three headers: 🔧 Always true · 🍀 Superstition · 📅 One real future (3 min). Check with the class. (3) Discuss with talking tokens (8 min): "Do you believe it? Have you tried it? Do you know another life hack or superstition?" (4) Each group writes 2 real life hacks on sticky notes with a zero conditional and puts them on the Life Hack Wall; the class reads the wall (4 min). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SContent first: "Which life hack will you try this week?" Then delayed correction: 4 heard sentences on the board (no names), e.g. "If it will rain, I stay home." · "Unless you don't hurry, you'll miss it." · "When I will get home, I call you." · one good sentence. Learners fix them together.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 2.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total908 + 6 + 10 + 12 + 7 + 7 + 10 + 20 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning 1: zero conditional = always true. Facts, rules, how a machine works, a person's regular habit, a life hack. The result happens every time.

"If you heat ice, it melts." Is this about one day, or every time? (Every time.) Is it a fact? (Yes.) · "When I'm tired, I drink coffee." Is this a habit? (Yes.) Can I say "If I'm tired" here? (Yes, the same meaning.)

Meaning 2: first conditional = one real, likely future. The speaker thinks it can really happen.

"If it rains this afternoon, I'll take the bus." Is it raining now? (No.) Is rain possible this afternoon? (Yes, maybe.) Is this about every day or about this afternoon? (This afternoon.) Is it a plan with a condition? (Yes.)
"If it will rain, I'll take the bus." Is this correct? (No!) Where does will go? (In the result, the other half.) · "When I get home, I'll call you." Will I get home? (Yes, I'm sure.) "If I get the job, I'll celebrate." Will I get the job? (Maybe.)
"I'll be late unless I leave now." Am I leaving now? (We don't know yet.) What happens if I don't leave now? (I'll be late.) Is "unless I don't leave" OK? (No, the "not" is inside unless.)

Form. Color the if-clause in one color and the result in another. Show that the halves can swap.

ZeroIf / When + present simple, present simpleIf you mix blue and yellow, you get green. · When I drink coffee late, I can't sleep.
FirstIf + present simple, will / won't + base verbIf we leave now, we won't be late. · If she calls, I'll tell her.
Other resultscan / might + base verb · imperativeIf you finish early, you can go home. · If you see John, tell him hi.
when / unlesswhen = sure · if = maybe · unless = if … not (+ positive verb)When I get home, I'll call you. · You won't pass unless you study.
CommaIf-clause first → comma · if-clause second → no commaIf I see her, I'll tell her. · I'll tell her if I see her.

Pronunciation. Use the contraction: I'll /aɪl/, it'll /ˈɪtəl/, we'll, won't /woʊnt/. The if-clause rises and waits, the result falls and finishes: "If it RAINS ↗ | I'll take the BUS. ↘". The pause at the comma is the "comma you can hear." unless is stressed on the second syllable: unLESS /ʌnˈlɛs/. Superstitions are often said with drama: "you'll have SEVen years of BAD luck!" — have fun with it.

Drill script
  1. Rise–fall chant: "If it rains ↗ | I'll take the bus ↘." Choral ×3 with a hand going up, then down. Half the room says the if-half, the other half the result. Swap. Then 4–5 individuals.
  2. Finish my sentence (zero): T: "If you heat ice…" → S: "…it melts." T: "When I'm hungry…" → S: "…I eat a snack." T: "If you don't water plants…" → S: "…they die."
  3. Chain (first): T: "If it's sunny on Saturday, I'll go to the park." S1: "If I go to the park, I'll play soccer." S2: "If I play soccer, I'll be tired." … around the room. Stop anyone who says "if I will" with the red ❌ gesture; they self-correct.
  4. Transform with unless: T: "I'll be late if I don't leave now." → S: "I'll be late unless I leave now." T: "You won't pass if you don't study." → S: "You won't pass unless you study."
  5. when or if? T gives a situation; learners choose: "I'm sure I'll get home at six." → "When I get home, I'll call you." / "Maybe I'll see Anna." → "If I see Anna, I'll give her the book."
Life Hacks & Superstitions card sort — how to run it
  • Groups of 3, one envelope each. Step 1: match the if-halves (plain border) to the result halves (gray border) to make 16 sentences. The grammar is a clue: a present result or a will result.
  • Step 2: sort the sentences under the three headers: 🔧 Always true (life hacks, facts, habits → zero) · 🍀 Superstition (a belief) · 📅 One real future (a plan or warning → first). Check with the class (answers in the answer key). Ask: "Look at the superstitions. Zero or first? Why?" (First: people say them like predictions: you'll have bad luck.) One superstition uses the zero form (If your ears are burning, someone is talking about you) — a nice discussion point.
  • Step 3: discuss with talking tokens. Roles: the asker reads the questions on the header card, the timekeeper gives each person about a minute, the reporter remembers the best answer. Go around once before open discussion.
  • Step 4: each group writes 2 life hacks they really use (cooking, cleaning, phones, saving money, the bus) as zero conditionals on sticky notes and puts them on the Life Hack Wall. Leave the wall up for C5.
  • Superstitions are beliefs, and some are connected to religion or family. Nobody has to say what they believe, and we never laugh at a belief. Say: "You can talk about a superstition you know, one from a movie, or say 'Pass.'" Keep the talk on everyday, playful superstitions (mirrors, umbrellas, black cats, salt). If someone mentions a serious religious practice, thank them and move on.
ICQs before the card sort
How many sentences do you make? (Sixteen.) · After matching, how many groups? (Three: always true, superstition, one real future.) · In the discussion, do you have to say what you believe? (No. You can say "Pass.") · What does a token do? (One token = one turn.) · What do you write on the sticky notes? (Two real life hacks, with "If… / When…".)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give 8 of the 16 sentences only (the ones with picture flashcards: umbrella, mirror, salt, water, bread, phone, bus, coffee), and pair the learner with a partner who reads aloud. Focus on one pattern each: zero = "If…, it…" (present, present) and first = "If…, I'll…". Target: 3 spoken sentences with If…, I'll… about this week, using the picture cards as prompts, and one life hack said aloud (a partner writes the sticky note).

Stretch

Use as soon as and before / after with the same rule ("As soon as I get home, I'll call you."). In the discussion, give a reason for belief or doubt ("I don't believe it, because…"). Write a life hack with unless ("Your bread goes hard unless you keep it in a bag."). Early finishers write three warnings for a new learner at the school ("If you're late, the door will be locked. Unless you bring your book, you won't…").

L1 watch-outs
  • "If it will rain" / "When I will get home": Russian, Polish, Ukrainian, French (quand je serai), Portuguese (quando eu chegar is subjunctive, but learners may translate as "will"), Tagalog (kung uulan, future aspect) and Korean speakers use a future form in the condition. This is the #1 error. Use the red ❌ gesture every time and praise self-correction.
  • "If it rains, I stay home" (present in the result for one future): Spanish, German, Dutch and Chinese speakers (the present can mean the future in the L1). It is understood; recast with I'll.
  • "If it rain tomorrow, I go" (no tense marking at all): Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai and Haitian Creole speakers. Focus on the -s in the if-clause and the 'll in the result. Vietnamese speakers may also drop final /l/ in I'll, so "I'll go" sounds like "I go": exaggerate the l.
  • "unless you don't…" (double negative): many learners, especially Spanish (a menos que), French and Arabic speakers who translate "if not" word for word. Teach: unless already has the "not" in it.
  • when vs if: German wenn means both "if" and "when," so German and Dutch speakers may say "When I win the lottery…". Also Chinese 如果 / 要是 and Arabic إذا cover both. CCQ: "Are you sure?"
  • No comma / comma everywhere: learners from Arabic, Chinese and Japanese writing traditions punctuate differently. Teach the one rule, and don't mark it hard in speaking tasks.
Homework

Online lesson 2.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_04.html. Listen to the model sentences and the "walk this afternoon" conversation with 🔊 and say them out loud. Do the online exercises and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 2.1: finish Ex 5 ("My six predictions": 6 real or invented plans with If I…, I'll…, including one unless and one when), "My new words" and the journal (one zero conditional about a daily habit + one first conditional plan for tomorrow).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B If it rains, I'll stay home. · Q2 A unless · Q3 (new) B When I get home tonight, I'll call you. Tip Time (Ex 1): 1 doesn't boil over (H) · 2 gets soft again (H) · 3 charges faster (H) · 4 you'll have bad luck (S) · 5 you'll get some money (S) · 6 will start at two (F) · 7 won't go out (F).

2.2 · C5

Second Conditional — Dreams, Dilemmas & Advice

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • form the second conditional: If + past simple, would / 'd / wouldn't + base verb: If I won the lottery, I'd buy a little house by the sea.
  • understand that the past verb means unreal, not past time (If I had a car… = I don't have a car now)
  • choose first (real, likely) or second (imaginary, unlikely) conditional for the same idea
  • use were for all persons, and give friendly advice with If I were you, I'd… / I wouldn't…
  • use could and might in the result: If I had a car, I could drive you home.
  • discuss everyday dilemmas: say what they would do, give a reason, and agree or disagree politely
Materials
  • Workbook 2.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "What would you do if…?")
  • Slides 9–13 · Flashcards (dreams & dilemmas) · Conditional-plans role-play A/B (warmer, 1 set per pair) · Dilemma cards (1 set per group of 3–4) + reaction-phrase strips · Advice role-play A/B cards (1 set per pair) · two cards per learner: "1st · REAL" and "2nd · DREAM" (or learners write 1 and 2 on scrap paper) · talking tokens · "imagine a person" cards
  • Listening scripts 2.2-A (Sam and Rosa daydream) and 2.2-B (real or dream?)

Left: the reality line from C4. Now fill the IMAGINED end: If + past simple, would ('d) + base verb. Under it, a thought bubble 💭 and the words "past verb = NOT REAL (not past time!)".

Center: If I won the lottery, I'd buy a house by the sea. · If I had more time, I would learn the guitar. · I wouldn't tell anyone. · What would you do if you found a wallet? · If I were you, I'd… (I / he / she / it + were)

Right: two columns. 📅 1st · REAL: If I have time, I'll call you. (maybe I will) · 💭 2nd · DREAM: If I had time, I'd call you. (but I don't). Below: result can be could (= would be able to) / might (= would maybe).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: plans with conditions8S–SReview C4. Pairs use the conditional-plans role-play A/B cards: A invites B to a weekend activity, B answers with conditions ("If it's sunny, I'll come. Unless I have to work, I'll be there."). Swap once. Two learners read a life hack from the Life Hack Wall.
Lead-in: a million dollars5T–S → S–SSlide 9. "Imagine: you open your phone and you won a million dollars. What would you do first?" Pairs brainstorm 3 ideas (1 min), and you write good answers on the board. Ask: "Is this real?" (No, just a dream.) "Did you win in the past?" (No!) Circle won: "Past form, but not past time. Interesting…"
Grammar: second conditional12T–SSlide 10. Meaning (imaginary, unlikely, impossible; now or future), form, the "past = unreal" idea, would → 'd, questions (What would you do if…?), could / might in the result. CCQs and drill (below).
Pronunciation: 'd, wouldn't, were5T–SMinimal pair game: T says "I go by bus." / "I'd go by bus." Learners show ✋ (real, now) or 💭 (dream, I'd). Then wouldn't /ˈwʊdənt/ (two syllables) and weak were /wər/ in "If I were you".
Real or dream? (first vs second)8T–SSlide 11. Read Script 2.2-B: 8 sentences. Learners hold up "1st · REAL" or "2nd · DREAM". Then the key question: "Do I really think it might happen?" Do 3 pairs of sentences on the board (have / had time).
Listening: Sam and Rosa7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 2.2-A twice (be both voices, or a confident learner reads Rosa). Learners answer true / false and correct the false ones. Pairs compare, then find three "I'd" sentences.
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 and 3 alone, then check in pairs. Board-check the first vs second items (Ex 3) only.
If I were you, I'd…7T–S → S–SSlide 12. The advice formula, and why were. Model with a learner: "I'm always tired in the morning." — "If I were you, I'd go to bed earlier." Then pairs use the advice role-play A/B cards: A reads a small problem, B gives advice, A reacts ("Good idea!" / "Hmm, maybe."). Swap. Workbook Ex 4 for homework or early finishers.
Freer: "What would you do if…?" dilemma discussion18S–S (groups of 3–4) → T–SSlide 13. Groups, dilemma cards face down, reaction-phrase strips, 3 talking tokens each. (1) The asker turns a card and reads it. Everyone answers in turn: "If I found a wallet, I'd… because…" (round-robin first). (2) Open discussion: agree / disagree / "That depends" (12 min, about 4 cards). (3) Each group chooses the card with the most different answers and the reporter tells the class: "Three of us would…, but Ana would…" (4 min). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SContent first: "Which dilemma was the hardest?" Then 4 heard sentences on the board, e.g. "If I would have money, I'd travel." · "If I find a wallet, I would give it back." · "If I was you, I'll call her." · one good one. Learners fix them.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 2.2; Workbook Ex 5 "What would you do if…?" (6 answers) and the journal.
Total908 + 5 + 12 + 5 + 8 + 7 + 10 + 7 + 18 + 5 + 5
If you run long

This class has a lot of talk, which is good. Cut the advice role-play to 3 cards each (the workbook Ex 4 covers the rest at home) and do only 4 sentences of Script 2.2-B. Never cut the dilemma discussion: it is the real communicative task of the lesson and where I'd… becomes automatic.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: imaginary, unlikely or impossible — now or in the future. The speaker steps out of reality.

"If I won the lottery, I'd quit my job." Did I win the lottery? (No.) Is it likely? (No, very unlikely.) Is won about the past? (No! It's about an imagined now / future.) Am I going to quit my job? (No, it's a dream.)
"If I had a car, I'd drive you home." Do I have a car? (No.) Can I drive you home? (No.) · "If animals could talk, life would be strange." Can animals talk? (No, impossible.)

Meaning: first vs second = how likely do I think it is?

"If I have time, I'll call you." / "If I had time, I'd call you." Which one: maybe I'll have time? (The first.) Which one: I don't have time? (The second.) · "If it rains, I'll stay home." Is rain realistic? (Yes.) "If it rained gold, we'd all be rich." Realistic? (No, impossible.)

Meaning: advice.

"If I were you, I'd take the job." Am I you? (No, impossible.) Is it advice? (Yes.) Is it strong like an order? (No, soft and friendly.) Is it softer than "You should take the job"? (Yes.) · "If I were you, I wouldn't worry." Should you worry? (No.)

Form.

PositiveIf + past simple, would ('d) + base verbIf I had more time, I'd learn to paint. · She'd be happier if she lived near the sea.
Negativedidn't + verb · wouldn't + base verbIf they didn't work so much, they wouldn't be so tired.
QuestionWhat would + subject + do + if + past…?What would you do if you found a wallet? · Would you tell anyone?
bewere for all persons (standard) · was = informal speechIf I were rich… · If it were warmer… · If I were you, I'd…
Other resultscould (= would be able to) · might (= would maybe)If I had a car, I could drive you. · If she asked me, I might say yes.
Don't mixpast + would ✅ · present + will ✅ · past + will ❌If I had time, I will call you. ❌

Pronunciation. In speech would is almost always 'd: I'd /aɪd/, you'd /jud/, she'd, we'd, they'd. If learners drop the /d/ ("I buy a house"), the sentence sounds real, not imagined, so this matters: exaggerate the /d/ at first. Before a /t/ or /d/ sound it almost disappears ("I'd take it") — accept that. wouldn't is /ˈwʊdənt/: the l is silent, and the middle is a small /ə/. were is weak in "If I were you" /ɪf aɪ wər ju/, stressed only in short answers. The question What would you do if…? links: "what-wud-ya-DO". The if-clause rises ↗, the result falls ↘, as in C4.

Drill script
  1. Back-chain: "by the sea" → "a house by the sea" → "I'd buy a house by the sea" → "If I won the lottery, I'd buy a house by the sea." Choral ×3, halves of the room, then 4–5 individuals.
  2. Make it a dream (first → second): T: "If I have time, I'll call you." → S: "If I had time, I'd call you." T: "If she knows, she'll tell us." → S: "If she knew, she'd tell us." T: "If it's warm, we'll swim." → S: "If it were warm, we'd swim."
  3. Question and answer chain: S1 to S2: "What would you do if you had a free day tomorrow?" S2: "I'd sleep late! What would you do if you could fly?" S2 → S3 … (open pairs, then closed pairs for 1 minute).
  4. were drill: T: "I / rich" → S: "If I were rich…" T: "it / warmer" → S: "If it were warmer…" T: "he / here" → S: "If he were here…" Then finish each with I'd / we'd / he'd.
  5. Advice: T mimes a problem (yawning, shaking a slow phone, looking at a watch). Learners: "If I were you, I'd go to bed earlier." / "…I'd buy a new phone." / "…I'd leave earlier."
Dilemma discussion — how to run it
  • Groups of 3–4. Dilemma cards face down, one reaction-phrase strip each, 3 talking tokens each. Roles: asker (turns and reads the card), timekeeper (about 1 minute per person), reporter. Change the asker with every card.
  • Round-robin first: everyone answers the card in turn with a full sentence and a reason: "If I found a wallet, I'd take it to the police station, because the owner might go there." The options A / B / C on the card help; "your own idea" is always allowed.
  • Then open discussion (tokens: one per turn): "I'd do the same." · "Really? I wouldn't. I'd…" · "That depends. If there was a phone number, I'd call." · "Why would you…?"
  • All dilemmas are light and everyday on purpose (a found wallet, too much change, a lottery win, a superpower, an ugly gift, a free vacation). Some touch on honesty; there is no one right answer, and nobody is judged. Remind the class: "These are imaginary. Answer as yourself, as your 'imagine a person' card, or invent an answer. You can say 'Pass' and take the next card."
  • Walk around and note sentences for feedback. Don't correct during the discussion (fluency first). If one learner is doing all the talking, stop by and say, "Let's hear from someone who still has tokens."
  • Fast groups: use the two blank cards to write their own light dilemma for another group.
