ICQs before the advice role-play and the dilemma discussion
Advice cards: Who has the problem, A or B? (A.) What are B's first words? ("If I were you, I'd…") · "If I was you" or "If I were you"? (Were.)
Dilemmas: Who reads the card? (The asker.) · First, does everyone answer, or only one person? (Everyone, in turn.) · What do you add after your answer? (A reason, with "because".) · What does a token do? (One token, one turn.) · Don't like a card? (Say "Pass" and take the next one.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Use the picture dilemma cards (the emoji + a partner reading aloud) and the phrase strip "If I…, I'd… because…". Give a ready answer frame on a card: "If I found a wallet, I'd _____." Target: the chunk I'd… + one verb, for 4 dilemmas, and one "If I were you, I'd…" piece of advice. Don't insist on were vs was at this stage.

Stretch

Use could and might in answers ("I might keep a little for myself — no, I'm joking!"). Answer with a condition inside a condition ("If there was an address, I'd take it back myself, but if not, I'd…"). In the report stage, compare the group ("Most of us would…, but…"). Early finishers write their own light dilemma card for another group.

L1 watch-outs
  • "If I would have money…" (would in both halves): German (wenn ich … hätte / würde), Dutch, Swedish and Norwegian speakers, and many others by analogy. Teach: would lives in the result only. Use the same red ❌ gesture as for "if … will" in C4.
  • "If I have money, I would…" (present in the if-clause): Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai and Indonesian speakers (no past form to signal "unreal"), and Japanese / Korean speakers whose conditional is one suffix. Point at the board: "Dream → past."
  • "If I had money, I bought a car" (past in both halves): Russian and Ukrainian (если бы…, я бы купил), Polish. Their L1 uses the past in both halves; the English result needs would.
  • Dropped 'd: "If I won, I buy a house." Spanish, Portuguese, Vietnamese and Chinese speakers often drop final /d/. Use the ✋ / 💭 listening game and exaggerate the /d/.
  • "If I am you" / "If I was you": Spanish (si yo fuera tú) and Portuguese speakers usually get were easily (subjunctive); Arabic, Chinese and Tagalog speakers may say "If I am you." Teach "If I were you, I'd…" as one fixed chunk.
  • Strong "should" advice: learners from cultures with direct advice (German, Dutch, Russian, Hebrew) may sound bossy with "You must…". Learners from indirect cultures (Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Filipino) may find any advice uncomfortable. "If I were you…" is the polite English middle.
Homework

Online lesson 2.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_05.html. Listen to the model sentences and the daydreaming conversation with 🔊 and read it aloud; do the exercises and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 2.2: finish Ex 4 (advice) if not done, Ex 5 ("What would you do if…?": 6 answers with one If I were… and one could / might), "My new words" and the journal. Note: the online project list includes "could live anywhere"; learners may choose any other prompt.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B If I had money, I would travel. · Q2 A If it rains, I'll stay home. · Q3 (new) B If I were you, I'd buy a new one. Sam and Rosa (Ex 1): 1 F (Rosa would travel for a year; Sam would buy a little house by the sea) · 2 T · 3 T · 4 F ("If I were you, I'd take it.") · 5 T · 6 T.

2.3 · C6

Wish & If Only — Small Wishes, Real Fixes

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use wish / if only + past simple for a present wish: I wish I had more free time. If only it were Friday!
  • understand the "flip": a wish is the opposite of reality (I wish I lived near work = I don't live near work)
  • use were for all persons after wish (I wish it were sunny), and wish + could for ability (I wish I could sing)
  • use wish + would to complain kindly about someone or something else (I wish the bus would come on time), never about themselves
  • choose hope (possible) or wish (not real now)
  • say 6+ small, everyday wishes (weather, skills, commute) and suggest a real fix with a conditional
Materials
  • Workbook 2.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My six small wishes")
  • Slides 14–18 · Flashcards (small wishes: weather, skills, commute) · Wish Mingle sheet (1 per learner) · Small Wishes, Real Fixes cards (1 set per pair) · "imagine a person" cards
  • Listening scripts 2.3-A (Maya and Leo on a gray day) and 2.3-B (dictation)

Left: the reality line, complete. Add under IMAGINED: I wish / If only + past. Two columns: REAL NOW | MY WISH: It's raining. | I wish it were sunny. · I can't sing. | I wish I could sing. · I live far from work. | I wish I lived closer. · I have a lot of work. | I wish I didn't have so much work.

Center: 😤 wish + would = other people / things, annoying: I wish the bus would come on time. · I wish my neighbor would turn the music down. A big ❌: "I wish I would be taller." → I wish I were taller.

Right: 🤞 hope = possible: I hope it's sunny tomorrow. · 🌠 wish = not real now: I wish it were sunny (but it isn't). · If only…! = a stronger wish (!).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: quick dilemmas7T–S → S–SReview C5. Quick-fire questions: "What would you do if you had a free day tomorrow? … if you could speak every language? … if you found $20 on the bus?" Then pairs give each other one piece of advice: A says a small problem from homework Ex 4, B: "If I were you, I'd…".
Lead-in: a gray day5T–SSlide 14. Show the umbrella and sunny flashcards. Look out the window (or at the umbrella card): "It's raining. I want sun. I wish it were sunny!" Elicit more: "I'm tired. I wish…" (I had more time to sleep / it were Friday). Write two learner wishes on the board.
Grammar: wish + past / were12T–SSlide 15. The link to C5: same "unreal past." The flip (REAL NOW | MY WISH on the board). were for I / he / she / it; was is informal. If only…! = stronger. CCQs and drill (below).
wish + could · hope vs wish7T–SI can't sing → I wish I could sing. Use the skill flashcards (sing, swim, dance, play the guitar, cook). Then hope vs wish: "The forecast says sun tomorrow: I hope it's sunny tomorrow. Today it's raining: I wish it were sunny."
wish + would8T–S → S–SSlide 16. Mime an annoyance (a phone ringing again and again, a bus that never comes): "I wish the bus would come!" Other people / things only. CCQs. Pairs turn 4 complaints into wishes: "The printer never works." → "I wish the printer would work!"
Listening: Maya and Leo6T–S → SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 2.3-A twice. Learners tick who wishes what and write the pattern (past / were / could / would). Quick check.
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2, 3 and 5 alone, then check in pairs. Board-check Ex 3 (which pattern?) and Ex 5 (hope or wish?). Ex 4 for homework.
Wish Mingle (Find Someone Who)12S–S (mingle) → T–SSlide 17. Drill the question first: "Do you wish you could sing?" — "Yes, I do! / No, I don't." Learners walk and ask; one name per box, then the extra question with would: "What song would you sing?" (8 min). Report with he / she: "Omar wishes he could cook rice like his grandmother." (4 min). ICQs first.
Freer: Small Wishes, Real Fixes13S–SSlide 17. Pairs, one set of cards. (1) A takes a card, reads the situation and makes a wish ("I wish the bus would come on time!"). B answers with a dream ("If it came on time, what would you do with the extra time?") or a fix ("If I were you, I'd take the earlier bus." / "If you leave ten minutes earlier, you'll get a seat."). Swap every card (7 min). (2) Each learner writes 3 small wishes of their own on the weather, a skill and the commute (or from a card), and shares them with a new partner, who suggests a fix (6 min). ICQs first.
Feedback + module check5T–SContent: "Which fix will you try?" Delayed correction: "I wish I can swim." · "I wish I would be more organized." · "I wish it will stop raining." · "I wish you come to my party." (→ I hope you can come). Then learners tick the Module 2 "I can…" lists.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 2.3; "My six small wishes" and the journal; preview C7: "Next class: reported speech. She said she was tired. The past again!"
Total907 + 5 + 12 + 7 + 8 + 6 + 10 + 12 + 13 + 5 + 5
Keep wishes small — say it out loud

Before the mingle and before the Small Wishes task, say: "Today we make small, everyday wishes: the weather, a skill, the bus, your phone, a noisy neighbor. You don't need to share anything private. Choose a card, invent a wish, or say 'Pass'." Never give or invite examples about family far away, home countries, migration, health or money problems, and don't ask "What do you wish about your life?" The online lesson's "Mindset" box and its "I wish I hadn't eaten so much last night" sentence (a past wish) are not used in class. If a learner offers a deep regret anyway, say "Thank you for sharing that," and gently move to the next card.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: wish + past = I want the present to be different. The past form is the same "unreal" signal as in the second conditional.

"I wish I lived closer to work." Do I live close to work? (No.) Do I want to? (Yes.) Is it about the past? (No, now.) · "I wish I didn't have so much homework." Do I have a lot of homework? (Yes!)
"I wish it were sunny." Is it sunny now? (No.) "If only it were Friday!" Is it Friday? (No.) Which is stronger, I wish or If only? (If only.)

Meaning: wish + could = ability.

"I wish I could swim." Can I swim? (No.) Would I like to? (Yes.) · "I wish I can swim" — OK? (No. can → could.)

Meaning: wish + would = someone / something else, and I'm annoyed.

"I wish the bus would come on time." Does the bus come on time? (No.) Am I happy about it? (No, annoyed.) Is it about me or about the bus? (The bus.) · "I wish I would be taller" — OK? (No. About me → "I wish I were taller.")

Meaning: hope vs wish.

"I hope it's sunny tomorrow." Is it possible? (Yes, maybe.) "I wish it were sunny." Is it sunny now? (No.) Your friend has a job interview tomorrow. Hope or wish? ("I hope you get it!")

Form.

Present wishwish / If only + past simpleI wish I had more time. · She wishes she spoke Italian. · If only I knew the answer!
Negativewish + didn't + verb · wish + weren'tI wish I didn't have so much work. · I wish my phone weren't so slow.
bewish + were (all persons) · was = informalI wish I were taller. · I wish it were the weekend.
Abilitywish + could + base verbI wish I could sing. · He wishes he could drive.
Annoyancewish + [someone / something else] + would / wouldn't + verbI wish it would stop raining. · I wish he wouldn't interrupt me.
hopehope + present / will (real, possible)I hope it's sunny tomorrow. · I hope you can come.

Pronunciation. wish /wɪʃ/: a short /ɪ/ and a soft /ʃ/ ("sh"), not /wiʃ/ or /wis/. Stress falls on the wished-for word, not on wish: "I wish it were SUNny." · "I wish I could SING." were is weak /wər/. If only has strong stress and emotion: "If ONly it were FRIday!" (a sigh helps). could /kʊd/: silent l. wish + would often has a slightly annoyed, falling tone: "I wish the bus would COME." ↘

Drill script
  1. The flip (point at REAL NOW on the board): T: "It's raining." → S: "I wish it were sunny." T: "I can't swim." → S: "I wish I could swim." T: "I don't have a car." → S: "I wish I had a car." T: "I have a lot of work." → S: "I wish I didn't have so much work."
  2. Stress drill: "I wish it were SUNny." / "I wish I could SING." / "I wish I lived CLOSer." Choral, then individuals. Then If only with feeling: "If ONly it were FRIday!"
  3. Annoyance chain: T: "The bus is late again." → S1: "I wish the bus would come on time!" S1: "My neighbor's music is loud." → S2: "I wish my neighbor would turn it down!" … around the room.
  4. hope or wish? T: "Tomorrow's forecast: sunny." → S: "I hope it's sunny tomorrow." T: "Today: rain." → S: "I wish it were sunny today." T: "Your friend has a test tomorrow." → S: "I hope you pass!"
  5. Wish → fix (preview of the freer task): S1: "I wish I could cook rice better." S2: "If I were you, I'd watch a video." / "If you use less water, it'll be perfect."
Small Wishes, Real Fixes — how to run it
  • Pairs, one set of 16 cards face down: ☀️ weather, 🎸 skills, 🚌 commute, 🏠 home & phone. Each card has a small situation and a pattern hint (past / were / could / would).
  • A takes a card, reads the situation and makes a wish with feeling: "It's so cold. I wish it were warmer!" B answers in one of two ways: 💭 Dream: a second-conditional question: "If it were warmer, what would you do?" or 🔧 Fix: "If I were you, I'd…" / a first conditional: "If you wear gloves, your hands won't hurt." A answers or reacts ("Good idea!" / "I might try that."). Swap after every card.
  • Round 2: each learner writes 3 small wishes of their own (weather, a skill, the commute) in the workbook table, or copies wishes from a card or an "imagine a person" card. Share with a new partner, who suggests a fix.
  • Keep it light, practical and a little funny. There are no deep regrets in this task — see the amber box above.
ICQs before the mingle and Small Wishes
Mingle: What is the question? ("Do you wish you could…?" / "Do you wish it were…?") · "Yes, I do" → what do you do? (Write the name and ask the extra question.) · How many names per box? (One.)
Small Wishes: A makes a wish. What does B do? (Asks a dream question or gives a fix.) · Can B say "If I were you, I'd…"? (Yes.) · Big wishes or small wishes? (Small, everyday.) · Do you have to share your real wishes? (No. Card, invented, or "Pass.")
Differentiation
Literacy support

Use two patterns only: I wish I could… (with the skill flashcards: sing, swim, dance, cook, play the guitar) and I wish it were… (with the weather flashcards: sunny, hot, cold). The partner reads the Small Wishes cards aloud. In the mingle, give a sheet with the questions fully written, and learners mark ✓ and the first letter of the name. Target: 4 spoken wishes and 1 "If I were you, I'd…".

Stretch

Add a second-conditional result to each wish: "I wish I lived closer to work. If I did, I'd walk every day." Use I wish there were… for the neighborhood ("I wish there were more buses at night."). In the fix, give two options with unless or might. Early finishers write a short, friendly complaint message to a roommate with one I wish you would… and one If you…, I'll….

L1 watch-outs
  • hope / wish mix-up: Arabic atamanna (أتمنى), Tagalog sana, Chinese xīwàng (希望), Vietnamese ước / hy vọng overlap, and French souhaiter can mean both. Expect "I wish you come to my party" and "I wish I will pass the test." Use the hope / wish drill: possible → hope.
  • "I wish I have / I wish I can" (present after wish): Chinese, Vietnamese, Korean, Japanese and Arabic speakers. Point at the flip on the board: "Wish → one step back."
  • "I wish that you would come" for a polite invitation: Spanish speakers (ojalá vengas = I hope you come). Teach: invitation → "I hope you can come!"
  • "I wish I would…" about oneself: German and Dutch speakers (ich wünschte, ich würde) and many others by analogy. would is for other people and things.
  • /ɪ/ vs /i/ and /ʃ/: Spanish, Portuguese, Arabic and Tagalog speakers may say "weesh" or "wis" (Spanish has no /ʃ/ in many accents; Tagalog has /ʃ/ only in loans). Model "wish, fish, dish."
Homework

Online lesson 2.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_06.html. Listen to the model sentences and the Maya and Leo conversation with 🔊 and read it aloud; do the exercises and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 2.3: finish Ex 4 (complaint → wish), "My six small wishes" (weather, skills, commute: real, from a card, or invented), "My new words" and the journal (3 small wishes + one small action for each).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B I wish I were on vacation. · Q2 A I wish my neighbor would turn the music down. · Q3 (new) B I hope it's sunny tomorrow. Maya and Leo (Ex 1): 1 Maya · were · 2 Leo · would · 3 Maya · past (lived) · 4 Leo · could · 5 Maya · were · 6 Leo · would.

Closing the Module

End of C6: Module 2 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, the finished reality line: If you heat ice, it melts. (always true) → If it rains, I'll take the bus. (real, likely) → If I won the lottery, I'd… (imagined) → I wish it were sunny. (not real now, but I want it). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C7 warmer and in the C13 progress-test catch-up.

Watch for in Module 3: reported speech also "steps back" a tense ("I'm tired" → She said she was tired). Learners who just learned "past = unreal" may be confused: in reported speech the past is about time (the words were said before). Say it clearly in C7. Keep a 2-minute conditional warmer in C7–C9 ("If it rains this weekend, I'll… / If I could fly, I'd… / I wish…").

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: zero vs first conditional, no will after if / when, when vs if, unless, the comma, the second conditional (+ / − / ?), first vs second choice, were, If I were you, I'd…, wish + past / were / could / would, and hope vs wish.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 3 — Reporting & Connecting Ideas

Three classes that let learners pass on other people's words and join their own ideas into longer sentences. First comes reported statements: the tense backshift, pronoun and time-word changes, and say vs tell. Then reported questions (statement word order, if / whether) and reported requests and commands (told / asked me (not) to…). Last, relative clauses: who, which, that, where, when, whose, defining vs non-defining clauses, and the commas that change the meaning.

3 lessons Lessons 3.1–3.3 Classes C7–C9 Weeks 4–5 90 min per class CEFR B1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups, mingle) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-3-reporting.html. Print materials/module-3/classroom-materials.html before C7. It holds the flashcards, the Gossip Chain news cards, the Survey Report cards, the Message Relay cards and "While you were out" notes, the "Who am I? / What is it?" definition cards and bingo, the sentence-combining strips, the new identity cards and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. A2 Lesson 6.2 taught defining relative clauses with who, which, that, where. Many learners also met said and told as everyday past verbs. Check what they know in each lead-in and go faster where they are strong. The new B1 work is the backshift system, reported questions and commands, and in 3.3 non-defining clauses, commas, whose / when and dropping the object pronoun.
  • Fluency before accuracy. In every freer task, listen and take notes; don't stop a speaker to fix a backshift. The feedback stage is where you fix errors, without names. Praise the message first ("Great story! Everyone understood."), then the form. At B1 a report that is clear but has one un-shifted verb is a success.
  • Dominant speakers. Reporting tasks are natural "turn" tasks, so use them: give every group a talking-turn token (a pen or a coin; you speak only when you hold it), give the strongest speaker a job (the checker, the timekeeper, the "last person in the chain"), and use "report your partner, not yourself" so quieter learners get the floor. Say it kindly: "Thanks, Omar. Let's hear from someone who hasn't spoken yet."
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons sometimes use British forms (film, flat, mum, holiday, neighbour, maths, moved house, memorise, practise, Mr with no period). Teach movie, apartment, mom, vacation, neighbor, math, moved, memorize, practice, Mr. Accept the British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

What they said → what they asked and wanted → who and what we mean. In C7 learners pass on news: She said she was starting a new job. In C8 they report the questions people asked them and the things people asked or told them to do: She asked me whether I liked cooking. The doctor told me not to eat before the test. In C9 they stop reporting and start connecting: they describe people, places and things exactly (the woman who called you) and add extra facts inside commas (My brother, who lives in Chicago, is a nurse.). Every class ends with the same kind of freer task: tell other people something real (or imagined) in longer, joined-up English.

Trauma-informed: whose words, whose questions?

Reported speech can bring back hard conversations: an immigration or asylum interview, a police officer, a court, a difficult doctor's visit, a landlord who wants rent. The online lesson 3.2 project suggests "a police interview" as one option. In class, keep every task everyday: friends, neighbors, work, a routine clinic visit, the building manager fixing a sink. Never ask "What did the officer ask you?", "What did the interviewer ask you at immigration?" or "What did your family say when you left?" The gossip in C7 is always about invented people on the news cards, never about classmates. For every personal task (reporting real things people said, the survey, "the people and places in my life"), offer three choices out loud: your real life, a new identity card (in the classroom materials), or something imagined. Anyone can say "Pass!" to a survey question. Say it every time, so choosing a card is normal.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): the backshift cards, the reporting verbs, the relative-pronoun cards and the picture cards. Card stock helps. Cut them before C7.
  • For C7: one set of Gossip Chain news cards (12 cards) and a small piece of paper for each line of learners.
  • For C8: one Survey card per learner (6 different topics, so neighbors have different questions) and one report frame per learner. Tape the 8 Message Relay cards on the walls around the room before class. Print 2 "While you were out" note sheets per pair.
  • For C9: one set of "Who am I? / What is it?" definition cards per group of 3–4, one bingo grid per learner, and one set of sentence-combining strips + comma cards per pair. An envelope per pair keeps the strips together.
  • Print the new identity cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Optional: a real phone to "play" the voicemail in C7 and C8 (read the script while holding the phone to your ear). Learners enjoy it and it makes the situation clear.

Lesson Plans

3.1 · C7

Reported Speech — Statements

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • change direct speech into a reported statement: "I'm tired." → She said (that) she was tired.
  • apply the backshift after said / told: present → past, present perfect and past simple → past perfect, will → would, can → could, may → might, must → had to; and know that would, could, might, should and the past perfect don't change
  • change pronouns and possessives to their own point of view (I → he / she, my → his / her, you → I)
  • change time and place words when the time or place has really changed (tomorrow → the next day, here → there)
  • choose said (no person) or told + person and pass on six or more things people said
Materials
  • Workbook 3.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "Things people said to me")
  • Slides 3–8 · Backshift flashcards (am → was, will → would…) · say / tell cards · Gossip Chain news cards (1 set) + paper slips · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.1-A (Lina's voicemail) and 3.1-B (dictation)

Left: two speech bubbles. Bubble: "I'm tired." → arrow → She said (that) she was tired. Under it: the "rewind" line: is / am → was · work → worked · have finished → had finished · saw → had seen · will → would · can → could · may → might · must → had to.

Center: pronouns: I → he / she · we → they · my → his / her · you (me) → I. Time and place: now → then · today → that day · tomorrow → the next day · yesterday → the day before · here → there · this → that · ago → before.

Right: the key card: SAY + (that)… · TELL + person + (that)… · She told me… ✔ · She said me ✘ · She told to me ✘

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: I wish…8S–SReview C6 (2.3, wish and if only). Pairs finish three sentence heads on the board: "I wish I could… · I wish my neighbor would… · If only I had…" (real or imagined; light topics). Then each learner tells the class what their partner wished: "Ana wishes she could drive." This is a secret preview of reporting. Note one or two good lines for later.
Lead-in: my neighbor's news6T–STell a short invented story: "Yesterday I met my neighbor, Mrs. Lee. She said, 'I'm moving to Florida! I've sold my car.'" Write her words in a bubble. Ask: "What did Mrs. Lee say?" Elicit anything; then write the report next to the bubble: "She said she was moving to Florida. She said she had sold her car." "What's different?" Let learners notice: no quotation marks, I → she, the verbs "go back."
Grammar: the backshift15T–SSlides 3–4. Meaning (passing on someone's words later), form (the rewind line), the "don't change" group (would, could, might, should, past perfect), and when backshift is optional (said today and still true). CCQs (below). Stick the backshift cards on the board as you go.
Drill7T–S → SRewind drill with the cards (see Drill script), then the chain: T says a direct line, learners report it in one breath.
Pronouns, time words, say / tell10T–SSlides 5–6. Pronouns: "From where I'm standing now, who is this person?" Time words: a Monday-to-Wednesday calendar on the board ("On Monday Tom said, 'I'll call you tomorrow.' Today is Wednesday."). Then say vs tell with the key card and the three ✘ examples. CCQs.
Listening: Lina's voicemail8T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Hold a phone to your ear and read Script 3.1-A twice (as Lina). Learners complete the reported sentences. Pairs compare, then read the report aloud as if telling a friend: "Guess what! Lina said she was starting a new job!"
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone (backshift, say / tell, time words, find the mistake), then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on. Early finishers start "Things people said to me."
Freer: Gossip Chain16S–S (lines) → T–SSlide 8. Lines of 5–6. Round 1 (6 min): the first learner reads a news card in silence, gives it back, and whispers a report to the next learner: "Mrs. Lee said she was moving to Florida…" Each learner passes on what they heard with said / told me. The last learner writes it on paper. Compare with the card: one point for every correct backshifted verb in the final version. Round 2 (6 min): new card, new first person; the last person reports it to the whole class. Then (4 min) pairs tell each other 3 things someone really said to them this week (or from a new identity card). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed error correction: write 4 sentences you heard (2 good, 2 with errors, no names): "She said me she was tired." · "He said he will call." · "She told that she had a new job." · "Maria said I was busy." (about herself). Learners fix them together. Praise one great "chain" and one clear report.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 3.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total908 + 6 + 15 + 7 + 10 + 8 + 10 + 16 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: reported speech. We pass on someone's words later, in our own sentence, not their exact words. Direct speech = the exact words in "quotation marks."

"Mrs. Lee said she was moving to Florida." Who is moving: me or Mrs. Lee? (Mrs. Lee.) Did she say "was"? (No, she said "I'm moving.") Is she moving now, in the past? (No, she's moving soon. "Was" is only because I'm reporting it later.) Are these her exact words? (No, my words.)

Meaning: the backshift. After a past reporting verb (said, told), we usually move the verb one step back, like pressing "rewind" once.

"He said he would help." What were his exact words? ("I will help.") · "She said she had seen the accident." Her exact words? ("I saw the accident." or "I've seen the accident.") · "He said he had to go." Exact words? ("I must go." or "I have to go.")
When is backshift optional? "She said she lives in Rome." Does she still live in Rome now? (Yes, so no change is OK.) · "He says he's tired." Is says past? (No, present: no backshift.) · Not sure? (Backshift. It's always safe after said.)

Meaning: time and place. Change them only if the time or place has really changed.

On Monday Tom said, "I'll call you tomorrow." Today is Wednesday. Is "tomorrow" still tomorrow? (No! It was Tuesday.) So: Tom said he would call me… (the next day). · Your friend said it this morning. Today → that day? (No, it's still today.)

Meaning: say vs tell.

"She told me she was busy." Who did she speak to? (Me.) · "She said she was busy." Do we know who she spoke to? (No, not in the sentence.) · Can I say "She said me…"? (No! "She told me…") · "She told to me"? (No to.)

Form.

Statementsubject + said + (that) + backshifted clauseHe said (that) he was hungry.
With a personsubject + told + person + (that) + clauseShe told me (that) she had lost her phone.
Present → pastam / is → was · are → were · work → worked · am working → was working"We're watching TV." → They said they were watching TV.
→ past perfecthave / has + p.p. → had + p.p. · past simple → had + p.p."I saw it." → He said he had seen it.
Modalswill → would · can → could · may → might · must → had to · would / could / might / should stay"You can borrow my car." → He told me I could borrow his car.
Alsois going to → was going to"I'm going to buy a car." → She said she was going to buy a car.

Pronunciation. that is weak /ðət/ or dropped completely in speech: "She said she was tired" is the normal spoken form. told /toʊld/: say the final d; learners who drop it say "tol' me," which is fine in fast speech but write the d. The past perfect is usually contracted: "He said he'd finished" /hid/, and would too: "She said she'd call." Teach learners to hear "he'd" as either he had or he would (the next verb tells you: he'd finished = had · he'd call = would). Storytelling intonation: a high, excited start: "Guess what! She told me she was getting married!"

Drill script
  1. Rewind drill (point at a backshift card): T: "am" → S: "was." T: "will" → S: "would." T: "can" → S: "could." T: "have seen" → S: "had seen." T: "must" → S: "had to." T: "might" → S: "might! No change." Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Report it: T (as a character, with a gesture): "I'm hungry!" → S: "He said he was hungry." T: "I've lost my keys!" → S: "She said she'd lost her keys." T: "I'll pay you back." → S: "She said she would pay me back."
  3. Say or tell? T gives a report without the verb and a person or no person: T: "… me she was busy" → S: "She told me she was busy." T: "… she was busy" → S: "She said she was busy."
  4. Time travel (point at the Monday / Wednesday calendar): T: "tomorrow" → S: "the next day." T: "yesterday" → S: "the day before." T: "here" → S: "there." T: "two weeks ago" → S: "two weeks before."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 says a true or invented line ("I'm going to the dentist tomorrow."); S2 turns to S3: "Ana said she was going to the dentist the next day." Then all pairs, 1 minute.
Gossip Chain — how to run it
  • Lines of 5–6 learners, standing or sitting in a row. The first learner reads a news card (about an invented person in the "Maple Street" apartment building) for 30 seconds, then gives the card back to you.
  • The first learner whispers a report, not the exact words: "Mrs. Lee said she was moving to Florida the next month. She said she had sold her car…" Each learner passes on what they heard, starting "[Name] said…" or "[Name] told me…" (not "you said"). One whisper only; no "Can you say it again?"
  • The last learner writes the report on a paper slip. Hold up the card and read the original. Score: 1 point for each correct backshifted verb in the written report, and 1 bonus point if the news is still basically true. The funniest changes get a laugh, not a penalty.
  • Rotate: the first person moves to the end, so a new learner reads the next card. Give the strongest speaker the job of the last person (writer and checker) in round 1: it keeps them busy and gives the quieter learners the speaking turns.
  • The gossip is about the people on the cards only, never about classmates or real neighbors. Say it before you start: "These people are not real. Please be kind."
  • Last 4 minutes: pairs report 3 real things someone said to them this week ("My daughter told me she wanted a cat."), or 3 lines from a new identity card.
ICQs before the Gossip Chain
Do you say the exact words from the card? (No, you report: "She said she was…") · What are the first words? ("Mrs. Lee said…" / "Mrs. Lee told me…") · How many times can you whisper? (Once.) · Who writes? (The last person.) · Can we gossip about people in our class? (No! Only the card people.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on three changes only: am / is → was, will → would, can → could, plus I → he / she. Give a phrase strip: "He said he was… / She said she would… / She told me she could…". In the Gossip Chain, stand next to the learner and read the news card aloud once, quietly; give them the middle of the line (listen and repeat, not first or last). Target: 3 spoken reports with was / would / could.

Stretch

Use a variety of reporting verbs beyond said / told: explained, mentioned, promised, admitted ("He admitted he had forgotten."). Notice when backshift is optional ("She said she lives in Rome," still true). In the chain, add a reaction: "…and I said I couldn't believe it!" Early finishers rewrite the Sam and Alex dialogue as a report from Sam's point of view.

L1 watch-outs
  • "She said me…" / "She told to me…": Spanish me dijo, Portuguese me disse, French elle m'a dit, Italian mi ha detto, Arabic qāl-li all put the person right after "say." Point at the key card every time: "tell needs a person; say doesn't."
  • No backshift: "He said he is tired." Russian, Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Vietnamese, Tagalog and Thai keep the original tense in reported speech (Russian он сказал, что устал literally "he said that he is tired"). It is understood. Recast and use the rewind gesture (a hand turning backward).
  • He / she mix-ups in the pronoun change: Tagalog (siya), Chinese (spoken tā), Turkish (o), Persian, Finnish and Hungarian use one word for "he" and "she." "My sister said he was tired" is very common. Point at the person on the news card.
  • Missing was / were: "She said she tired." Arabic, Russian, Chinese, Vietnamese and Haitian Creole speakers may drop be, which also removes the backshift. Color the be verb on the board.
  • Over-changing time words: learners who memorized the list change "today" even when it is still today. Keep asking: "Is it still tomorrow?"
Homework

Online lesson 3.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_07.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 3.1: finish "Things people said to me" (the 3 rows from class + 3 more on the back of the page, 6 in all: at least 2 with said, 2 with told + person, 1 would, 1 could), "My new words" and the journal (one real line as direct speech, then reported, with the changes listed).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B He said he had finished his homework. · Q2 C She told me that she was late. · Q3 (new) B Tom said he would call me the next day. Lina's voicemail (Ex 1): 1 was starting · 2 had found · 3 were · 4 could · 5 would finish · 6 had seen · 7 had to.

3.2 · C8

Reported Questions, Requests & Commands

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • report wh- questions with asked (me) / wanted to know + the wh- word + statement word order: She asked where I lived.
  • report yes/no questions with if or whether: He asked if I was ready.
  • remove do / does / did and the question mark, and apply the backshift from 3.1
  • report commands and requests with told / asked + person + (not) to + base verb: The doctor told me not to eat before the test.
  • choose tell (an order) or ask (a polite request), and use remind, warn, advise with the same pattern
Materials
  • Workbook 3.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Survey Report frame)
  • Slides 9–13 · Reporting-verb cards (ask, want to know, tell, remind, warn, advise) · Survey cards (1 per learner) · Message Relay cards (8, taped on the walls) · "While you were out" notes (2 per pair) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.2-A (at the clinic front desk) and 3.2-B (a voicemail from the building manager)

Left: "Where do you live?" → She asked where I lived. Cross out do and ? in red. Under it: 1️⃣ keep the wh- word · 2️⃣ subject + verb · 3️⃣ backshift · 4️⃣ no ?

Center: "Are you ready?" → He asked if / whether I was ready. · "Do you speak French?" → She asked if I spoke French.

Right: "Sit down!" → She told me to sit down. · "Please help me." → He asked me to help him. · "Don't worry." → She told me not to worry. Under it: remind / warn / advise + person + (not) to…

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: what did they say?8S–S → T–SReview C7. Pairs report 2 things from their homework ("My brother told me he had a new job."). Then a fast "rewind" round with the backshift cards (T: "can" → S: "could"). Two volunteers retell the funniest Gossip Chain news from C7.
Lead-in: so many questions!6T–S"Last week I went to a new clinic. The receptionist asked me so many questions!" Write two of them in bubbles: "Do you have an appointment?" "What time is your appointment?" Ask: "What did she ask me?" Let learners try; write the correct reports next to the bubbles. "What's missing?" (do, the question mark.)
Grammar: reported questions14T–SSlides 9–10. Wh- questions: the two-step recipe (reshape, then backshift). Yes/no questions: if / whether. Cross out do / did and "?" on the board with a red pen every time. CCQs and drill (below). Then Script 3.2-A (clinic): learners complete Workbook Ex 3.
Drill6T–S → SQuestion-to-report drill (see Drill script). Keep a fast pace; the goal is to feel statement word order after the wh- word.
Controlled practice 18S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1 (report the questions) and Ex 2 (fix the mistake) alone, then pairs check. Board-check Ex 2: learners come up and cross out the extra did or "?".
Freer 1: Survey Report16S–S (mingle) → S–S (groups)Slide 12. (1) Each learner gets a survey card (5 questions on a light topic) (1 min). (2) Mingle: ask 3 people your questions, write short answers (7 min). (3) Sit in groups of 3–4 with new people. Each learner reports both sides: "I asked Omar what he usually ate for breakfast. He said he ate eggs." · "Lina asked me whether I liked spicy food. I told her I loved it." (6 min). (4) Two learners report one surprising answer to the class (2 min). ICQs first.
Grammar: requests & commands10T–SSlide 11. "Sit down!" (point at a chair, firm voice) → "I told you to sit down." · "Could you open the window, please?" (polite) → "I asked Ana to open the window." Negative: not to. Then remind / warn / advise. CCQs. Then Script 3.2-B (voicemail from the building manager) as a live demo of a message: learners tell you what to do ("He asked us to be at home between nine and twelve.").
Freer 2: Message Relay12S–S (pairs)Slide 13. The 8 message cards are on the walls. In pairs, A = runner, B = writer. A walks to a card, reads one line, walks back and reports it: "Your boss called. She asked you to come in at eight tomorrow." B writes it on the "While you were out" note as a to-do. After 2 cards, swap roles. Finally pairs check their notes against the cards. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SBoard: 4 heard sentences, e.g. "She asked me where did I live." · "He asked was I ready." · "The doctor told me don't eat." · "She said me to wait." Learners fix them. Praise one clear report from each part of the room.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 3.2; workbook "My interview report" and the journal.
Total908 + 6 + 14 + 6 + 8 + 16 + 10 + 12 + 5 + 5
If you run long

This is the busiest class of the module (three shapes in one lesson). Do only 2 Message Relay cards per learner, and move Workbook Ex 4–5 to homework. Never cut the Survey Report: it is where the word-order rule becomes real. If you have a very strong class, you can shorten the drill instead.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: a reported question is not a question any more. It is part of a statement, so it has statement word order and a period.

"She asked where I lived." Is this a question? (No, a sentence about a question.) Do we need a question mark? (No.) Do we need did? (No.) What were her exact words? ("Where do you live?")
"He asked if I was ready." What was the question? ("Are you ready?") Was there a wh- word? (No, it was a yes/no question.) So what joins the report? (if or whether.) Is if here about a condition? (No, it means "whether.")

Meaning: commands and requests.

"The doctor told me to rest." Did the doctor ask a question? (No, an instruction.) Who has to rest? (Me.) · "She asked me to close the door." Was it an order or a polite request? (A polite request.) · "He told me not to worry." Should I worry? (No.)
"She reminded me to call the bank." Did I forget? (Maybe, she helped me remember.) · "He warned us not to go there." Is it dangerous? (Yes, maybe.) · "She advised me to rest." Is it an order? (No, a recommendation.)

Form.

Wh- questionasked (+ person) / wanted to know + wh- word + subject + verb (backshifted)"Where do you work?" → She asked where I worked. · "What time does it start?" → He asked what time it started.
Yes/No questionasked (+ person) + if / whether + subject + verb"Can you drive?" → He asked if I could drive. · "Did you call?" → She asked whether I had called.
Commandtold + person + to + base verb"Open your books." → The teacher told us to open our books.
Requestasked + person + to + base verb"Please wait for me." → My friend asked me to wait for her.
Negativetold / asked + person + not to + base verb"Don't be late." → Mom asked me not to be late.
More verbsremind, warn, advise, order + person + (not) to…She reminded me to call the bank. · He warned us not to go there.

Pronunciation. A reported question falls at the end, like a statement: "She asked where I lived. ↘" (no rise). Learners often rise because their ear hears "a question": model the contrast. whether sounds like weather /ˈwɛðər/: tell learners so; they are often surprised and pleased. asked is one syllable: /æskt/ ("askt"); the -sk-t cluster is hard, and many learners say "ask" or "as-ked." Drill it slowly: "ass – k – t → askt." In commands, to is weak /tə/ and not to is stressed on not: "She told me NOT to worry."

Drill script
  1. Question → report (T asks, learners report what T asked, pointing at themselves): T: "Where do you live?" → S: "You asked where I lived." T: "What are you doing?" → S: "You asked what I was doing." T: "Why did you leave?" → S: "You asked why I had left."
  2. Yes/no → if: T: "Are you hungry?" → S: "You asked if I was hungry." T: "Do you speak Spanish?" → S: "You asked whether I spoke Spanish." T: "Have you finished?" → S: "You asked if I had finished."
  3. Pronunciation: choral asked /æskt/ ×3, whether = weather ×2, then the falling report: "She asked where I lived. ↘"
  4. Command or request? T says it in two voices: firm "Sit down!" → S: "You told me to sit down." Polite "Could you sit down, please?" → S: "You asked me to sit down." T: "Don't touch it!" → S: "You told me not to touch it."
  5. Chain: S1 asks S2 a real question ("Do you like coffee?"); S2 answers; S3 reports both: "Ana asked Omar if he liked coffee. He said he did."
Survey Report — how to run it
  • Six survey topics (Food, Weekends, Phones & apps, Learning English, Music & movies, Work & dream jobs), 5 questions each. Give neighbors different topics so the reports are new to the group.
  • Every card mixes wh- and yes/no questions, so learners must choose between the wh- word and if / whether in the report.
  • The mingle is fast: ask 3 people, write 1–3 words per answer. The report is the main event. In groups, each learner reports what they asked and what the person said, and at least one question someone asked them: "Lina asked me whether I had a favorite app."
  • Anyone can answer "Pass!" or answer as their new identity card. The questions are light on purpose; learners may not add personal questions about family, age or home country.
  • Dominant speakers: in the group stage, each learner has two turns (use the talking-turn token). A learner who finishes early listens and checks: "Did she remove the do?" This gives the strong speaker a useful job.
Message Relay — how to run it
  • Before class, tape the 8 message cards on the walls, far from the desks: the boss, the doctor's office, the building manager, the child's school, the pharmacist, the neighbor, the car mechanic, the dentist's office. Each card has 3–4 lines of direct speech: a command, a negative command, a polite request and a question.
  • Pairs: A = runner, B = writer. The runner may not carry the card or a pen. They read one line, walk back and report it: "The pharmacist told you to take one pill twice a day. She asked if you had any allergies." The writer writes a short to-do on the "While you were out" note ("take 1 pill ×2 a day").
  • After 2 cards, swap roles. At the end, pairs walk to their cards and check their notes. A note that is correct in meaning is a success, even if the report had small errors.
  • The messages are everyday and routine on purpose (a repair visit, a lab test, school pick-up time). Keep it that way; don't add eviction, debt or bad medical news.
ICQs before the Survey and the Relay
Survey: How many people do you ask? (Three.) · Do you write long answers? (No, 1–3 words.) · In your group, what do you report? (What I asked, what they said, and one question someone asked me.) · "Where do you live?" is OK on this card? (No! Only the card questions.)
Relay: Can the runner take the card? (No.) · How many lines at a time? (One.) · What does the writer write? (A short to-do.) · What words start the report? ("Your boss told you to… / asked if…") · When do you swap? (After 2 cards.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with two frames only: "She asked me if I…" and "He told me to…". Give a survey card with only 3 yes/no questions and picture cues. In the Relay, pair the learner as runner with a partner who writes, and let them use the picture on each message card (doctor, teacher, mechanic) as a memory hook. Target: 3 reported yes/no questions and 3 reported instructions.

Stretch

Use wanted to know, wondered, whether or not, and the full set of verbs (remind, warn, advise, order, beg, invite). In the Relay, combine lines into one smooth report ("She reminded you to bring your card and asked whether you could come ten minutes early."). Early finishers write a 6-line job-interview dialogue and report it.

L1 watch-outs
  • Question word order in the report: "She asked where did I live?" / "I asked him why was he angry." Almost everyone does this, because the ear expects a question. Spanish, Portuguese, Russian, Arabic and Chinese learners may also keep the question intonation. Use the red pen on the board: cross out do / did and "?" every time.
  • "He asked was I ready" (no if): Russian (li is placed after the verb), Chinese and Vietnamese (a question particle at the end) have no joining word like if. Drill "asked if…" as one chunk.
  • "She told me that I sit down" / "She said me to go": Spanish and Portuguese use decir que + subjunctive (me dijo que me sentara), Italian mi ha detto di, French elle m'a dit de. Show the English chunk: told me to + verb.
  • "The doctor told me don't eat": Chinese, Vietnamese and Tagalog speakers may keep the direct command. Also "told me to not worry." Teach not to as one piece: "told me not to eat."
  • "asked to me" / "asked for me to help" (Spanish le pidió a, Arabic ṭalab min "asked from"). ask takes the person directly: "asked me to…"
  • /æskt/: Spanish, Arabic, Vietnamese, Japanese and Korean speakers find the -skt cluster hard and may say "ask," "as-ked" or "ass-uh-ku-to." Accept "ask" in fast speech early on, but model "askt."
Homework

Online lesson 3.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_08.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 3.2: finish "My interview report" (a short everyday conversation, 6 reports including one if / whether and one not to; the online project's "police interview" option is not needed), "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B She asked where I lived. · Q2 A He told me not to touch the paint. · Q3 (new) A He asked if I had a car. At the clinic (Ex 3): 1 if / whether he had · 2 his appointment was · 3 he had been · 4 to spell · 5 whether / if he had · 6 to fill out · 7 not to forget.

3.3 · C9

Relative Clauses — Joining Ideas

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • choose the right relative pronoun: who (people), which (things), that (people or things, defining only), where (places), when (times), whose (possession)
  • build defining clauses that say which one, with no commas: The woman who called you is my aunt.
  • build non-defining clauses that add an extra fact, with two commas, no that: My brother, who lives in Chicago, is a nurse.
  • drop the pronoun only when it is the object of a defining clause: The movie (that) I watched was great.
  • define people, places and things in a game, join two short sentences into one, and describe six people, places and things in their life
Materials
  • Workbook 3.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "People, places & things in my life")
  • Slides 14–17 · Relative-pronoun cards · Picture flashcards (people, places, things) · "Who am I? / What is it?" cards (1 set per group) · bingo grid (1 per learner) · sentence-combining strips + comma cards (1 envelope per pair) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.3-A (definitions bingo) and 3.3-B (comma dictation)

Left: the pronoun table: 👤 who · 📦 which · 👤📦 that (no commas only) · 📍 where · 🕐 when · 🔑 whose (= his / her / their).

Center: two sentences, one with a box and one with commas drawn big: DEFINING "My sister who lives in Rome is a lawyer." (I have 2+ sisters: which one?) · NON-DEFINING "My sister, who lives in Rome, is a lawyer." (I have one sister: extra fact.)

Right: ✂️ The movie (that) I watched… ✔ (object: drop OK) · The man who called me… (subject: keep it!) · ❌ My car, that is red… · ❌ The book which it changed my life.

Stage plan

Warmer: messages8S–S → T–SReview C8. Pairs read one line from their homework interview report. Then a quick "What did they ask / tell you?" round: T reads 4 lines from the Relay cards; learners report them ("The mechanic told you not to drive fast."). Two learners read their journal (only if they want to).
Lead-in: one sentence, not two5T–SBoard: "I have a friend. She lives in Canada." "Is this good English?" (Yes, but choppy.) "Make it one sentence." Elicit "I have a friend who lives in Canada." "Do you remember who and which from A2?" Let the class list the pronouns they know.
Relative pronouns10T–SSlide 14. The six pronouns with a picture example each. New for most: when and whose (= his / her / their). CCQs. Then Workbook Ex 1 (choose the pronoun) together, fast.
Drill + bingo5T–SPronoun drill (see Drill script), then read Script 3.3-A: learners mark the picture on their bingo grid for each definition. First "Bingo!" reads back their three definitions.
Freer 1: Who am I? / What is it?14S–S (groups)Slide 15. Groups of 3–4, cards face down. A player takes a card and defines it without saying the word: "It's a person who works in a hospital and who helps doctors." · "It's a place where you send letters." · "It's a thing which keeps food cold." The first to guess keeps the card. Stretch: the "Don't say" words on the card. ICQs first. Then Workbook Ex 2.
Defining vs non-defining12T–SSlide 16. "Which one?" vs "extra fact." The sister example (one sister or two?), the "remove it" test, no that after a comma, and the pause at the commas when you speak. CCQs. Then slide 17: dropping the object pronoun. Then Script 3.3-B: a short dictation in which learners must hear the pauses and write the commas.

Freer 2: my people, places & things14S → S–S (groups)(1) Each learner chooses 6: two people, two places, two things or times (real, a new identity card, or imagined) and writes key words only (3 min). (2) In groups of 3, each learner talks for 1–2 minutes: "My best friend is someone who… · The town where I grew up… · My phone, which is very old, …" Listeners ask one follow-up question each (9 min). (3) Each group shares the best sentence with a comma pause (2 min).
Feedback + module check5T–SDelayed correction: "The man called me was angry." · "London, that is huge, …" · "The book which it changed my life." · "I have a neighbor who's dog barks." Then learners tick the three Module 3 "I can…" lists.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 3.3; the "people, places & things" writing; preview C10: "Next class: The bridge was built in 1890. Who built it? We don't care! The passive."
Total908 + 5 + 10 + 5 + 14 + 12 + 12 + 14 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: a relative clause describes the noun just before it. It joins two ideas into one sentence.

"That's the woman whose car was stolen." Whose car was stolen? (The woman's. Her car.) · "This is the café where we met." Is where a question here? (No, it means "in this café.") · "I remember the day when we moved." Is when about a time or a place? (A time.)

Meaning: defining vs non-defining.

"My sister who lives in Rome is a lawyer." (no commas) How many sisters do I have? (Two or more.) Why do I say "who lives in Rome"? (To say which sister.) · "My sister, who lives in Rome, is a lawyer." (commas) How many sisters? (One.) Is "who lives in Rome" needed? (No, it's extra.)
"Paris, which is the capital of France, is beautiful." Do we know which Paris? (Yes.) So: commas or no commas? (Commas: it's extra.) · Can I say "Paris, that is the capital…"? (No! No that after a comma.)
Dropping: "The movie I watched was great." Is a word missing? (Yes, that / which.) Is it OK? (Yes, I is the new subject.) · "The man called me was angry." OK? (No! who is the subject: "The man who called me.")

Form.

Pronounswho people · which things · that people / things (defining) · where places · when times · whose possessionThat's the man whose dog barks all night.
Definingnoun + pronoun + clause · no commas · that OKThe keys that open the garage are lost.
Non-definingnoun, + who / which + clause, · two commas · no that · never drop the pronounOur new house, which we bought last year, has a garden.
Drop the pronoundefining + object only (a new subject follows)The people (who) we met were lovely. · ❌ The woman lives here…
where = which … inwhere + subject + verbThe house where I live = the house which I live in.

Pronunciation. In speech, a non-defining clause is a little "aside": a short pause at each comma and a slightly lower, faster voice inside: "My brother | who lives in Chicago | is a nurse." A defining clause has no pause: "The woman who called you is my aunt." Use your hands: push the extra fact to the side. whose and who's sound the same /huz/; only writing shows the difference. that in a defining clause is weak /ðət/. which and witch sound the same; the wh is /w/ in American English.

Drill script
  1. Which pronoun? (hold up a flashcard) T: nurse → S: "a person who…" · fridge → "a thing which…" · post office → "a place where…" · summer → "a time when…"
  2. Join it: T: "I have a friend. She speaks five languages." → S: "I have a friend who speaks five languages." T: "This is the book. It changed my life." → S: "This is the book that changed my life."
  3. Pause or no pause? T reads; learners show ✋ (pause, commas) or 👉 (no pause, no commas): "My mother, who is a nurse, works nights." ✋ · "The bus that goes to town leaves at seven." 👉 · "Venice, which is full of canals, is sinking." ✋
  4. Drop it? T: "The movie that I watched…" → S: "The movie I watched." ✂️ T: "The man who called me…" → S: "Keep it!"
  5. whose substitution: T: "I know a boy. His father is a pilot." → S: "I know a boy whose father is a pilot." T: "That's the woman. Her car was stolen." → S: "That's the woman whose car was stolen."
"Who am I? / What is it?" — how to run it
  • Groups of 3–4, a set of 24 cards face down: 8 people (jobs), 8 places, 8 things. Each card has a picture, the word and two "Don't say" words.
  • A player takes a card and defines it without saying the word, using a relative clause: "It's a person who… / It's a place where… / It's a thing which (that)… / You use it when…". The first person to guess correctly keeps the card. Most cards wins.
  • Level 1: the "Don't say" words are allowed. Level 2 (stretch): the player may not say the "Don't say" words either ("chef: don't say cook or restaurant").
  • The guessers must answer with a full sentence: "Is it a nurse? A nurse is a person who helps doctors!"
  • Dominant speakers: the guesser who wins a card must wait one turn before guessing again. This gives quieter learners time to think.
ICQs before the game and the sentence strips
Game: Can you say the word on the card? (No!) · What words start your definition? ("It's a person who… / a place where… / a thing which…") · Who keeps the card? (The first person who guesses.)
Strips: How many sentences do you make from two strips? (One.) · When do you use the comma cards? (When the information is extra.) · Can you put that after a comma? (No.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on defining clauses with who, which and where only. In the game, use the picture side of the card and three frames on a strip: "a person who… / a thing which… / a place where…". Give only the first 5 sentence strips (all defining). Target: 4 spoken definitions and 3 joined sentences. The commas can wait.

Stretch

Use whose and when in the game. In the strips, find the two sentences that can be both defining and non-defining and explain the difference in meaning. In "my people, places & things," use two non-defining clauses and one which is why…. Early finishers write 3 sentences that change meaning with commas.

L1 watch-outs
  • The extra pronoun: "The book which I read it" / "The man who I saw him." Arabic, Hebrew, Persian, Turkish (in some forms), Irish and many Chinese speakers use a "resumptive" pronoun. The lesson's "double subject" warning ("The book which it changed my life") is the same error. Cross out the extra pronoun.
  • Clause in the wrong place or avoided: Chinese, Japanese and Korean put the describing clause before the noun (like "the lives-in-Canada friend"), so learners may avoid relative clauses and use two sentences. Praise every attempt; accuracy comes later.
  • who / which mix-ups: "the man which…" or "the car who…": Spanish que, French qui / que, Portuguese que and Tagalog na / -ng use one word for people and things. The board symbols (👤 / 📦) help.
  • "My car, that is red, …": Spanish, Portuguese and Italian allow que / che after a comma. Teach: comma → who / which.
  • Commas everywhere: German (and Russian, Polish, Czech) put a comma before every relative clause, so learners write "The man, who lives next door, is a doctor" for a defining clause. Show that the commas change the meaning in English.
  • whose / who's: same sound for everyone. Spelling only: who's = who is.
Homework

Online lesson 3.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_09.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back, pausing at the commas; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 3.3: finish "People, places & things in my life" (6 sentences, at least one whose and one non-defining clause with commas), "My new words" and the journal (one sentence that changes meaning with commas, and why).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B whose · Q2 B My father, who is retired, plays golf. · Q3 (new) B "The man called me was angry." is NOT correct. Bingo (Script 3.3-A): nurse, chef, mechanic, waiter, airport, post office, bank, station, fridge, umbrella, passport, key.

Closing the Module

End of C9: Module 3 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, the "three shapes and one join": She said (that) she was… (statements) → She asked where / if I… (questions) → She told me (not) to… (commands) → the woman who… / my sister, who…, (connecting ideas). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C10 warmer and again in the C13 progress-test catch-up.

Watch for in Module 4: the passive (was built, is made) uses be + past participle, and relative clauses combine with it naturally ("the bridge which was built in 1890"). Learners who just learned the backshift may mix was + participle with the past perfect ("He said it had built…"). Keep a 2-minute "report it" warmer (one statement, one question, one command) in C10–C12.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: the backshift (including will → would, can → could, must → had to), pronoun and time-word changes, say vs tell + person, reported wh- and yes/no questions (word order, if / whether), told / asked + person + (not) to, the relative pronouns, defining vs non-defining clauses with commas, and when the pronoun can be dropped.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 4 — Structures for Fluency

Three classes that finish the first half of B1. First comes the passive (present and past simple) for facts, products and processes: Coffee is grown in Brazil. The bridge was built in 1932. Then modals of deduction (must / might / can't be) to reason from clues, set against the obligation and advice modals learners met at A2. Last, a task-based review: learners hold a mini debate and tell a short story with the grammar of Modules 1–4, while you score a speaking checklist that feeds Progress Test 1 (C13).

3 lessons Lessons 4.1–4.3 Classes C10–C12 Weeks 5–6 90 min per class CEFR B1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. C10 and C11 follow the usual shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework. C12 is a task-based review: the task comes first and language work comes out of it.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-4-fluency-structures.html. Print materials/module-4/classroom-materials.html before C10. It holds the flashcards, the How It's Made quiz, the process strips, the fact-file information gap, the mystery close-ups, the detective cards, the "Whose bag?" case, the rules-or-advice cards, the mini-debate cards, the story cards, the C12 speaking checklist, the Module 1–4 Review Race board game and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. Learners met past participles with the present perfect (B1 Module 1, and A2), and have to / must / mustn't / should at A2. Build on this. The new B1 work is the passive voice, must / can't / might for deduction, and the choice between obligation, no obligation, prohibition and advice.
  • Keep B1 honest. Learners produce the present and past simple passive and present deductions only. They may meet other forms (is being repaired, has been sent, must have left) outside class: teach these for recognition ("You'll understand this; you don't need to make it yet"). Past perfect is Lesson 5.1: accept it in C12 stories, never require it.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use some British forms (city centre, parcel, postman, carriage, guard, half past nine, practise, travelling). Teach downtown, package, mail carrier, (train) car, conductor, nine-thirty, practice, traveling. Accept British forms from learners. Note: Americans rarely say mustn't. For rules they say can't, aren't allowed to or (in writing) must not. Teach mustn't so learners understand it, and give the American forms next to it.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Move the spotlight → measure how sure you are → say what you think. In C10 learners move the focus of a sentence from the doer to the thing (This watch is made in Switzerland.) and describe products and processes. In C11 they reason from clues and show how certain they are (The lights are off, so they must be out.), and keep that apart from rules and advice (You have to… / You should…). In C12 they use everything from Modules 1–4 in two real tasks: argue a point in a small group and tell a short story. Every class ends with a speaking task that is longer and freer than the one before.

Trauma-informed: facts, guesses and opinions

C10: "facts about your country" is an option, never a requirement. Learners can describe any product, any place, or a process like making bread. Don't ask one learner to "represent" their country. C11: deductions are about pictures, objects and card people, never about people in the room ("She must be pregnant," "He must be poor"). Say this rule before every detective task. C12: debate topics are everyday (city life, phones, cooking, transportation), never politics, religion or immigration. Every learner argues from an opinion card, so no one has to share a real opinion. Stories can be true, changed, or completely invented (story cards). A light story is a good story. Say "Real or invented, both are great" every time.

Two B1 habits for every class in this module

Fluency before accuracy. In freer tasks, don't stop speakers to correct. Listen, write down 4–6 sentences you hear (good ones and ones with errors), and give feedback in two steps: first content ("Which group had the most surprising fact? Who changed their mind?"), then language on the board with no names, and the class fixes it together. Correct on the spot only when a mistake blocks meaning.

Dominant and quiet speakers. B1 groups often have one confident speaker who fills the silence. Use structure, not criticism: talking tokens (3 each; you put one down every time you speak; when your tokens are gone, you listen), a chair role that rotates and invites others ("What do you think, Ana?"), think time (30 seconds silent before anyone speaks) and a fixed speaking order for the first round. Give the strongest speaker a job that needs listening: chair, timekeeper, or "question master." Quiet learners rehearse in pairs before they speak to a group.

Before the Module

  • Print the picture flashcards (Set A: products, clue objects, debate topics). Card stock helps.
  • For C10: one How It's Made quiz set per class (Set B), one set of process strips per pair (Set C, cut and mixed), one fact-file A/B page per pair (Set D). Bring 3–4 real things with labels: a coffee packet, a T-shirt ("Made in…"), a can or a bottle ("Recycle me"), a phone box.
  • For C11: one mystery close-up set per group (Set E), one detective card set per group of 3–4 (Set F), one "Whose bag?" case per group (Set G), one rules-or-advice set per pair (Set H). For the lead-in, a real bag with 5–6 objects in it (see the C11 plan).
  • For C12: mini-debate topic cards, opinion cards, chair cards and talking tokens (Set I; coins or paper clips work as tokens), story cards (Set J), one speaking checklist class grid for you and one score slip per learner (Set K), and one Review Race board per group of 3–4 with a die and counters (Set L).
  • Optional: learners' phones for recording stories in C12. Ask the class in C11 who is happy to use a phone.
