Lesson Plans

4.1 · C10

The Passive Voice — How It's Made

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • form the present simple passive (am / is / are + past participle) and the past simple passive (was / were + past participle), with negatives and questions
  • explain when the passive is the better choice: the doer is unknown, unimportant, obvious or "people in general," or the focus is a process or result
  • add by + agent only when the doer is interesting or important (never "by someone / by people")
  • change active sentences into passive with the 3-step method, and use made of / made from / made in / made by
  • describe a product, a place or a process in 6+ passive sentences
Materials
  • Workbook 4.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My 6 facts" plan)
  • Slides 3–7 · Product flashcards (Set A) · 3–4 real labeled objects · How It's Made quiz (Set B) · Process strips (Set C, 1 set per pair) · Fact-file information gap A/B (Set D, 1 page per pair)
  • Listening scripts 4.1-A (How coffee is made) and 4.1-B (News in brief)

Left: ACTIVE: Farmers grow coffee in Brazil. (the doer is the star) → PASSIVE: Coffee is grown in Brazil. (the thing is the star). Under it: be + past participle.

Center: two columns. PRESENT: It is made · They are grown · It isn't recycled · Is it made with milk? | PAST: It was built · They were sent · It wasn't delivered · When was it built?

Right: Why passive? ❓ unknown · 🤷 not important · 👮 obvious · 🌍 people in general · ⚙️ a process. Below: by + agent only if interesting: Hamlet was written by Shakespeare. ✅ · My bike was stolen by someone.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: define it8S–S (pairs)Review C9 (3.3, relative clauses). A picks a flashcard and defines it without saying the word: "It's a thing which you use when it rains." "It's a person who works in a restaurant." B guesses. Swap after 4 cards. Listen for which / who / where. Keep two definitions for the lead-in: "It's a thing which is used for…" is a passive!
Lead-in: where was it made?6T–S → S–SHold up your real objects. "Look at the label. What does it say?" Elicit Made in… Learners check a label near them (a pen, a bag, a phone) and tell a partner. Write two on the board: "This T-shirt was made in Bangladesh." "This coffee is grown in Colombia." Ask: "Who made it? Do we know? Do we care?" (No.) That's the passive.
Grammar: be + past participle15T–SSlides 3–4. Meaning (focus on the thing, not the doer), form (only be changes for tense and person; the participle is the same one learners know from the present perfect), negatives, questions. CCQs and drill (see notes). Quick participle check: T says a verb, learners say the participle (grow → grown, build → built, steal → stolen, write → written, sell → sold).
Listening: How coffee is made8T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 4.1-A twice. First listen: "Where is coffee grown?" Second listen: learners number the six steps. Pairs check by saying the steps: "First, the coffee cherries are picked…" Point out: we never hear who does it.
Why passive? + the agent7T–SSlide 5. The five reasons with an example each. Read Script 4.1-B (News in brief): learners write the passive verbs they hear, then say why the doer is missing in each. Then by + agent: "The Mona Lisa was painted by Leonardo da Vinci. Is by Leonardo useful? (Yes! It's famous.) My phone was stolen by someone: useful? (No.)"
Controlled practice12S → S–SSlide 6 (active → passive in 3 steps), then Workbook Ex 2, 3 and 4 alone. Pairs check. Board-check only items pairs disagree on (usually the tense of be in Ex 2 and when to keep by in Ex 3). Early finishers do the process strips (chocolate) and say the steps.
Freer 1: How It's Made quiz14S–S (teams) → T–SSlide 7. Teams of 3–4. You read a quiz card (Set B) with three options. Teams talk (30 seconds), then one speaker gives the answer as a full passive sentence: "Paper was invented in China." 1 point for the right fact, +1 for a correct passive sentence. Rotate the speaker every question. 12–14 questions. ICQs first.
Freer 2: fact-file information gap10S–SSet D. A has the full file on the Riverside Bridge; B has the full file on Sunrise Chocolate. Each asks passive questions to fill the gaps in the other file: "When was the bridge built?" "Where are the beans grown?" Don't show the cards. Pairs who finish choose one file and present it to another pair in 5 sentences.
Feedback5T–SContent first: "Which quiz answer surprised you?" Then 4 sentences you heard (no names), e.g. "Coffee grown in Brazil." · "The bridge is built in 1932." · "Paper was invent in China." · "An accident was happened." Learners fix them.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 4.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below): the "My 6 facts" plan is the online project. Learners tick the "I can…" box.
Total908 + 6 + 15 + 8 + 7 + 12 + 14 + 10 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Cut the quiz to 8 questions and move the fact-file information gap to the C11 warmer (it recycles the passive before deduction). Never cut the be check in the grammar stage: a missing be ("English spoken here") is the top error, and it is easy to fix today and hard to fix later.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the passive moves the spotlight. The thing that receives the action becomes the subject. The doer is not important, not known, obvious, or "people in general." It is not a new tense; it is a new voice.

"This watch is made in Switzerland." Do we know who makes it? (No.) Is that important? (No.) What is the sentence about: the workers or the watch? (The watch.) · "My car was stolen last night." Who stole it? (We don't know.) Present or past? (Past: was.)
"He was arrested on Friday." Who arrested him? (The police: it's obvious.) · "Rice is eaten all over Asia." Who eats it? (People: everybody.) Do we need to say "by people"? (No.)
"Hamlet was written by Shakespeare." Why do we say by Shakespeare here? (It's famous, interesting information.) · "The office is cleaned every evening." Can I say by them? (You can, but it adds nothing. Leave it out.)

Form. Color the be verb in one color and the past participle in another. be shows the tense and agrees with the subject; the participle never changes.

Presentsubject + am / is / are + past participleEnglish is spoken here. · These cars are made in Japan.
Pastsubject + was / were + past participleThe bridge was built in 1890. · The letters were sent yesterday.
Negativeisn't / aren't / wasn't / weren't + participlePlastic isn't recycled here. · The package wasn't delivered.
Question(Wh-) + is / are / was / were + subject + participle?Is this dish made with milk? · When was it built? · Where are they grown?
Agent… + by + doer (only if useful)The Mona Lisa was painted by Leonardo da Vinci.
made…made of (material you can see) · made from (changed) · made in (place) · made by (maker)made of wood · made from milk · made in Italy · made by hand

Pronunciation. In speech, be is weak and linked: "It's MADE in JApan" (/ɪts/), "They're GROWN…", "It was BUILT" /wəz/. The stress falls on the participle and the new information, not on was. -ed endings matter here: invented, painted, designed /ɪd/ adds a beat only after /t/ or /d/; used, opened, delivered end in /d/; picked, packed, cooked in /t/. used in "It is used for…" is /juzd/ (z), not /just/ as in "used to." ground (from grind) rhymes with found.

Drill script
  1. Back-chain: "in Brazil" → "is grown in Brazil" → "Coffee is grown in Brazil." Choral ×3, half the room, then 4–5 individuals. Then "was built in 1890" → "The bridge was built in 1890."
  2. Tense switch (hold up a card: NOW or a year): T: "coffee / grow / Brazil" + NOW → S: "Coffee is grown in Brazil." T: "the bridge / build" + 1890 → S: "The bridge was built in 1890." T: "these shoes / make / Italy" + NOW → S: "These shoes are made in Italy."
  3. Subject switch (is / are): T: "English / speak" → "English is spoken…" T: "Spanish and Portuguese / speak" → "…are spoken." Point at the subject every time.
  4. Question chain: S1: "When was the Eiffel Tower built?" S2: "It was built in 1889. Where are bananas grown?" S3: "They're grown in… (Ecuador / India / the Philippines)."
  5. Cut the agent: T says an active sentence; learners say the passive and decide on by: "Someone stole my phone." → "My phone was stolen." "Shakespeare wrote Hamlet." → "Hamlet was written by Shakespeare."
How It's Made quiz — how to run it
  • Teams of 3–4 with a team name. One sheet of paper per team for the score. Read each card and its three options twice.
  • 30 seconds to talk (in English!). Then the team's speaker for that round says the answer as a full passive sentence. The speaker changes every round, so everyone speaks. This is also your fix for a dominant speaker.
  • Scoring: 1 point for the right fact, +1 for a correct passive sentence (right be + participle). A wrong fact in a perfect sentence still gets 1 point. Say this: good English matters as much as knowing the fact.
  • After the answer, give one extra fact from the card's note (in the passive) and let a learner repeat it.
  • Last round: each team writes one question for the other teams about their own town, a product they know, or any country ("Where is ___ grown?", "When was ___ built?"). Nobody has to ask about their home country.
ICQs before the quiz and the information gap
Quiz: Who talks first: the team or the speaker? (The team, 30 seconds.) · How do you give the answer? (A full passive sentence.) · How many points for a correct sentence? (One more.) · Is the speaker always the same person? (No, it changes.)
Fact files: Can you show your card? (No.) · What kind of questions do you ask? (Passive questions: "When was it built?") · When you finish, what do you do? (Present one file to another pair.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with four fixed chunks: It is made in… · It is grown in… · It was built in… · It was made by… Use the product flashcards and labels as prompts. In the quiz, the learner can answer by pointing to the option and then saying the chunk with a partner. In the fact files, give a question strip with the questions already written; the learner reads a question and writes only the answer (a year, a place, a number). Target: 4 spoken passive sentences about real objects.

Stretch

Use sequence words in a process (First… Then… After that… Finally…) and a relative clause (The beans are roasted, which gives them their color.). Recognize other tenses of the passive when you meet them (is being repaired, has been sent) and say what they mean. Rewrite a short news article from a free news site for learners in the active, then back in the passive, and decide which version sounds better.

L1 watch-outs
  • No be: "English spoken here" / "Coffee grown in Brazil": Chinese, Vietnamese, Arabic, Russian and Haitian Creole speakers (no be in many present sentences). Point at the be color every time: "No be, no passive."
  • "Here speaks English" / "It sells in the market": Spanish se habla, Portuguese fala-se, Italian si parla, French on parle build the passive idea with se / on. Show the English pattern: "English is spoken here." "It is sold at the market."
  • "An accident was happened" / "He was died": common for Arabic, Chinese, Japanese, Korean and Turkish speakers, and for anyone who has just learned the passive. happen, die, arrive, go, sleep have no object, so no passive.
  • Passive only for bad news: Chinese 被 bèi and the Japanese / Korean "suffering" passive are mostly used for unpleasant events. Learners may avoid neutral passives ("Rice is eaten…"). Give many neutral examples.
  • "The house becomes built" / "The bridge will built": German and Dutch werden / worden (= become) make the passive. Drill "is / was + participle."
  • Base verb or regular -ed instead of the participle: "are make," "was builded," "was stealed." Everyone. Keep the irregular list from Module 1 on the wall.
  • "made of" for everything (Spanish and Portuguese hecho de / feito de, French fait de). Teach the four: made of wood · made from milk · made in Italy · made by hand.
Homework

Online lesson 4.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_10.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 4.1: finish Ex 5 ("My 6 facts": a product, a place or a process, with one by + agent), "My new words" and the journal (one active sentence about your life, rewritten in the passive, and which version you like better and why).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B were sent · Q2 A The school was built in 1985. · Q3 (new) B My bag was stolen. Coffee (Ex 1, Script 4.1-A), in order: picked → skins removed → dried in the sun → roasted → ground → packed and sold. News in brief (Script 4.1-B): was opened · was designed · were given · was stolen · was found · was hurt.

4.2 · C11

Modals of Deduction & Obligation — Detectives at Work

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use must (sure it's true) and can't (sure it's not true) + base verb to make present deductions from clues
  • use might / may / could (+ not) when they are not sure, and place a guess on the certainty scale
  • link a deduction to its clue with so: The lights are off, so they must be out.
  • use can't, not mustn't, for a negative deduction, and tell deduction-must from obligation-must
  • tell apart have to / must (necessary), don't have to (not necessary), mustn't (not allowed) and should (advice)
Materials
  • Workbook 4.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My 3 deductions")
  • Slides 8–13 · Clue-object flashcards (Set A) · Mystery close-ups (Set E) · Detective cards (Set F) · "Whose bag?" case (Set G) · Rules or advice? cards (Set H)
  • A real bag with 5–6 objects (see the lead-in) · Listening scripts 4.2-A (Where's Sam?) and 4.2-B (dictation)

Left: the certainty scale, top to bottom: ✅ must (sure YES, ~95%) → 🤔 might / may / could (maybe, ~50%) → ❌ can't (sure NO, ~95%). Beside it: CLUE, so + DEDUCTION: The ground is wet, so it must be raining.

Center: modal + base verb: She must be tired. · He can't be hungry. · They might know. (no to, no -s). A box: can't ✅ / mustn't ❌ for "sure it's not true."

Right: RULES & ADVICE: have to / must = necessary · don't have to = not necessary (your choice) · mustn't (US: can't / not allowed) = forbidden · should = a good idea. Below: must has 2 jobs: 📜 "You must sign here." (rule) · 🕵️ "You must be tired." (deduction)

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: What is it?8S–S (pairs) → T–SReview C10. A thinks of an object; B asks only passive yes/no questions: "Is it made of glass?" "Is it used in the kitchen?" "Was it invented a long time ago?" A answers "Yes, it is. / No, it wasn't." Swap. Two volunteers read a "My 6 facts" sentence from homework.
Lead-in: the mystery bag7T–SPut your bag on the desk. "I found this bag on the bus. Whose is it?" Take out one object at a time (e.g. a bus ticket, reading glasses, a baby's sock, a library book, keys with a soccer key ring). After each one, elicit guesses. Recast them: "Maybe she has a baby." → "She might have a baby." "It's not a child's bag." → "It can't be a child's bag." "She has a baby, sure!" → "She must have a baby." No grammar talk yet.
Grammar: must / might / can't15T–SSlide 9. The certainty scale, the form (modal + base verb), and so between clue and deduction. CCQs and drill (see notes). Highlight the trap: the opposite of deduction-must is can't, not mustn't.
Mystery close-ups10T–S → S–SSlide 8, then Set E. Show a very close-up picture: "What is it?" Learners guess with all three levels: "It might be a lamp. It can't be food, it's metal. It must be a key!" Reveal. Then groups of 3 do Set E: one learner holds the close-up; the others deduce; the holder answers "Warmer! / Colder!" and reveals.
Listening: Where's Sam?7T–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 4.2-A twice. Learners write the clue and the deduction for each idea (stuck in traffic → can't; still asleep / sick → might, may; on his way → must). Check: "What's the clue? So…?"
Obligation vs advice10T–S → S–SSlides 10–11. "One word, two jobs": You must sign here vs You must be tired. Then the rules family: have to / must vs don't have to vs mustn't vs should. CCQs. Pairs sort the Rules or advice? cards (Set H) into four columns and say each one: "At the pool, you mustn't run." Check the two traps: mustn't ≠ don't have to, and have to (a rule) vs should (my advice).
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone, then pairs check. Board-check only disagreements (usually Ex 5, obligation or deduction?).
Freer: the detective agency13S–S (groups)Slides 12–13. Read the lost-bag model dialogue (slide 12) in two halves of the room. Then groups of 3–4: (1) the "Whose bag?" case (Set G): turn over one clue at a time and move up and down the scale until the group is sure (6 min); (2) detective cards (Set F): take a card, read the situation, make 3 deductions (must / might / can't) with the clue, and one "should" advice sentence (7 min). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SContent first: "Whose bag was it? What was the best clue?" Then board, no names: "He mustn't be at home." · "She must to be tired." · "It can be the mail carrier." · "You mustn't pay, it's free." Learners fix them.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 4.2; "My 3 deductions" and the journal. Preview C12: "Next class you'll tell a short story and have a mini debate. Think of a small story: true or invented."
Total908 + 7 + 15 + 10 + 7 + 10 + 10 + 13 + 5 + 5
If you run long

This class has two jobs. If time is short, keep the deduction work whole (it is new) and shorten the obligation review to slide 11 + half the Rules or advice cards: learners met these modals at A2. In the freer stage, do the "Whose bag?" case and only 3–4 detective cards.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: deduction. A guess with a reason. The speaker looks at a clue and says how sure they are. Nobody told them the answer.

"The light's on, so she must be home." Did she tell me she's home? (No.) What's my clue? (The light.) How sure am I? (Very sure, about 95%.) Is it a rule? (No, it's a guess.)
"He can't be hungry. He just ate." Is he hungry? (No.) Am I sure? (Yes, very.) Can I say "He mustn't be hungry"? (No! mustn't means "not allowed.")
"She's not answering. She might be busy." Is she busy? (Maybe.) Can I say "may be busy" or "could be busy"? (Yes, the same.) Can I say "She can be busy" for this? (Not for one situation now; use might / could.)

Meaning: obligation and advice.

"You have to show ID at the bank." Is it my choice? (No, it's a rule.) · "You don't have to come on Saturday." Can I come? (Yes.) Do I need to? (No, it's my choice.) · "You mustn't park here." Is it OK? (No, it's against the rules.) · "You should rest." Is it a rule? (No, it's advice: a good idea.)
"You must be tired after that trip." A rule or a guess? (A guess: deduction.) · "You must sign here." A rule or a guess? (A rule.) How do you know? (The situation. be tired is a state; sign is an action someone asks you to do.)

Form. Modals never change: no -s, no to, no do. The verb after them is the base form, very often be. For "happening now," use be + -ing: It must be raining. They might be sleeping.

Sure YESsubject + must + base verbShe must be home. · It must be raining.
Maybesubject + might / may / could (+ not) + base verbHe might be busy. · It could be a mistake. · She may not know.
Sure NOsubject + can't + base verbThat can't be right. · They can't be asleep. It's noon!
Necessaryhave to / has to · mustI have to work tomorrow. · You must show ID.
Not necessarydon't / doesn't have toYou don't have to pay. It's free.
Not allowedmustn't · (US) can't / aren't allowed toYou mustn't smoke here. · You can't park here.
Adviceshould / shouldn'tYou should rest. · You shouldn't worry.

Pronunciation. The big one in American English: can't /kænt/ vs can /kən/. In a positive sentence can is weak and unstressed; can't is strong and stressed, and the final t is often not released. Learners must stress CAN'T or the listener hears the opposite. must is weak in deductions ("She /məs/ be TIRED"); stress the new information. mustn't /ˈmʌsənt/: the first t is silent. have to = /ˈhæftə/ ("hafta"), has to = /ˈhæstə/. Deductions often have a thinking, falling tone: "Hmm… it MIGHT be…" with a pause before the guess, which is natural and gives thinking time.

Drill script
  1. Scale drill (point at the scale on the board): T: "be home" + ✅ → S: "She must be home." + 🤔 → "She might be home." + ❌ → "She can't be home." Choral, individuals, then faster.
  2. Clue → deduction (T gives a clue, learners finish with so): T: "He's yawning…" → S: "…so he must be tired." T: "She doesn't have a brother…" → S: "…so that can't be her brother." T: "I don't know who's at the door…" → S: "…so it might be the mail carrier."
  3. can / can't listening: T says "You can park here. / You can't park here. / It can be cold here in winter. / It can't be cold, it's July." Learners show 👍 (can) or 👎 (can't). Then learners test each other with a stressed CAN'T.
  4. Fix the mustn't: T: "He mustn't be at home. His car's gone." → S: "He can't be at home." T: "The shop mustn't be open." → S: "The shop can't be open."
  5. Rule, not necessary, or advice? T: "Seatbelt, car." → S: "You have to wear a seatbelt." T: "Free museum, pay." → "You don't have to pay." T: "Library, phone calls." → "You mustn't / can't make phone calls." T: "Cold, sleep." → "You should go to bed early."
The detective agency — how to run it
  • The rule first: "Detectives make deductions about pictures, objects and card people. Never about people in this room." Say it before every round.
  • "Whose bag?" (Set G): the four owner profiles go face up; the clue cards go face down in order (1–5). Turn over one clue. Every learner makes one deduction about one person with the clue: "It can't be Grace's, because the ticket is to Toronto." Then the next clue. After clue 5 the group writes its answer ("It must be ___'s bag, because…"). The group with the best reasons, not the fastest, wins.
  • Detective cards (Set F): a player reads the situation aloud. Each other player adds one deduction at a different level (must / might / can't), always with the clue: "There are three newspapers at the door, so they can't be at home." The reader adds one piece of advice with should: "We should call them." Next player reads.
  • Talking tokens work well here: 3 each. When your tokens are gone, you listen until everyone has spoken.
  • Early finishers turn two detective cards into a short dialogue like the lost-bag model and read it to another group.
ICQs before the detective agency
Do we make deductions about people in this room? (No! Only pictures, objects and card people.) · In "Whose bag?", do you turn over all the clues at once? (No, one at a time.) · What word goes between the clue and the deduction? ("So.") · With the detective cards, how many deductions? (Three: must, might, can't.) · And the reader? (One piece of advice with "should.")
Differentiation
Literacy support

Use the three picture symbols ✅ 🤔 ❌ in place of the words, and three fixed frames: It must be… · It might be… · It can't be… The close-ups are ideal: no reading needed. In the detective agency, a partner reads the situation card aloud and the learner chooses a symbol and finishes the frame. Target: 4 spoken deductions, at least one of each level, and one "You should…".

Stretch

Use be + -ing for deductions about now (They must be sleeping.) and give two competing deductions for one clue (She might be at work, or she could be at the gym.). Recognize past deduction (She must have left) in the online lesson and say what it means, but keep your own deductions in the present. Write a short "mystery" (3 clues, one answer) for a classmate to solve.

L1 watch-outs
  • German "muss nicht" = don't have to. German speakers say "You mustn't come" when they mean "You don't have to come." The meaning reverses. Show the pair side by side and drill it.
  • "He mustn't be at home" for a negative deduction: everyone, but especially Spanish and Portuguese speakers (no debe de estar / não deve estar = probably isn't). Fix it every time: "sure NO → can't."
  • "It can be the mail carrier" for a possibility now: Spanish puede ser, Portuguese pode ser, French ça peut être, Italian può essere. In English, can is for general possibility ("It can be cold here in winter"); for one situation now, use might / could.
  • "She must to be tired" / "It must is broken": learners whose L1 uses a full verb for must (Spanish debe, Arabic yajib an). Chant: "modal + plain verb."
  • "He should be at home" as a deduction (Chinese 应该 yīnggāi = should / probably). It is possible English ("I expect so"), but it is not must. Accept it and offer must for "I'm sure."
  • can / can't in sound: Vietnamese, Cantonese and Spanish speakers often drop the final t, so can't sounds like can. Stress and a longer vowel save the meaning: "I CAN'T /kæːnt/."
Homework

Online lesson 4.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_11.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 4.2: finish "My 3 deductions" (look around your room: one must, one might, one can't, each with the clue), "My new words" and the journal. Think of a short story for C12 (true, changed, or invented).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A must · Q2 A You don't have to pay. · Q3 (new) B can't. Where's Sam? (Ex 1, Script 4.2-A): stuck in traffic → can't (he rides a bike) · still asleep → might (his alarm didn't ring?) · sick → may / might (he looked pale) · on his way → must (his coat isn't on his chair). "Whose bag?": it must be Mei's. Slide 8 close-ups: 1 a key · 2 an umbrella · 3 an egg · 4 a remote.

4.3 · C12

Review Project — Opinions & Storytelling

90 min
A task-based review, not a new-grammar class

There is no new grammar today. The only new language is functional: phrases to give, support, agree and disagree. The class is built around two real tasks: a mini debate in groups of four and a short story (true or invented) told to a small group. Learners plan, rehearse, then perform while you listen with the speaking checklist (Set K). Language work comes from the tasks: short focus-on-form moments when you hear a gap, not presentations. The checklist results feed C13 (Progress Test 1): they are your spoken evidence for Modules 1–4, and they tell you who goes to which catch-up station.

Objectives — learners can…
  • give an opinion with a range of openers (In my opinion… · If you ask me… · As far as I'm concerned…) and support it with a reason and an example
  • agree (That's a good point. · I couldn't agree more. · So do I. / Neither do I.) and disagree politely by acknowledging first (I see what you mean, but…)
  • take part in a 10-minute group mini debate, with a chair who manages turns
  • tell a connected 2–3 minute story, true or invented, using at least four structures from Modules 1–4
Materials
  • Workbook 4.3 (opinion phrases, Exercises 1–5, the debate planner, the story frame, the Module 4 "I can" checklist)
  • Slides 14–18 · Review Race board game (Set L) + dice + counters · Mini-debate topic cards, opinion cards, chair cards, talking tokens (Set I) · Story cards (Set J) · Speaking checklist: one class grid + score slips (Set K)
  • Listening scripts 4.3-A (the discussion) and 4.3-B (the model story: The bag on the train) · optional: phones for recording

Left: an opinion = CLAIM + REASON + EXAMPLE. Openers: In my opinion… · If you ask me… · As far as I'm concerned… · It seems to me that… Support: The main reason is… · For example… · On the one hand… on the other hand…

Center: 👍 That's a good point. · I couldn't agree more. · So do I. / Neither do I. 🙏 I see what you mean, but… · That may be true, but… · I'm not sure I agree. Rule: acknowledge → then disagree.

Right: the story road: ① THEN (used to / was) → ② WHAT HAPPENED (past simple + a relative clause) → ③ WHAT PEOPLE SAID (reported speech) → ④ A GUESS / A FACT (must / might / can't · the passive) → ⑤ NOW & IF (present perfect · second conditional / wish)

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Review Race10S–S (groups of 3–4)Review C1–C11 with the Review Race board game (Set L). Roll, move, answer the square in a full sentence. The group says "Good!" or helps. Stop after 10 minutes; the player nearest the finish wins. Walk around and note which squares (which modules) are hard: this is early evidence for C13.
Opinion language12T–S → S–SSlides 14–15. Elicit ways to say "I think." Present the openers, the support phrases, and agree / disagree. CCQs. Drill with intonation (see notes). Then quick pairs: T says a statement ("Summer is the best season."); A gives an opinion + reason; B agrees or disagrees politely. Swap. Workbook Ex 1 and 4 (So do I / Neither do I) fast.
Model discussion: listen8T–SRead Script 4.3-A twice (be both voices, or ask a confident learner to read B). First listen: "Does A think social media is good or bad? Does B agree?" Second listen: Workbook Ex 2, learners tick the phrases they hear. Ask: "How did B disagree? Was it rude?"
Mini-debate prep7S (then S–S)Slide 16. Groups of 4. Each group takes one topic card (Set I). Each learner takes an opinion card (strongly agree / agree a little / disagree a little / strongly disagree), so nobody has to give their real opinion. One learner is the chair (they also speak). Everyone fills the debate planner in the workbook: opinion + 2 reasons + 1 example + 1 "If…" sentence. Notes only.
Mini debate + checklist15S–S (groups) · T–SRound 1 (fixed order): each speaker gives their opinion for 1 minute. Round 2 (open, 5–6 min): the chair invites responses; everyone uses talking tokens. Round 3 (1 min): the chair sums up: "Most of us think…" Then groups swap topics and chairs if time allows. You sit with one group at a time and score criteria 1, 4 and 5 of the speaking checklist.
Model story: listen6T–SRead Script 4.3-B (The bag on the train) twice. First listen: "What happened? How did it end?" Second listen: Workbook Ex 3, learners write one example of each structure they hear (used to, a relative clause, reported speech, a deduction, a passive, present perfect, second conditional or wish).
Plan the story8SSlide 17. Learners fill the story frame (Workbook 4.3): notes, not full sentences. A true story, a changed story, or a story card (Set J). Walk around and help; check that each step has one target structure. Quick pair rehearsal if time: tell it once to a partner, fast.
Story circles + checklist14S–S (groups of 3–4) · T–SNew groups of 3–4. Each learner tells the story (2–3 min) with notes turned over (a quick look is OK). Each listener asks one question and makes one comment with an opinion phrase ("If you ask me, you did the right thing!"). You sit with one group at a time and score criteria 1, 2 and 3 (and finish 4–5 for learners you didn't hear in the debate).
Feedback + module wrap5T–SContent first: "Which story had the best ending? Did anyone change their mind in the debate?" Then 3–4 sentences from your checklist notes, no names. Learners tick the Module 4 "I can…" lists. Celebrate: halfway through B1.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 4.3; the opinion paragraph and story in the journal. Tell learners plainly about C13: "Next class is a friendly check of Modules 1–4. It helps me help you." Hand out score slips in C13, not today.
Total9010 + 12 + 8 + 7 + 15 + 6 + 8 + 14 + 5 + 5
Hearing everyone

In 29 minutes of tasks you can score about 8–10 learners well. Choose one of these for the rest, and tell the class which one before the debate: (a) learners record their story on a phone during the story circles and play it for you in the C13 catch-up time; (b) the rest tell their story to you in the C13 catch-up stations; (c) in a large class, score the debate in C12 and the stories in C13. Anyone who prefers to tell the story only to you (not a group) may do so. That is a normal choice, not a problem.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation (review focus)

Meaning: an opinion is a claim plus support.

"In my opinion, cities are better for young people." Is this a fact or an opinion? (An opinion.) Is it enough? (No, we need a reason.) · "The main reason is that there are more jobs." What does this add? (The reason.) · "For example, my cousin found work in a month." And this? (An example.)
"I see what you mean, but I think exams are useful." Does the speaker agree? (No.) Is it polite? (Yes.) Why? (They show they listened first.) · "You're wrong." Polite? (No, it sounds rude in English.)
"I don't like crowds." — "Neither do I." Does the second person like crowds? (No.) · "I've been there." — "So have I." Why have, not do? (We copy the helper verb.)

Meaning: each structure has a job in the story. Show the story road on slide 17.

"I used to take the train every day." Do I still take it? (No, or not always.) Which step? (① Then.) · "She told me that she was on her way to an interview." What did she say? ("I'm on my way to an interview.") Which step? (③.) · "If I found a bag again, I'd do the same." Did I find a bag again? (No, it's imaginary.) Which conditional? (The second.)

Form: the review table.

OpinionIn my opinion, … · If you ask me, … · As far as I'm concerned, … · It seems to me that … · Personally, I'd say …If you ask me, public transportation should be free.
SupportThe main reason is (that) … · For example, … · On the one hand … on the other hand … · What's more, …The main reason is that it's cheaper for everyone.
AgreeThat's a good point. · Exactly! · I couldn't agree more. · So do I. / Neither do I. / So have I."I love cooking." — "So do I."
DisagreeI see what you mean, but … · That may be true, but … · I'm not sure I agree. · I'd look at it differently.That may be true, but it costs a lot of money.
Module 1present perfect (simple / continuous) · past simple · used to / wouldI've always wanted to… · I've been learning… · I used to…
Module 2first and second conditional · wishIf we banned cars, the air would be cleaner. · I wish I had more time.
Module 3reported speech · relative clausesShe told me that she was late. · the friend who helped me, which surprised everyone
Module 4the passive · modals of deduction · obligation / adviceIt is often said that… · That can't be true. · You should…

Pronunciation. Opinion openers are said as one chunk, with a small pause after: "If you ask me | …", "As far as I'm conCERNED | …". Polite disagreement has a friendly rise on the first part and a fall on the new idea: "I SEE what you mean ↗ | but I think it's too exPENsive ↘." A flat, low "No." sounds rude even with polite words: model the warm version. So do I / Neither do I: stress the last word, I. Neither is /ˈniðər/ or /ˈnaɪðər/; both are correct.

Drill script
  1. Opener chain: T: "public transportation / free" → S1: "In my opinion, public transportation should be free." → S2 (new opener, same idea): "If you ask me, …" → S3: "As far as I'm concerned, …" Round the room; no one repeats the previous opener.
  2. Add the support: T: "Cooking should be taught at school." → S: "…The main reason is that… For example, …"
  3. Acknowledge, then disagree: T gives an opinion; learners answer only with the polite frame: "I see what you mean, but…" / "That may be true, but…" Say the rise-fall together.
  4. So / Neither: T: "I love coffee." → "So do I." "I can't swim." → "Neither can I." "I've been to Canada." → "So have I." "I didn't sleep well." → "Neither did I."
  5. Story upgrade: T says a plain sentence; learners upgrade it with one B1 structure: "I took the train." → "I used to take the train every day." "A woman left her bag." → "A woman who was sitting across from me left her bag."
The mini debate — how to run it
  • Opinion cards make it safe. Deal the four cards face down. Learners argue the card's view, not their own. Say: "Good debaters can argue any side." Learners may swap cards within the group if a topic feels personal.
  • The chair keeps time (a phone timer), opens ("Today's question is… Let's start with you, Omar."), invites quiet speakers ("What do you think, Ana?"), stops long turns kindly ("Thanks, let's hear from someone else"), and sums up at the end. Give the chair card to a dominant speaker: the job needs listening.
  • Talking tokens in round 2: 3 each; put one in the middle every time you speak. No tokens left? Listen until everyone has used theirs, then everyone gets 3 more.
  • Every turn in round 2 starts with a response to the last speaker: agree or acknowledge first, then add a point. This keeps it a discussion, not four speeches.
  • Topics avoid politics, religion and immigration. If a group gets heated, the chair (or you) says: "Great ideas. Let's hear one reason from each side and sum up."
The speaking checklist — how to score it (feeds C13)

Five criteria, 0–3 points each, /15: 0 = no response · 1 = not yet B1 · 2 = B1 · 3 = strong B1. Score the whole performance across the debate and the story. Tick the module boxes (M1–M4) when you hear a structure used correctly. The full grid and descriptors are in the classroom materials (Set K).

CriterionWhat 3 points sounds like
1 · TaskDebate: a clear opinion with 2 reasons and an example. Story: connected, with a beginning, a middle and an end; the listener can follow it.
2 · Range (Modules 1–4)Uses structures from at least 3 of the 4 modules (e.g. used to, a second conditional, reported speech, a passive or a deduction), without being prompted.
3 · AccuracyErrors are few and don't block meaning; tenses are controlled (past simple for the events, present perfect for life experience).
4 · Interaction & opinion languageVaried openers; acknowledges before disagreeing; responds to the last speaker; asks or invites others.
5 · Fluency & pronunciationKeeps going with natural pauses; chunks and stress make it easy to follow; little translating in the head.

Feeding C13. Record each learner's scores and module ticks on the class grid. In C13, send learners to a catch-up station for any module with no tick and to the station for their lowest criterion: no M1 tick → tenses station (present perfect vs past simple, used to); no M2 tick → conditionals and wish station; no M3 tick → reported speech and relative clauses station; no M4 tick → passive and modals station (Sets B, E, F); criterion 4 ≤ 1 → opinion language (Workbook 4.3 Ex 1, 4, 5). If Progress Test 1 has its own speaking task, keep this grid anyway: it is your evidence of extended speaking for Modules 1–4, and it lets you give each learner one kind, specific next step.

ICQs before the mini debate and the story circles
Debate: Do you have to give your real opinion? (No, you use your opinion card.) · What does the chair do? (Keeps time, invites everyone, sums up.) · What do you do with a token? (Put one down every time you speak.) · Before you disagree, what do you say? ("I see what you mean, but…")
Stories: Does it have to be true? (No: true, changed, or invented.) · Can you read your notes? (Only a quick look.) · What do the listeners do? (Ask one question and give one opinion.) · Do you have to tell it to the group? (No, you can tell it only to the teacher.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give a phrase strip with 3 lines: "In my opinion, … because …" · "I agree. / I see what you mean, but…" · "For example, …". In the debate, the learner speaks third in round 1 (after hearing two models) and uses the strip. For the story, use the picture version of the frame: draw a small picture in each step. Target: a 5–6 sentence story with used to, the past simple, one "She said…" and one "It must be…". Score on the same checklist: a clear, short story can earn full marks on criteria 1, 3 and 5.

Stretch

Be the chair and sum up both sides fairly. Use On the one hand… on the other hand… and a "hedge" (It seems to me that…). In the story, use all five steps plus a passive (I was given…) and a wish. Tell the story in 2 minutes with no notes. Listeners ask two follow-up questions, not one. Write the story (8–10 sentences) for homework and underline one structure from each module.

L1 watch-outs
  • "I am agree" / "Are you agree?": Spanish estoy de acuerdo, French je suis d'accord, Portuguese estou de acordo, Italian sono d'accordo. Very common. Drill "I agree. · Do you agree?"
  • "According to me…" (French selon moi, Spanish según yo, and common in Indian English): according to is for other people ("according to the news"). Offer "In my opinion…"
  • "I think no" / "I think not" (Spanish creo que no, Portuguese acho que não): "I don't think so."
  • "In my opinion, I think…" (double opener, many L1s). Accept in speech; point out in writing.
  • "We discussed about…" (Spanish discutir sobre, Arabic): discuss has no preposition. Also note discutir can mean argue.
  • Directness differs. Dutch, German, Russian and Hebrew speakers may disagree very directly ("No, that's wrong."); Japanese, Korean, Chinese and Filipino speakers may avoid disagreeing at all. Teach the English middle: acknowledge, then disagree. Both styles are normal; English just has its own.
  • Tense drift in stories (Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai have no past endings): prompt with the step: "① Then: used to or use to?" Past perfect ("I had never done that") may appear; accept it, it's coming in 5.1.
Homework

Online lesson 4.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_12.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Record the two-part review project on your phone if you like (a story + an opinion). Workbook 4.3: write the opinion paragraph (5–6 sentences) and your story (6–8 sentences) in the journal, and underline one structure from each module. Review the Module 1–4 "I can…" lists before C13.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B I see what you mean, but I think they do measure some skills. · Q2 B If we banned cars, the air would be cleaner. · Q3 (new) B Neither do I. Discussion (Ex 2, Script 4.3-A): heard: If you ask me · the main reason is · for example · I'm not sure I agree · I see what you mean, but · on the other hand · That's a good point · Exactly · It seems to me that · I take your point. Not heard: In my opinion · I couldn't agree more.

Closing the Module

End of C12: Module 4 wrap-up and the halfway point

Recap on slide 18: It is made in… / It was built in… · It must be… / It might be… / It can't be… · You have to… / You don't have to… / You should… · In my opinion… / I see what you mean, but… Learners tick the three "I can…" lists. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Use those notes, the Review Race observations and your speaking checklist to plan the C13 catch-up stations.

Celebrate the halfway point. Learners have finished half of B1. Ask each person to say one thing they can do now that they couldn't do in Week 1, with an opinion phrase if they like ("If you ask me, my stories are much better now!"). Keep it short and warm.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it can test: present and past simple passive (+ / − / ?), when to use by + agent, active → passive, made of / from / in / by, must / might / may / could / can't for present deduction, can't vs mustn't, have to / don't have to / mustn't / should, and opinion, agreeing and disagreeing phrases. The C12 speaking checklist gives you the spoken evidence.

Watch for in Module 5: the past perfect (5.1) brings had + past participle, the same participle as the passive. Learners may mix them ("The bridge had built in 1932"). Narrative tenses (5.2) reuse today's story road, so keep the story frames. Keep a 2-minute deduction warmer ("It must be… / It can't be…") in C14–C16: it is quick and learners enjoy it.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 5 — Narrating Past & Future

Three classes that turn learners into storytellers and planners. First the past perfect lets them rewind to "the past before the past" (When I got to the station, the train had already left). Then the three narrative tenses work as one team to tell a real story with a scene, a problem and an ending. Last, learners sort out will / going to / the present continuous and add the future continuous and time clauses (This time tomorrow I'll be flying. I'll call you when I land.). The module starts right after Progress Test 1, so C14 opens by handing the tests back in a warm, low-stress way.

3 lessons Lessons 5.1–5.3 Classes C14–C16 Weeks 7–8 90 min per class CEFR B1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups, discussion) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-5-narrating.html. Print materials/module-5/classroom-materials.html before C14. It holds the flashcards, the story & future identity cards, the listening scripts, the What Had Happened? picture-clue game, the Worst Day Ever story-chain kit and the Life in 2040 discussion cards.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences and dialogues again at home.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. Learners met the past continuous + past simple ("I was cooking when the phone rang") and going to / will / might / present continuous for arrangements at A2. Only the past perfect, the future continuous and time clauses are really new. Check what they know in each lead-in and move fast where they are strong. Spend the saved time on speaking.
  • Fluency first, accuracy after. In the freer stages, don't stop a story to fix a tense. Listen, write down 4–6 sentences you hear (good ones too), and correct them together at the end, with no names. During the controlled stages, correct on the spot and briefly.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use some British forms (film, cinema, holiday, lift = a free ride, cancelled, labelled, organised, practise, round the table). Teach movie, movie theater, vacation, ride, canceled, labeled, organized, practice, around the table. Accept the British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Rewind → tell → fast-forward. In C14 learners learn to rewind: to explain what had already happened before a past moment (the cause behind a surprise). In C15 they put the whole camera together: background (was raining), main events (the bus stopped) and flashback (I'd forgotten my umbrella), with connectors, to tell a real story. In C16 they fast-forward: they choose the best future form, picture themselves in the middle of a future moment (This time next week I'll be…), and discuss how life might look in 2040. Every class ends with a longer speaking turn than the class before: a 6-sentence story → a 2-minute group story → a 15-minute discussion.

Trauma-informed: stories and futures are personal

Past stories. "Tell me about your worst day" can open painful memories (war, migration, loss, accidents, illness). This module keeps stories light and fictional by default: the picture-clue game and the story chain use invented characters. When learners tell their own story, the choice is always theirs: a small, light real story (a missed bus, lost keys, a cooking disaster, a funny mistake), a story identity card, or an invented story. Say the "light" rule out loud: "Choose a small problem with a happy or funny ending." Never ask a follow-up about why someone chose a card.

Futures. For some adults the future is uncertain: a visa or asylum decision, a job search, family far away. Never ask "Where will you be living next year?", "Will you stay here?" or "When will you see your family?" In C16 the main discussion is about society (transport, work, shopping, schools in 2040). The personal part is optional and short: this time next week (safe and small), a future identity card, or an imagined person in 2040. Anyone can say "Pass." Offer these choices every time, so choosing a card is normal and not a sign that something is wrong.

Before the Module

  • Mark Progress Test 1 before C14. Write a short, positive comment on every paper: one thing they did well + one thing to practice. Collect 4–5 common errors from different papers for the C14 board (no names). Note which learners scored below about 60% so you can offer a quiet 1:1 chat at the break.
  • Print the picture flashcards (one set for the board). Card stock helps.
  • For C14: one What Had Happened? picture-clue deck per group of 3–4 (16 cards). Fold or cover the answer strip at the bottom of each card before class, or cut it off and keep the answers yourself.
  • For C15: one Worst Day Ever kit per group of 4: the story map (A4), 1 starter card, 12 event cards, 1 reaction strip. Cut the cards.
  • For C16: one set of Life in 2040 topic cards and one set of role cards per group of 4, plus 3 talking chips per learner (paper clips, coins or the printed chips).
  • Print the story & future identity cards (6 people). Keep them on a side table for all three classes: anyone can take one without asking.
  • Optional: a timer on screen (phone or free online timer) for the story retells in C15 and the discussion in C16.

Module at a Glance

5.2C15 · wk 8Narrative tenses: past simple (main events) + past continuous (background, while) + past perfect (flashback); connectors first, then, after that, while, suddenly, by the time, a few minutes later, in the end; story shape; listener reactionstell a 1–2 minute story with a scene, a problem and an ending, using all three tenses and 4+ connectors, and react as a good listener
5.3C16 · wk 8Which future: will / going to / present continuous; future continuous will be + -ing (in progress at a future time; polite questions); time clauses when, as soon as, before, after, until + present simplechoose the best future form, say what they'll be doing at a future moment, and discuss life in 2040 with reasons, using 3+ future forms

Lesson Plans

5.1 · C14

Past Perfect — The Earlier Past

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • reflect on their Progress Test 1 results and set one personal goal
  • form the past perfect for every person: had / 'd + past participle, hadn't, Had you…? and short answers Yes, I had. / No, I hadn't.
  • put the earlier of two past actions in the past perfect and the later one in the past simple: When I arrived, the train had already left.
  • use before, after, by the time, already, just, never … before, with already / just / never between had and the participle
  • explain a cause with because … had … and know when two past simples are enough (a plain sequence)
  • tell a 6-sentence "when I arrived, … had already …" story (light real, a story identity card, or invented)
Materials
  • Marked Progress Test 1 papers (in folders or face-down) · sticky notes · Workbook "My goal after Progress Test 1" box
  • Workbook 5.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My story" plan)
  • Slides 2–8 · Picture flashcards (5.1 "what had happened" events) · What Had Happened? picture-clue decks (1 per group of 3–4) · Story identity cards
  • Listening scripts 5.1-A (Why was Sam late?) and 5.1-B (dictation)

Left (C14 only): "We can…" + three class strengths from the test (e.g. talk about experiences · imagine with "if" · report what people said).

Center: a time line with three points: ① the train left (had left) → ② I arrived (arrived) → ③ NOW. Under it: When I arrived, the train had already left. = The train had already left when I arrived.

Right: had ('d) + past participle (all persons) · hadn't + pp · Had you + pp…? → Yes, I had. / No, I hadn't. Signal words: by the time · before · after · because · had already / just / never + pp.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Welcome & Progress Test 1 return12T–S · S–S · SSlide 2. See "Returning Progress Test 1" below: strengths on the board, papers back privately, pairs fix 4 anonymous errors, each learner writes one goal in the workbook box.
Lead-in: what had happened?6T–SSlide 3 (station picture). Tell it with feeling: "Last week I ran to the station. I got to the platform at 8:05… and the platform was empty." Elicit guesses: "The train left!" Accept, then ask: "Which happened first: the train leaving, or me arriving?" Draw the time line (board center). Say the model: "When I arrived, the train had already left." Don't explain yet: get 3–4 learners to repeat it.
Grammar: past perfect15T–SSlides 4–6. Meaning (the earlier past; order, not calendar time), form (had + participle, same for everyone; 'd; hadn't; Had…?), signal words and their position. CCQs and drill (see notes). Quick participle check: T says "go, see, eat, write, take, lose, forget, leave"; learners say the participle (the same list as the present perfect in Module 1).
Listening: Why was Sam late?8T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 5.1-A twice. First listen: "Was Sam late? Was his manager angry?" Second listen: learners number the six events in the order they happened (not the order Sam says them). Pairs compare, then say two sentences: "When he got to the bus stop, the bus had already left."
Pronunciation: 'd & hadn't5T–S"I'd finished" vs "I finished": say pairs; learners show 1 finger (past simple) or 2 fingers (past perfect). Then Script 5.1-B dictation (4 sentences, each read 3 times). Learners check against the slide.
Controlled practice12S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone, then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on (usually Ex 3 item 4 and Ex 5, "Do we need the past perfect?"). Early finishers start the "My story" plan.
Freer: What Had Happened? game20S–S (groups) → T–SSlides 7–8. Groups of 3–4 with one picture-clue deck. (1) Demo one card with the class (3 min). (2) Play: the reader shows the scene and clues; the others ask Had…? questions and make guesses until someone finds the reason (12 min). (3) Each group chooses its funniest wrong guess and tells the class (3 min). (4) Early finishers: "My story" plan in the workbook. ICQs first.
Feedback7T–SDelayed error correction: write 5 sentences you heard (2 good, 3 with errors, no names), e.g. "When I arrived, the train already left." · "She had forget her keys." · "Had you did it?" Pairs fix them. Praise one good question and one creative guess.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 5.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box.
Total9012 + 6 + 15 + 8 + 5 + 12 + 20 + 7 + 5
If you run long

The test return must stay at 12 minutes. Cut the dictation (Script 5.1-B) and set Ex 5 as homework. If the game is short on time, stop after 10 minutes of play and skip the "funniest wrong guess" round. Never cut the game: it is where Had…? questions and 'd + participle become real.

Returning Progress Test 1 (12 min) — keep it warm

1. Celebrate first (3 min). Before any paper comes out, write "We can…" on the board and list three things the whole class did well (for example: talk about experiences with the present perfect · imagine with "If I had…, I would…" · report what people said). Say it out loud.

Four modules of B1! You can talk about your experiences, imagine, report what people said, and use the passive. That's a lot of English. Give yourselves a hand.

2. Return privately (2 min). Hand papers back face-down or in folders. Never read scores aloud and never compare learners. Everyone gets a written comment: one strength + one thing to practice.

3. "Let's fix it!" (5 min). Show 4 anonymous errors from the tests. Use your own learners' errors if you can; typical ones at this point: I have seen that movie last week. · If I will see her, I'll tell her. · She said me that she was tired. · The bridge was build in 1990. (Also common: I wish I have more time. · The woman which lives next door… · He must to be at work.) Pairs fix them. Take answers. Praise the fixing, not the mistake-free papers.

4. One goal (2 min). Each learner writes one goal in the workbook box ("My goal after Progress Test 1") or on a sticky note: "I want to practice ___. Every week I will ___." Give two examples: "I want to practice reported speech. I will report one conversation a day in my journal." / "I want to speak more. I will tell a friend one story in English every weekend."

A test is a map. It tells us where we are. It doesn't tell us who we are.

Some adults carry hard memories of school and tests. Keep your voice light and move on quickly. Offer a 1:1 chat at the break to anyone who struggled (below about 60%), and plan catch-up with the Module 1–4 online lessons. Don't let the test return grow past 12 minutes. The best encouragement is a good, successful lesson straight after it, and today's grammar is kind: learners already know the past participles from the present perfect (Lesson 1.1).

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the past before the past. Two past actions; the past perfect marks the one that happened first. It is about order, not about "a long time ago."

"When I arrived, the train had already left." Two actions: what are they? (I arrived; the train left.) Which happened first? (The train left.) Did I see the train? (No.) Which verb is in the past perfect? (had left: the earlier one.)
"When I arrived, the train left." Did I see the train? (Yes, it was leaving as I arrived.) So is it the same meaning? (No!)
"I was hungry because I hadn't eaten all day." Which came first: not eating, or being hungry? (Not eating.) Is "hadn't eaten" the cause or the result? (The cause.)

Meaning: when you don't need it. A plain sequence in order: I got home, cooked dinner and watched TV. The order is already clear, so use past simples. Save the past perfect for when you jump back.

"I got up, had breakfast and left." Do I need "had had breakfast"? (No. The order is clear: one, two, three.) · "After I'd finished, I rested." Is "after I finished" also OK? (Yes. "After" already shows the order.)

Contrast with the present perfect (stretch, 1 minute): I've eaten, so I'm not hungry now. / I'd eaten, so I wasn't hungry then.

Form.

Positivesubject + had ('d) + past participleShe had gone. · They'd left. · I'd finished my work.
Negativesubject + hadn't + past participleWe hadn't met before that day.
QuestionHad + subject + past participle…?Had you seen the email? — Yes, I had. / No, I hadn't.
Wh- questionWh- + had + subject + pp…?Why had he already gone? · What had they done?
Positionhad + already / just / never + ppThe bus had already gone. · I had just sat down. · I'd never flown before.
Signal wordsby the time · before · after · because · whenBy the time we arrived, it had closed.

Pronunciation. In speech had is almost always 'd, and it is very small: I'd /aɪd/, she'd /ʃid/, they'd /ðeɪd/, it'd /ˈɪtəd/. Learners must learn to hear it: "I'd finished" and "I finished" sound almost the same, so the participle and the context carry the meaning. Stress falls on the participle and the time word: "the train had alREADy LEFT." hadn't /ˈhædənt/ is stressed and clear (two syllables). In short answers use the full, stressed form: "Yes, I HAD." (never "Yes, I'd.") Remind learners that 'd + participle = had; 'd + base verb = would (I'd gone / I'd go).

Drill script
  1. Back-chain: "left" → "already left" → "the train had already left" → "When I arrived, the train had already left." Choral ×3, half the room, then 4–5 individuals. Then a personal version with 'd: "When I got there, I realized I'd forgotten my phone."
  2. Two actions → one sentence (T gives two past actions in the real order; learners join them): T: "The movie started. Then we arrived." → S: "When we arrived, the movie had started." T: "The shop closed. Then I got there." → S: "By the time I got there, the shop had closed."
  3. 'd substitution: T: "I had forgotten my keys." → S: "I'd forgotten my keys." T: "She… lose her phone" → S: "She'd lost her phone." T: "They… never see snow" → S: "They'd never seen snow."
  4. Question and short answer, open pairs: S1: "Had you eaten when you came to class?" S2: "Yes, I had. / No, I hadn't." S2 → S3: "Had you used English at work before this course?" S3 answers… (Keep questions small and everyday.)
  5. Because chain: T: "Omar was tired." → S1: "…because he hadn't slept well." → T: "Ana was happy." → S2: "…because she'd passed her test."
What Had Happened? — how to run it
  • Groups of 3–4, one deck face down. Player 1 is the reader: takes a card, reads the scene aloud ("When Ana got to the platform, she was very upset.") and shows the picture clues. The answer strip is hidden (folded under).
  • The others are detectives. They ask yes/no questions with Had…? ("Had the train left?" "Had she lost her ticket?") and the reader answers "Yes, she had." / "No, she hadn't." They can also guess with a statement: "I think she'd missed the train."
  • The first detective to find the answer on the strip keeps the card. After 8 questions with no answer, the reader reads the strip aloud ("The train had already left.") and keeps the card. Then the next player is the reader. The most cards wins.
  • Stretch rule: before a detective can guess, they must say the earlier and the later action ("First the train left, then she arrived").
  • Round 2 (if time): groups use the two blank cards to invent a scene for another group.
  • Walk around and listen. Note sentences for feedback; don't stop the game to correct.
ICQs before the game
Can the detectives see the answer? (No!) · What's the first word of your question? ("Had.") · "Had she lost her ticket?" What does the reader say? ("Yes, she had." or "No, she hadn't.") · How many questions before the reader tells the answer? (Eight.) · Who keeps the card? (The person who finds the answer.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with a small bank of 8 participles (left, gone, lost, forgotten, eaten, closed, started, finished) on a strip, and the frame "When she arrived, ___ had ___." In the game, the teacher or a partner reads the scene aloud; the learner uses the picture clues and one question frame: "Had he/she ___?" Target: 4 spoken past perfect sentences and 3 questions. In Ex 1, number only 4 events (the first four).

Stretch

Use all six signal words in the "My story" plan. Add a because … had … cause and a never … before first-time experience. Contrast the present perfect ("I've lost my keys" = now I can't get in) with the past perfect ("I'd lost my keys" = back then). In the game, give the reason in two ways: "When she arrived, the train had left." / "The train had left by the time she arrived."

Handling dominant speakers (all module)

In group games one confident learner often answers every card. Use turn rules, not personality: the reader changes every card; each detective may ask only one question per turn (clockwise); the person who just won a card asks last next time. Say it as a game rule for everyone, so no one feels singled out. If one learner still leads, give them a job: "You're the judge. Check that every question starts with Had." Quiet learners can read the card aloud first, which gives them a safe, prepared turn.

L1 watch-outs
  • "When I arrived, the train already left." (past simple + already): Chinese (已经), Vietnamese (đã), Thai, Indonesian, Tagalog and Russian (uzhe) show order with a time word, not a tense. It is understood. Recast and point at the time line: "Which came first? → had left."
  • "She had forget / had went": Arabic, Spanish and many others. Same participle list as the present perfect: go → gone, forget → forgotten, leave → left.
  • "Had you did it?" / "Did you had seen…?": learners who have just drilled did questions. "One helper only: had."
  • Overusing the past perfect for any "long ago" past: "I had been born in 1985." "When I was a child, I had lived in a village." Spanish, Portuguese, French and Italian speakers sometimes use their pluperfect this way. Ask "Earlier than what?" If there is no later past moment, use the past simple.
  • Not hearing 'd: Vietnamese, Chinese and Korean speakers (final consonants) may drop the /d/ in I'd and the /t/ in hadn't. Use the 1-finger / 2-finger listening game and the dictation.
  • 'd = had or would? Learners from any L1 mix these in reading. Teach the test: look at the next verb (participle → had; base verb → would).
Homework

Online lesson 5.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_13.html. Listen to the model sentences and the airport conversation with 🔊 and say them out loud. Do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 5.1: finish "My story" (6 past perfect sentences: light real, a story identity card, or invented), "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B had cooked · Q2 C The bus had already left when we arrived. · Q3 (new) B had never flown. Sam (Ex 1): 1 forgot to charge his phone · 2 woke up at 7:30 · 3 left his ID card at home · 4 got to the bus stop · 5 waited twenty minutes · 6 arrived at the office.

5.2 · C15

Narrative Tenses — Telling a Story

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use the past simple for the main events, the past continuous for the background and actions in progress, and the past perfect for a flashback to something earlier
  • combine a long background action and a short event with while (+ past continuous) and when (+ past simple): While I was walking home, it started to rain.
  • order a story with connectors: first, then, after that, while, suddenly, a few minutes later, by the time, in the end
  • build a story in five steps: scene → flashback → problem → what happened next → ending
  • react as a listener: No way! · Oh no! · Then what happened? · How did it end?
  • tell a 1–2 minute story (fictional, a story identity card, or a light real one)
Materials
  • Workbook 5.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My story map")
  • Slides 9–13 · Flashcards (5.2 mishaps + connector cards) · Worst Day Ever story-chain kit (1 per group of 4) · Story identity cards
  • Listening scripts 5.2-A (Dev's worst day) and 5.2-B (dictation)

Left: 🎥 the camera: BACKGROUND = was / were + -ing (it was raining) · ACTION = past simple (the bus stopped) · FLASHBACK = had + pp (I'd forgotten my umbrella).

Center: the story map: ① SCENE → ② FLASHBACK → ③ SUDDENLY… → ④ THEN / WHILE… → ⑤ BY THE TIME… → ⑥ IN THE END…

Right: while + was -ing (long) · when + past simple (short) · Listener: No way! · Oh no! · Then what happened? · How did it end?

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: why?8T–S → S–SReview C14. Quick-fire "why" prompts from the What Had Happened? deck ("Ana was upset at the station. Why?") → "Because the train had left." Then pairs read one sentence from their "My story" homework and the partner asks one Had…? question.
Lead-in: Dev's worst day8T–S → S–S"You'll never believe what happened to my friend Dev." Read Script 5.2-A once for gist, like a real story (pause, use your face). Gist questions: "Was it a good day or a bad day? Did it end well?" Second listen: Workbook Ex 1 (six questions). Pairs compare.
Clarify: three tenses, one story14T–SSlides 9–10. The camera: background / action / flashback. Take 3 sentences from Dev's story and put them on the board time line. While vs when. CCQs and drill (below). Then Workbook Ex 4 together, quickly: label S / C / P in the Maria story.
Connectors & story shape8T–S → S–SSlide 11. Stick the connector flashcards on the board in story order. Show the 6-step story map. Pairs retell Dev's story in 6 steps, one step each, using the map (2 min).
Controlled practice12S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 (choose the tense), Ex 3 (connectors) and Ex 5 (order the events, then write the story) alone, then check in pairs. Board-check only disagreements.
Listener reactions5T–S → S–SSlide 12. Read the model dialogue in two halves of the room, then swap. Drill 6 reactions with feeling: "No WAY!" "Oh NO!" "Then what HAPpened?" Say: "A story needs a good listener too."
Freer: Worst Day Ever story chain22S–S (groups) → S–S (new pairs)Slide 13. (1) Groups of 4 build a story with a starter card, event cards and the story map: one step each, round the table, twice (10 min). (2) Each learner retells the group's story to a partner from another group, 2 minutes; the partner reacts and asks one question (4 min). (3) Change partners; retell faster, in 90 seconds (3 min). (4) Option: learners who want to may tell a light real story or a story identity card story instead in step 3. Two volunteers tell the class (3 min). ICQs first.
Feedback8T–SDelayed error correction: 5 sentences heard (2 good, 3 with errors), e.g. "Suddenly the phone was ringing." · "While I walked home, it started to rain." · "At the end, everything was fine." Pairs fix. Praise one great scene-setting sentence and one great reaction.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 5.2; workbook journal (a 4–6 sentence story from this week, verbs labeled S / C / P). Learners tick "I can…".
Total908 + 8 + 14 + 8 + 12 + 5 + 22 + 8 + 5
If you run long

Set Ex 5 as homework, and do only one retell round (step 2) in the story chain. Never cut the retell: telling the same story again to a new listener is where fluency grows (the second telling is always smoother). Keep the listener reactions: they make the retell a real conversation.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the camera. The past simple is the action shots that move the story forward. The past continuous is the wide background shot (weather, place, what was going on). The past perfect is a quick flashback.

"It was raining when Dev left the house." Which started first: the rain, or Dev leaving? (The rain.) Did the rain stop when he left? (No, it continued.) Which is the background? (It was raining.)
"While Dev was drinking his coffee, the bus stopped." Which is long? (Drinking coffee.) Which is short and sudden? (The bus stopped.) Can I say "the bus was stopping" here? (Not for a sudden event: "stopped.")
"He'd left his umbrella at home." Did this happen in the middle of the story or before it? (Before.) Why does the listener need it? (To understand why he got wet.)

Balance. The past simple is the most common tense in a story. The past perfect appears only a few times, for the moments you step back. Tell learners: "Most of the story: past simple. A little background. One or two flashbacks."

Form.

Past simpleverb + -ed / irregularmain events, in orderShe opened the door and walked in.
Past continuouswas / were + -ingbackground · action in progressIt was raining and the wind was blowing.
Past perfecthad + past participlewhat had happened earlierShe had left her umbrella at home.
while / whenwhile + was -ing · when + past simplelong action + short interruptionWhile we were eating, the lights went out. · I was walking home when I saw an old friend.
Connectorsfirst · then · after that · while · suddenly · a few minutes later · by the time (+ had) · in the end

Pronunciation. Storytelling is performance. was and were are weak in the middle of a sentence: /wəz/, /wər/ ("it wəz RAINing"). Pause before suddenly and stress it: "…and SUDdenly…". In the end falls to finish the story. Past simple -ed: remind learners of /t/ /d/ /ɪd/ (walked, arrived, started); it carries the whole story. Listener reactions need big intonation: "No WAY! ↗↘" A flat "no way" sounds bored.

Drill script
  1. Background + event (T gives two prompts; learners join them with when): T: "I / walk home · it / start to rain" → S: "I was walking home when it started to rain." T: "we / have dinner · the lights / go out" → S: "We were having dinner when the lights went out."
  2. Now with while: same prompts → "While I was walking home, it started to rain." Choral, then individuals.
  3. Add a flashback: T: "I got wet." → S: "I got wet because I'd forgotten my umbrella." T: "She was nervous." → S: "She was nervous because she'd never given a speech before."
  4. Connector chain: T holds up a connector card; the next learner adds a sentence to a class story: "First…" → "Then…" → "While…" → "Suddenly…" → "By the time…" → "In the end…"
  5. Reaction ping-pong: T says a story line ("…and the bus broke down!"); learners react with the right phrase and intonation ("Oh no!" / "No way!" / "Then what happened?").
Worst Day Ever story chain — how to run it
  • Groups of 4 with the story map (A4) in the middle, a starter card (a fictional character and a big day: a wedding photographer, a first day at a new job, an anniversary dinner…) and 12 event cards face down.
  • Player 1 reads the starter card and says step ① (the scene) with the past continuous: "It was a hot Saturday morning, and the birds were singing." Player 2 takes an event card and uses it in step ② or ③, and so on round the table. The map shows which tense each step needs. Go round the table twice (steps 1–6, then a better second version).
  • Anyone can change an event card for a new one. The story should end well or funny: "In the end, … and everyone laughed."
  • Retell: every learner can tell the group story alone, so everyone practices the whole thing. Partners from other groups listen, react ("No way!") and ask one question ("How did it end?").
  • Real stories are optional. In the second retell, learners may tell their own light story (a small problem with a happy or funny ending) or a story identity card story. Never ask anyone to tell a real "worst day."
  • Third and mixed conditionals. Stories invite "If I had left earlier, I would have…". This is B2. Accept it if a learner says it, and don't teach it. Offer the B1 way: "I should leave earlier next time!" or "Next time I'll check the time."
ICQs before the story chain
Is this a true story or an invented story? (Invented.) · Step 1: which tense? (was / were + -ing: the background.) · Step 5 starts with…? ("By the time" + had.) · How many times do you go round the table? (Twice.) · Must the story end badly? (No, happy or funny.) · When your partner tells a story, what do you do? (React and ask one question.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Use the story map with picture icons only (🎥 scene, ⏪ flashback, ⚡ suddenly, ➡️ then, ⏱️ by the time, 🏁 in the end) and a sentence strip for each step ("It was ___ and ___ was ___-ing." / "Suddenly, ___." / "In the end, ___."). In the chain, the learner takes steps 1, 3 or 6 (the easiest frames). Target: tell 3 steps of the story with 3 connectors. In Ex 1, answer questions 1, 3 and 6 only.

Stretch

Add a second flashback with just or never … before. Use a few minutes later and it turned out that…. In the retell, change the point of view (tell it as a different character) or add direct speech ("She said, 'Don't worry!'"). Early finishers write the group story (8–10 sentences) and label every verb S / C / P.

Fluency before accuracy — the retell

In the retell rounds, your job is to listen, not to correct. Walk around with a notebook and write sentences down exactly as you hear them. Correct only if the listener can't understand. The time limit is part of the task: telling the same story again, faster, to a new person builds fluency. Afterwards, do the delayed correction with anonymous sentences, and always include one or two excellent sentences to praise.

L1 watch-outs
  • Continuous for sudden events: "Suddenly the phone was ringing." Russian and Polish (aspect), Hindi/Urdu and Turkish speakers may choose by "how it feels," not by "short or long." Ask: "Long background or one short moment?"
  • Continuous with state verbs: "I was knowing him." "The house was having a garden." Spanish, Portuguese and French speakers map their imperfect (which covers states) onto was -ing. Teach: know, have (own), like, want, believe stay simple.
  • Dropping was / were: "I walking home when…" Arabic, Chinese, Vietnamese, Haitian Creole. Point at the board: was + -ing.
  • he / she in a story about a character: Tagalog, Chinese, Finnish, Turkish, Persian, Yoruba and Hungarian speakers (one word for he/she). Stories with named characters make this very visible. Recast gently: "Dev? He got wet."
  • "At the end" / "At the final" / "Finally" for "in the end": Spanish al final, Portuguese no final, French à la fin. In the end = the result of the story; at the end of + noun (at the end of the day).
  • "Actually" for "now / at the moment": Spanish actualmente, French actuellement, Portuguese atualmente. In English actually = "in fact" (a surprise), which is great in stories: "It was actually my old teacher!"
Homework

Online lesson 5.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_14.html. Listen to "A Story in Action" with 🔊 and read it aloud; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 5.2: "My story map" (plan one story: the group story, a story identity card, or a light real one), "My new words" and the journal (4–6 sentences, label each verb S / C / P). Ready for next class: tell your story in 1 minute.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A was walking … heard · Q2 C had baked · Q3 (new) C went. Dev (Ex 1): 1 at ten o'clock · 2 sunny (the sun was shining) · 3 it broke down (it stopped suddenly) · 4 his umbrella · 5 soaked / completely wet, with coffee on his shirt · 6 he got the job.

5.3 · C16

Future Forms & Future Continuous

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • choose the best future form: will (decision now, prediction, offer, promise), going to (plan / intention, evidence), present continuous (fixed arrangement)
  • form and use the future continuous (will be + -ing) for an action in progress at a future time: This time tomorrow I'll be flying to Chicago.
  • ask polite, no-pressure questions: Will you be using the car tonight?
  • use the present simple (not will) after when, as soon as, before, after, until, by the time: I'll call you when I arrive.
  • discuss life in 2040 with reasons and at least three future forms, and agree / disagree politely
Materials
  • Workbook 5.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My future sentences," Module 5 look-back)
  • Slides 14–18 · Flashcards (5.3 future topics) · Life in 2040 topic cards + role cards (1 set per group of 4) · talking chips (3 per learner) · Future identity cards
  • Listening scripts 5.3-A (Nora and Sam plan the week) and 5.3-B (dictation)

Left: "Which future?" three boxes: ⚡ decide now / I think → will · 🧠 plan / 👀 evidence → going to · 📅 in the diary → present continuous.

Center: a time line: NOW → ⭐ this time tomorrow with a long arrow across the star: I'll be flying. · won't be + -ing · Will you be using the car tonight?

Right: I'll call you when I arrive. (❌ when I will arrive) · as soon as · before · after · until · by the time + present simple.

Stage plan

(3) dark clouds → "It's going to rain." Learners say the sentence; you ask "Decision now, arrangement or evidence?" This is A2 review: move fast.
Clarify: which future when8T–S → S–SSlide 14 table. CCQs for will vs going to, and going to vs present continuous. Make it clear that several forms are often possible: "Choose by meaning; don't panic." Pairs do Workbook Ex 2 items 1–3 at once.
Future continuous12T–SSlide 15. "Tomorrow at 10 a.m. I'll be teaching you. At 10 p.m. I'll be sleeping!" Draw the arrow across the point. Meaning, form, the polite question. CCQs and drill (below). Then Workbook Ex 4 (Priya's day) together.
