If you run long

Cut the listening's second task (find the forms) and set Ex 5 as homework. The lead-in and the "which future" stage are A2 review: if the class is strong, join them into 8 minutes and give the time to the discussion. Never cut the discussion: it is the first long, free B1 discussion of the course, and it prepares Module 7.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: which future? (review, fast)

(Phone rings.) "I'll get it!" Did I plan this? (No.) When did I decide? (Now.) · "I'm going to call her tonight." Plan or decision now? (A plan I already had.) · "I'm meeting Sara at six." Is it in my diary? (Yes: a fixed arrangement.) · "Look at those clouds: it's going to rain." Can I see something? (Yes: evidence.)

Meaning: future continuous. An action in progress at a point in the future: already started, not finished. It is the future version of the past continuous.

"This time tomorrow, I'll be flying to Chicago." At this time tomorrow, is the flight starting or already happening? (Already happening: I'm in the middle of it.) · "At 8 p.m. I'll watch the game." / "At 8 p.m. I'll be watching the game." Which one starts at 8? (The first.) In which one am I already watching at 8? (The second.)
"Will you be using the car tonight?" Am I asking you to do something? (No.) Am I asking about your plans? (Yes, politely.) Why is it polite? (It doesn't push. Maybe I want the car, but I don't say it directly.)

Meaning: time clauses. After when, as soon as, before, after, until, by the time, the meaning is future but the verb is present.

"I'll call you when I arrive." Have I arrived? (No: future.) Which verb is future? ("I'll call.") Why not "when I will arrive"? (After when, we use the present.) · "as soon as" = slow or fast? (Immediately.) · "We'll wait until she comes." Do we wait after she comes? (No, we stop waiting.)

Form.

willwill ('ll) + base verbdecision now · prediction · offer · promiseIt's cold. I'll close the window.
going toam / is / are going to + base verbplan / intention · evidenceWe're going to paint the kitchen.
Present continuousam / is / are + -ing + timefixed arrangementWe're flying to Madrid on Saturday.
Future continuouswill be + -ing · won't be + -ing · Will you be + -ing?in progress at a future time · polite questionAt 8 p.m. I'll be watching the game. · Will you be joining us?
Time clausemain clause (will) + when / as soon as / before / after / until + present simplefuture meaningI'll text you as soon as I land.

Pronunciation. I'll be links into one beat: /aɪl bi/ ("ail-bee"); stress the main verb: "I'll be FLYing to ChiCAgo." won't be /woʊnt bi/: keep the t (it carries the negative). In time clauses the stress is on both verbs: "I'll CALL you when I arRIVE." Polite questions rise gently at the end: "Will you be using the CAR tonight? ↗". In the discussion, softeners make predictions sound natural and open: "I think… · Probably… · I don't think… will…".

Drill script
  1. This time tomorrow (T shows a clock time and an activity): T: "9 a.m. · work" → S: "At 9 a.m. tomorrow, I'll be working." T: "8 p.m. · cook dinner" → S: "At 8 p.m., I'll be cooking dinner." Change the person: "she / they."
  2. Negative + reason: T: "Don't call me at 7." → S: "I won't be answering. I'll be driving."
  3. Make it polite: T: "Will you use the printer later?" → S: "Will you be using the printer later?" T: "Will you come to the meeting?" → S: "Will you be coming to the meeting?"
  4. Time-clause chain: S1: "I'll relax when I get home." → S2: "When I get home, I'll…" → S3: "As soon as I…, I'll…" Around the room.
  5. Which future? — fast: T gives a situation; learners answer at once: "Someone knocks at the door." (I'll get it.) · "Your flight is booked for Friday." (I'm flying on Friday.) · "You decided to learn to cook." (I'm going to take a class.) · "Your friend is about to drop a glass." (You're going to drop that!)
Life in 2040 discussion — how to run it
  • Warm-up (pairs, optional personal): "This time next week, I'll be…" Learners choose: their real next week (small and safe), a future identity card, or an imagined person in 2040 ("In 2040, Maya will be working on a farm in the city."). Nobody talks about visas, family plans or where they will live.
  • Groups of 4 with roles (role cards): the Chair reads the topic and makes sure everyone speaks; the Question-asker asks "Why do you think so?" and "What about…?"; the Timekeeper moves on after 3 minutes; the Reporter notes the most surprising prediction. Everyone also gives opinions.
  • Topics are about society, not about the learners' own lives: getting around, work, shopping, homes, schools, food, money, phones, our city, free time, doctors and hospitals, learning languages. Each card has two questions and a sentence starter.
  • Talking chips: each learner has 3 chips. Put one in the middle every time you speak for more than a sentence. When your chips are gone, you listen and ask questions until everyone has used theirs. Then everyone takes their chips back.
  • Language targets on the card: 1 prediction with will / might / won't / will probably, 1 will be + -ing ("In 2040 most people will be working from home."), 1 time clause ("When cars drive themselves, people won't need a license."), and one polite disagreement ("I'm not sure. I think…").
ICQs before the discussion
Are we talking about your own life in 2040? (No, about society: work, transport, shopping…) · Must you talk about your own next week? (No. You can choose a card or an imagined person.) · What does the Chair do? (Makes sure everyone speaks.) · When do you put a chip in the middle? (When I speak.) · No chips left: what do you do? (Listen and ask questions.) · What do you say if you disagree? ("I'm not sure. I think…")
Handling dominant speakers

B1 discussions often turn into one learner's speech. The talking chips and roles fix most of it without comment. Give a strong, talkative learner the Question-asker or Timekeeper role: they stay active but spend their energy getting others to talk. Give a quiet learner the Chair role with the card in hand: reading the question aloud is a safe first turn. If someone still takes over, walk past and say to the whole group: "Chair, has everyone answered this one?" Never correct a quiet learner in front of the group during the discussion.

Differentiation
Literacy support

Use the picture topic cards (🚗 💼 🛒 🏠 🏫) and three frames only: "In 2040, people will ___." / "I think ___ might ___." / "I don't think ___ will ___." For the future continuous, use the clock frame "At ___ tomorrow, I'll be ___-ing" with the Priya pictures from Ex 4. Target: 3 predictions and 1 "I'll be -ing" sentence. Pair the learner with a supportive Reporter.

Stretch

Use will probably / probably won't in the right position, by 2040 with the future continuous ("By 2040, we'll be using…" is fine), and two time clauses in one idea ("As soon as batteries get cheaper, most people will drive electric cars, and when that happens, cities will be quieter."). Early finishers write a 6-sentence "A day in 2040" paragraph with four future forms.

L1 watch-outs
  • "When I will arrive": French (quand j'arriverai), Russian (kogda ya priedu, future form), Greek and others use a future form after "when." Spanish and Portuguese use a subjunctive, so learners guess. Link it to the first conditional: "like if: present after when."
  • Present simple for the future: "I call you tomorrow." "Next week I go to Chicago." (German, Russian, Spanish, Chinese, Japanese). Understood; recast with the best form. Timetables are the exception: "The train leaves at 6."
  • "I'll flying" / "I will be fly": missing be (Arabic, Vietnamese, Chinese) or missing -ing. Board chant: "will + be + -ing."
  • "After ten years, cars will fly." for "in ten years": Spanish después de, Chinese 十年后, Korean 10년 후. In + amount of time = from now; after + amount = after something else.
  • German / Dutch "will" = want: "I will a new job" = I want. Back again from A2: "I want (a wish) / I'll (future)."
  • Very direct disagreement: "No. You are wrong." Some learners (German, Dutch, Russian, Israeli) may sound blunt; others (Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Filipino) may never disagree. Teach the middle: "I'm not sure. I think…" and "Maybe, but…".
Homework

Online lesson 5.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_15.html. Listen to the colleagues' conversation with 🔊 and read it aloud; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 5.3: "My future sentences" (next week or an identity card, plus 2040), "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B I'll phone you when I get home. · Q2 B will be flying · Q3 (new) B 'll be doing. Nora and Sam (Ex 1): 1 a small party for Leo's birthday · 2 he's seeing the dentist · 3 pick up the cake · 4 as soon as she gets the order number · 5 working from home. Forms: arrangement I'm having / I'm seeing · plan I'm going to order · offer I'll pick it up · future continuous Will you be using / I'll be working / we'll be eating · time clause as soon as I get it / until the party starts.

Closing the Module

End of C16: Module 5 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "rewind, tell, fast-forward": had + pp (the earlier past) → was -ing / past simple / had + pp together (a whole story) → will / going to / -ing / will be -ing (the future). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C17 warmer.

Check the C14 goals. At the end of C16, invite learners to read their Progress Test goal again (the "Look back" box in Workbook 5.3): "Did you practice it? What will you do next?" This is private: no one reports to the class.

Watch for in Module 6: articles (a / the / zero) show up in every story. Learners who just told stories will say "I went to the work" or "The life is hard." Keep a 2-minute story warmer in C17–C19: one learner gives a 3-sentence story, the class listens for articles as well as tenses.

Final assessment (C26) covers all eight modules. From this module it tests: past perfect (+ / − / ?), earlier vs later action, by the time / already / just / never … before, narrative tenses in a story, while / when, story connectors, the choice of future form, the future continuous, and time clauses with the present simple.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 6 — Precision & Detail

Three classes on the small words that make B1 English sound exact. First the articles (a / an, the and no article), organized around one question: Does my listener already know which one I mean? Then verb patterns: which verbs take -ing and which take to, plus the verbs that change meaning (stop, remember, forget, try). Last, degree and quantity: too / enough, so / such … that, (a) few / (a) little and all / most / some / none of, used for real in complaints and compliments.

3 lessons Lessons 6.1–6.3 Classes C17–C19 Weeks 9–10 90 min per class CEFR B1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups, mingle, role-play) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-6-precision.html. Print materials/module-6/classroom-materials.html before C17. It holds the flashcards, the new identity cards, the Editor texts, the Describe & Draw place cards, the verb-sort and meaning-change sentence cards, the Find Someone Who game, the complaints & compliments role-play cards, the Community Center problem-solving task and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences and dialogues again at home.
  • This is a precision module, not a new-tense module. Learners already communicate without perfect articles or verb patterns. The goal is accuracy that grows over time, not overnight perfection. Say this out loud in C17: "Article mistakes almost never stop people understanding you. We're polishing, not fixing something broken." Learners from article-free languages need to hear it.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. A2 covered like / want / would like + -ing / to and too / enough with adjectives. Check what learners know in each lead-in and move fast where they are strong. The new B1 work is the choice: the article decision flow and the fixed cases, verb + object + to, -ing after prepositions, the meaning-change verbs, so / such … that and few vs a few.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons often use British forms: film, holiday, favourite, practise, travelling, post a letter, queue, play football, at the weekend, and (6.1) go to hospital. Teach movie, vacation, favorite, practice, traveling, mail a letter, line, play soccer, on the weekend and go to the hospital (American English keeps the here). Accept the British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Which one? → Which form? → How much? In C17 learners decide which thing they mean (a new one, the one we both know, or things in general), and they act as editors who fix a real text. In C18 they decide which verb form follows another verb, and they learn that sometimes the form changes the meaning (I stopped buying coffee ≠ I stopped to buy coffee). In C19 they say how much (too cold, not warm enough, so loud that…, a few towels) and use it for a real-life job: complaining politely and giving a compliment. Every class ends with a speaking task where precision makes a difference to the listener.

Fluency first, then accuracy — and dominant speakers

Precision is easy to over-correct. In the freer stages, do not stop speakers to fix an article or an -ing form. Walk around with a notepad and write down what you hear (good and not yet right). Correct after the task, on the board, without names. Only interrupt if a partner truly cannot understand. In controlled stages (workbook, drills), correct on the spot, quickly and kindly.

Dominant speakers. B1 classes often have one or two fluent, confident talkers. Give every pair and group task a visible structure: a timer for each speaker, a talking token (a pen that passes), or a role (speaker / listener / checker in C18). In whole-class feedback, use "a new voice, please" and wait 5 seconds before you accept an answer. Give strong speakers a stretch job (the checker, the Editor B text, the manager role in C19) so they are busy, not bored.

Trauma-informed: places, memories and complaints

Three tasks in this module can touch painful things. C17 asks learners to describe a place they know well: for some, "home" is a camp, a shelter, or a place they lost. C18 uses remember doing: childhood memories are not easy for everyone. C19 practices complaints: some learners have had real, frightening conflicts with landlords, employers or officials. For every personal task, offer three choices out loud: a real place or memory, a new identity card (in the classroom materials), or a place card / an invented one. In C19 the role-plays are clearly roles on cards; nobody has to use a real complaint. Anyone can say "Pass." Say it every time, so choosing a card is normal and not a sign that something is wrong.

Before the Module

  • Print the picture flashcards (one set for the board): the a/an sound cards, the fixed-phrase cards (go to bed, by bus, play the guitar…), and the degree & quantity cards. Card stock helps. Cut them before C17.
  • Print the new identity cards and keep them on a side table for all three classes, where anyone can take one without asking.
  • For C17: one Editor B text per pair (the Editor A text is in the workbook), and one Describe & Draw place card per learner. Blank paper for drawing.
  • For C18: one verb-sort set and one meaning-change sentence card set per pair (cut and clip them), one Find Someone Who sheet per learner, and one two-minute talk checker card per pair. A phone timer.
  • For C19: one complaints & compliments A/B card set per pair (6 situations), and one Community Center problem-solving sheet per group of 3–4 (for early finishers or as the alternative task).
  • Bring a real mug or cup (the "coffee" lead-in in C17) and something too heavy or too high to reach (C19 lead-in).

Module at a Glance

LessonClassFocusLearners can…

There aren't enough towels. The staff were so helpful!)

Lesson Plans

6.1 · C17

Articles — a / an, the & No Article

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use a / an for a first mention, for "one of many," and for jobs (She's an engineer.), choosing an by the sound (an hour, a university)
  • use the when the listener knows which one: second mention, only one exists, clear from the situation, superlatives and ordinals, defined by what follows (the woman who called you)
  • use no article for general plurals and uncountables (I love music. Dogs are loyal.), most countries, languages, meals, days and sports
  • use the fixed cases as chunks: go to bed / school / work, have breakfast, by bus, play the guitar (and American go to the hospital)
  • find and fix article mistakes in a short text, and describe a place in 8+ sentences
Materials
  • Workbook 6.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "A place I know well" plan)
  • Slides 3–8 · a real mug or cup · a/an sound flashcards + fixed-phrase flashcards · Editor B text (1 per pair) · Describe & Draw place cards (1 per learner) + blank paper · new identity cards
  • Listening script 6.1-A (My new neighborhood). Script 6.1-B (dictation) is for the C18 warmer.

Left: the question in a box: Does my listener know which one? → YES: the · NO + one countable thing: a / an · NO + general plural / uncountable: — (no article)

Center: one noun, three ways: I need a coffee. (any cup) · The coffee here is great. (this coffee) · I don't drink coffee. (in general) · Under it: I saw a dog. → The dog was huge. (first → second mention)

Right: an + vowel SOUND: an apple · an hour · an honest man | a + consonant SOUND: a car · a university · a European city. Below: fixed: go to bed · have lunch · by bus · play the guitar · play soccer.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: this time tomorrow8S–S → T–SReview C16 (5.3, future forms). Pairs finish three sentence starters on the board: "This time tomorrow I'll be…" · "When I get home tonight, I'll…" · "I'm … on Saturday." (real, a new identity card, or imagined). Two pairs share one sentence each. Correct only "When I will get home" (time clause).
Lead-in: one noun, three ways7T–S → S–SHold up a mug. Say three sentences: "I need a coffee." "The coffee in this building is terrible!" "My sister doesn't drink coffee." Write them on the board. Ask: "What's different? Why a, why the, why nothing?" Let pairs guess for 1 minute. Then a quick class survey: "Does your first language have words like a and the?" Many will say no. Say: "Good. Today we find the one question behind them."
Grammar: a / an · the · zero15T–SSlides 3–4. Build the board: the question box, then the three uses of a / an, the reasons for the, and the zero article. Use the "which one?" test with the CCQs (below). Then slide 5: a or an? It's the sound. Hold up the sound flashcards (apple, egg, umbrella, hour, university, uniform); learners call out "an!" or "a!". Drill the weak forms (see Pronunciation).
Fixed cases6T–SSlide 6 + fixed-phrase flashcards. "go to bed" (to sleep) vs "The cat is on the bed" (the object). "go to school" (as a student) vs "go to the school" (the building, for a meeting). Meals: "have breakfast." Transport: "by bus / by car" (but "on the bus"). Instruments take the, sports don't. Flag the US/UK difference: "go to the hospital" (US).
Listening: my new neighborhood8T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 6.1-A twice at natural speed (weak articles). Learners write a / an / the / — in the 13 gaps. Pairs compare and say why for three gaps ("the park: second mention"). Check on slide or board.
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 (a, an, the or —) and Ex 4 (one mistake in each sentence) alone, then pairs check. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on. Early finishers write Ex 5 (one noun, three ways).
The Editor13S–SSlide 7. Workbook Ex 3: learners are the editors of a community newsletter. The writer's first language has no articles; the text has 12 mistakes (6 missing, 3 extra, 3 wrong). Pairs mark each one with the editor's code (^ = add, / = delete, circle = change) and write the correct article (8 min). Then pairs join another pair and compare (3 min). Class check on the slide: learners read the corrected sentence and say the reason (2 min). Fast pairs take the Editor B text (10 mistakes). ICQs first.
Freer: Describe & Draw15S–SSlide 8. (1) Each learner chooses a place: a real place they know well, a place card, or the place on their new identity card. They make notes in the workbook plan: 4 things with a / an, what's special about them with the, and 2 general sentences (4 min). (2) A describes; B draws, and asks "Where's the table?" "Is there a window?" (4 min). Swap (4 min). (3) Compare the drawings with the speaker's notes. Pairs tell each other one article they want to check (3 min). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed error correction: write 4 sentences you heard (2 good, 2 with errors, no names), e.g. "There is table near the window. Table is very old." · "I love the nature." Learners fix them and give the reason. Praise one clear a → the description.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 6.1 on screen and point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total908 + 7 + 15 + 6 + 8 + 8 + 13 + 15 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Shorten the fixed cases to the three most common (go to bed, have breakfast, by bus) and let Ex 2's last items do the rest. Don't cut the Editor: finding mistakes in someone else's writing is where learners start to see articles. If you must, do Describe & Draw one way only (A describes, B draws) and ask B to describe their own place at home for homework.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the one question. Does my listener already know which one I mean? If yes → the. If no, and it's one countable thing → a / an. If I mean things in general (a plural or an uncountable) → no article.

"I saw a dog in the park." Do you know this dog? (No, it's new.) · "The dog was huge." Which dog? (The one I just told you about.) · "Can you close the door?" Which door? (That one. We can both see it.) · "The sun is hot." How many suns? (One. It's unique.)
"I love music." All music, or one song? (All music, in general.) · "I love the music in this café." In general, or this music? (This music, here.) · "She's a doctor." Is this her job? (Yes.) Can I say "She's doctor"? (No. Jobs need a / an.)
"an hour" — the first letter is h. Why an? (We don't say the h. The first sound is a vowel.) · "a university" — the first letter is u. Why a? (It sounds like "you." That's a consonant sound.)
Fixed cases: "I go to bed at eleven." Am I going to the bed to fix it or to sleep? (To sleep.) · "My mom is in the hospital." American or British? (American. British says "in hospital.") · "She plays the piano. He plays soccer." Which one has the? (Instruments. Sports don't.)

Form.

a / ansingular countable noun: new, one of many, a jobI bought a phone. · Pass me a pen. · He's an engineer.
thesingular, plural or uncountable: the listener knows whichThe phone stopped working. · the sun · the best day · the woman who called
— (zero)plural or uncountable noun, in generalI like cats. · Water is essential. · Life is hard.
zero (fixed)most countries, languages, meals, days, cities, streets, sportsin Japan · speak Spanish · have lunch · on Monday · play chess
the (fixed)some countries, instruments, unique thingsthe USA · the UK · play the guitar · the internet
purpose vs placego to bed / school / work / church (for its purpose)go to the school (the building) · US: go to the hospital

Pronunciation. Articles are almost always weak and unstressed: a /ə/, an /ən/, the /ðə/ before a consonant sound. Before a vowel sound many speakers say the /ði/: "thee apple," "thee hour." An links to the next word: "an‿apple" sounds like "a-napple." Learners who stress every article ("I saw A dog") sound slow and robotic; drill the weak form in chunks: "a‿dog, the‿dog, an‿hour." The th sound /ð/: Spanish, French, Russian, Tagalog, Arabic and Vietnamese speakers may say "de" or "ze"; show the tongue between the teeth, but don't make it the focus today.

Drill script
  1. First → second mention: T: "I bought… jacket." → S: "I bought a jacket. The jacket was expensive." T: "I saw… man." → S: "I saw a man. The man was singing." Choral ×2, then 5–6 individuals with new nouns (a cake, an umbrella, a letter).
  2. a or an? T says a noun; learners say it with the article, fast: "hour" → "an hour" · "university" → "a university" · "honest man" → "an honest man" · "European city" → "a European city" · "umbrella" → "an umbrella" · "uniform" → "a uniform".
  3. General or specific? T: "music, general" → S: "I love music." T: "music, this café" → S: "I love the music in this café." T: "dogs, general" → S: "Dogs are loyal." T: "dogs, next door" → S: "The dogs next door are loud."
  4. Jobs chain: S1: "I'm a cook. What do you do?" → S2: "I'm an office assistant. What do you do?" (real job, a new identity card, or a dream job: "I want to be a…").
  5. Fixed chunks, choral: "go to bed · go to work · have breakfast · by bus · on the bus · play the guitar · play soccer." Then T gives a picture card; learners make a sentence about their day.
The Editor — how to run it
  • Set the scene: "You're the editors of the community newsletter. A new volunteer wrote this. Her first language has no articles. Her ideas are great; your job is to make it perfect before it's printed." This makes the mistakes someone else's and treats the writer with respect.
  • Tell them the number: 12 mistakes: 6 missing, 3 extra, 3 wrong. Knowing the number keeps pairs searching and stops them "fixing" correct articles. There are also correct articles that look suspicious (The children come every Friday; Most beds belong to families). Those are traps.
  • Editor's code: ^ add an article · / delete it · circle it and write the new one above. Pairs must agree on a reason for each change, using the board question.
  • Finished early? Editor B (a text message about a new job, 10 mistakes: 4 missing, 5 extra, 1 wrong). Answers are in the answer key.
  • L1 note to share: "If your language has no articles, like Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Russian, Vietnamese or Polish, missing articles are your typical mistake. If your language has articles, like Spanish, French, Portuguese or Arabic, extra the in general sentences is your typical mistake. Which editor are you?" Learners enjoy spotting their own pattern.
Describe & Draw — how to run it
  • A sits with the notes; B has a blank paper and a pencil. B must not see A's notes. A describes: "There's a big window on the left. The window has green curtains. Under the window there's a sofa…" B draws and checks: "Is the sofa under the window?"
  • Each description needs 8+ sentences: new things with a / an, then the same things with the, plus 2 general sentences with no article ("It sells coffee and cakes." "I love quiet mornings there.").
  • Place cards (café, bus station, library, laundromat, playground, workplace kitchen) give ready ideas. A learner with a new identity card describes that person's place. Nobody explains why they chose a card.
  • While they talk, you write down sentences for the feedback stage. Don't correct during the task.
ICQs before the Editor and Describe & Draw
Editor: How many mistakes? (Twelve.) · How many are missing articles? (Six.) · What does ^ mean? (Add an article.) · Do you change every article? (No! Some are correct.) · Do you need a reason? (Yes, for every change.)
Describe & Draw: Can B see A's notes? (No.) · First time you say "window": a or the? (A.) · Second time? (The.) · Must you describe your real home? (No. A place card or a new identity card is fine.) · How many sentences? (Eight or more.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on two jobs of articles: a for a new thing and the for the same thing again, plus a / an with jobs. In the Editor, give a large-print copy of the first four lines only, with each mistake already underlined: the learner only decides "add, delete or change." In Describe & Draw, the learner uses the place card's picture list and says 4 pairs of sentences ("There's a table. The table is big."). Pair them with a patient partner who draws.

Stretch

Add the + defined by what follows (the café on the corner, the man who works there) and superlatives (the best seat, the only window) to the description. In the Editor, explain each change in writing using the grammar words (first mention, general, unique). Early finishers do Editor B and then write a "trap" sentence with a correct but surprising article for the class to judge.

L1 watch-outs
  • No articles at all ("I bought phone. Phone is broken."): Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Russian, Polish, Ukrainian, Vietnamese, Thai, Hindi/Urdu, Turkish (bir ≈ one only), Swahili and many more. These learners often cannot hear the weak /ə/ at first. Be patient: noticing comes first, then accuracy. Tagalog markers (ang, ng, si) are not articles, so Filipino learners also drop or add them unpredictably.
  • Extra "the" for general ideas ("The life is hard." "I like the animals." "She studies the medicine."): Spanish, Portuguese, French, Italian, German and Arabic (al- works for general meaning, al-ḥayāt = "the life"). Teach "In English, general = no article."
  • No "a" with jobs ("She's doctor." "I want to be teacher."): Spanish soy doctora, Portuguese, French je suis médecin, German Ich bin Lehrer, Arabic (no indefinite article), Russian. Very frequent. Drill it.
  • "the France / the Brazil": French la France, Portuguese o Brasil, Italian l'Italia. Most countries = no article; only the USA, the UK, the Netherlands, the Philippines (plural or "united / republic / kingdom" names).
  • Meals and instruments: "have the breakfast" (French le petit-déjeuner, Spanish el desayuno), "play guitar" (fine in casual US English, so accept it; teach play the guitar as the standard).
  • a / an by spelling: "a hour, an university." Everyone, especially learners who read more than they listen. Always say it; don't look at it.
Homework

Online lesson 6.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_16.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them aloud, with weak articles. Do the three online exercises and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 6.1: finish Ex 5 and write "A place I know well" (8+ sentences, from the plan), "My new words" and the journal (one noun, three ways, with the reasons).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B She's a teacher at a big school. · Q2 B music … the music · Q3 (new) B an hour. Script 6.1-A (Ex 1): 1 a · 2 a · 3 the · 4 a · 5 the · 6 an · 7 an · 8 a · 9 The · 10 the · 11 — · 12 — · 13 —. Editor A (Ex 3): a group · an empty lot · the Maple Street · into a garden · the garden (not a) · also a bed · a volunteer · the tomatoes · The gardening · on the first Saturday · an expert (not a) · a hat (not the).

6.2 · C18

Gerunds & Infinitives — Verb Patterns

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use verb + -ing after enjoy, avoid, finish, suggest, keep, mind, can't stand, feel like, give up, miss, practice
  • use verb + to after want, decide, hope, promise, learn, offer, refuse, manage, agree, plan, need, can't afford
  • use verb + object + to: She told him to wait. The doctor advised her to rest.
  • use -ing after a preposition (good at drawing, looking forward to seeing you) and as the subject (Swimming is good for you.)
  • explain and use the meaning-change verbs: stop, remember, forget, try + -ing vs + to
  • talk for two minutes about their habits, plans and preferences with 10 different verb patterns
Materials
  • Workbook 6.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, the two-minute talk plan)
  • Slides 9–13 · Verb-sort cards (1 set per pair) · Meaning-change sentence cards (1 set per pair: 12 sentence cards + 12 meaning cards) · Find Someone Who (1 per learner) · Two-minute talk checker card (1 per pair) · a phone timer · new identity cards
  • Listening scripts 6.1-B (dictation, for the warmer) and 6.2-A (Which one did you hear?)

Left: three columns. + -ing: enjoy, avoid, finish, suggest, keep, mind… · + to: want, decide, hope, promise, offer, refuse, manage… · + person + to: tell / ask / advise / want someone to…

Center: always -ing: after a preposition (good at drawing · look forward to seeing you) · as a subject (Swimming is good for you.)

Right: the meaning changes! stop buying coffee (no more) ≠ stop to buy coffee (pause → in order to) · remember locking the door (a memory ←) ≠ remember to lock the door (a task →) · try restarting it (a test) ≠ try to open the jar (an effort)

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: dictation + fix8T–S → S–SReview C17. Read Script 6.1-B (5 sentences) at natural speed, each twice. Learners write them. Pairs compare and underline every article, then say why for two. Show the sentences on the board. Two volunteers read one sentence from their "A place I know well" homework.
Lead-in: my confessions5T–STell the class 4 true (or invented) facts: "I enjoy cooking, but I want to learn to bake bread. I stopped drinking soda last year. I keep forgetting my keys!" Learners ask one follow-up question each. Write two sentences on the board and ask: "After enjoy? After want?" Elicit -ing and to. Most learners know this from A2.
Verb sort: -ing or to?12S–S → T–SPairs get the verb-sort cards and sort 20 verbs into -ing / to (4 minutes, from memory and guessing). Check with slide 9. Then pairs make one true sentence with 4 of the cards. Leave the four "both" cards (stop, remember, forget, try) aside: "These are special. Later!" Drill (below).
Object + to, prepositions, -ing subject8T–SSlide 10. Verb + person + to: "The doctor said: 'You should rest.' → The doctor advised her to rest." -ing after a preposition, including the "to" trap (looking forward to seeing, be used to getting up): "Can you put a noun after it? Looking forward to the weekend. Yes, so -ing." -ing as a subject: "Learning a language takes time." CCQs.
Controlled practice 18S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1 (-ing or to) and Ex 3 (verb + object + to) alone, then pairs check. Board-check the items pairs disagree on, usually Ex 1 item 6 (looking forward to) and Ex 3 item 5 (warned him not to).
Meaning-change verbs15T–S → S–SSlide 11. Act out stop: walk across the room, "stop" and mime buying a coffee: "I stopped to buy a coffee." Then mime pushing coffee away: "I stopped buying coffee. Too expensive!" CCQs (below). Then remember / forget (memory ← or task →) and try (test or effort). Pairs match the meaning-change sentence cards to their meaning cards (5 min). Then Script 6.2-A: you read 8 sentences; learners circle the meaning in Workbook Ex 4.

One name per line; ask the extra question (9 min). Sit down: 3–4 learners report one surprising answer with he/she: "Tomás can't stand waiting in line. He always shops at night." (3 min). ICQs first.
Freer 2: the two-minute talk7S–SPairs. Speakers use their workbook plan (habits · plans · preferences, real or a new identity card). A talks for 2 minutes; B is the checker and ticks each verb pattern they hear on the checker card. Swap. Each checker tells the speaker their number: "You used 11 patterns!"
Feedback5T–SBoard: 4 heard sentences, e.g. "I'm looking forward to see my family." · "She told to wait." · "I enjoy to cook." · "I stopped to smoke" (said by someone who quit). Learners fix them. Praise the best checker score.

Total908 + 5 + 12 + 8 + 8 + 15 + 5 + 12 + 7 + 5 + 5
If you run long

This class has a lot of grammar. Do the verb sort with 12 cards instead of 20. Skip controlled practice 2 (set Ex 2 as homework; it's short). Never cut the meaning-change stage: stop / remember / try is the part learners remember and enjoy. If time is very short, choose either Find Someone Who or the two-minute talk, and do the other as the C19 warmer.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: two forms after a verb. The first verb decides. Many -ing verbs are about experiences and reactions (enjoy, avoid, mind); many to-verbs look forward (want, hope, plan, promise). It's a helpful idea, not a rule. Learn them in groups.

"I enjoy cooking." Is cooking an activity I like now, in general? (Yes.) · "I want to learn to bake." Now or in the future? (The future.) · "She told him to wait outside." Who waits? (He does.) Who gives the instruction? (She does.) · "He asked to help." Who helps? (He does.) "He asked me to help." Who helps now? (I do.)
"I'm looking forward to seeing you." Can I say "looking forward to the weekend"? (Yes.) So is this to part of a verb, or a preposition? (A preposition.) So…? (-ing.) · "Swimming is good for you." What's the subject of the sentence? (Swimming.)

Meaning: the four special verbs.

stop: "He stopped working last year." Does he work now? (No.) · "On the way home, he stopped to buy bread." Did he stop walking or driving? (Yes.) Why? (To buy bread.) Does to mean "in order to"? (Yes.)
remember / forget: "I remember locking the door." Did I lock it? (Yes.) Is it a memory? (Yes, the past.) · "Remember to lock the door." Did you lock it? (Not yet.) Is it a task for later? (Yes.) · "I forgot to call my mom." Did I call her? (No.)
try: "My laptop froze. Try restarting it." Is it difficult to restart a laptop? (No.) Is it a test, an idea to fix the problem? (Yes.) · "I tried to open the jar, but I couldn't." Easy or difficult? (Difficult.) Did I make an effort? (Yes.)

Form.

verb + -ingenjoy, avoid, finish, suggest, keep, mind, can't stand, feel like, give up, miss, practice, imagineDo you mind waiting? · He suggested going out.
verb + towant, decide, hope, promise, learn, offer, refuse, manage, agree, plan, need, can't afford, would likeWe managed to finish on time.
verb + person + towant, tell, ask, advise, allow, remind, invite, warn, encourage, teachI want you to listen. · She warned him not to touch it.
preposition + -inggood at, interested in, tired of, before, after, without, instead of, look forward to, be used toWe walked instead of taking the bus.
-ing as subject-ing + verb…Getting up early is hard in winter.
like / love / hate+ -ing or to (almost the same)I like swimming. = I like to swim.

Pronunciation. -ing is /ɪŋ/: one nasal sound at the back, no hard /g/ after it ("sing-g" is a common over-correction by Spanish and Polish speakers) and not /ɪn/ in careful speech. to is weak /tə/ and joins the verb: "want to" /ˈwɑntə/, "decided to" /dɪˈsaɪdɪd tə/. In "stopped to buy," the /t/ of stopped and to run together, so learners may hear "stop to buy." Stress carries the meaning: "I stopped to BUY coffee" / "I stopped BUYing coffee." Vietnamese and Cantonese speakers may drop the -ed and final consonants; clap the two beats: "stopped · to."

Drill script
  1. -ing or to, quick-fire: T: "enjoy / swim" → S: "I enjoy swimming." T: "decide / stay home" → S: "I decided to stay home." T: "mind / wait" → "Do you mind waiting?" T: "manage / finish" → "We managed to finish." Choral, then individuals, faster.
  2. Report the instruction: T: "Teacher to Ana: 'Open the window.'" → S: "The teacher told Ana to open the window." T: "Doctor to Omar: 'You should rest.'" → S: "The doctor advised Omar to rest." T: "Mom to me: 'Don't be late.'" → S: "My mom told me not to be late."
  3. Preposition + -ing chain: S1: "I'm good at cooking. What are you good at?" → S2: "I'm good at fixing bikes. What are you interested in?" → S3: "I'm interested in learning Korean. What are you looking forward to?" → S4: "I'm looking forward to seeing…"
  4. Two meanings, two gestures: T says a sentence; learners point back over the shoulder (memory) or forward (task): "I remember meeting her." (back) · "Remember to call her." (forward) · "I forgot to pay the bill." (forward: a task I didn't do) · "I'll never forget seeing the ocean." (back).
  5. Stop, act it: learners walk; T says "Stop to tie your shoe!" (they stop and tie) / "Stop walking!" (they freeze). Fun and fast; 1 minute.
Meaning-change sentence cards — how to run them
  • Each pair gets 12 sentence cards (6 pairs, e.g. "She stopped buying coffee." / "She stopped to buy a coffee.") and 12 meaning cards ("She doesn't buy coffee anymore." / "She paused on her way so she could buy one."). Spread them face up.
  • Pairs match each sentence to its meaning (5 min). The two sentences in a pair look almost the same, so learners must read the form carefully: -ing or to.
  • Check: one pair reads a sentence, another pair reads the meaning. Ask "Why?" and point at the board ("memory or task?", "test or effort?").
  • Round 2 (stretch or fast pairs): turn the meaning cards face down. A reads a sentence; B says the meaning without looking. Or A reads a meaning; B says the sentence.
  • Keep the cards for the C19 warmer (turn them over and play memory / pairs).
Find Someone Who & the two-minute talk — how to run them
  • Before the mingle, learners turn two prompts into questions with you: "enjoys cooking for other people" → "Do you enjoy cooking for other people?" · "remembers learning to ride a bike" → "Do you remember learning to ride a bike?" The -ing / to stays the same in the question. That's the point.
  • One name per line; ask each person 1–2 questions, then move on. A "No" is fine: "OK, thanks!" and ask someone else. Anyone can answer as their new identity card person, or say "Pass."
  • The two-minute talk: the speaker talks; the checker listens and ticks patterns on the card (enjoy + -ing, decide + to, told me + to, good at + -ing, a meaning-change verb, an -ing subject…). The checker does not correct. They only count. This keeps confident speakers honest and gives quiet learners a clear job.
  • Dominant speaker in a pair? The timer rules: at 2:00 the speaker stops, even mid-sentence. Then swap.
ICQs before the mingle and the talk
Mingle: Do you read the line, or make a question? (Make a question.) · "enjoys cooking": what's the question? ("Do you enjoy cooking…?") · "Yes": what do you do? (Write the name, ask the extra question.) · How many names for one person? (One.)
Talk: How long does A speak? (Two minutes.) · What does the checker do? (Listens and ticks the patterns.) · Does the checker correct? (No, only count.) · Must you talk about your real life? (No. A new identity card is fine.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with 6 verbs only: enjoy, like, can't stand (+ -ing) and want, need, would like (+ to), plus stop. In the verb sort, give 6 cards with a small picture next to the verb (😊 enjoy, 😖 can't stand, 🎯 want). In the mingle, give a sheet with the questions fully written; the learner reads the question and writes the first letter of the name. Talk target: 1 minute with 5 patterns, using the plan's sentence starters.

Stretch

Add regret (I regret not studying more) and need + -ing (My car needs washing) as "extra collection" verbs. In the two-minute talk, aim for 15 patterns, all four meaning-change verbs and two -ing subjects. Early finishers write 4 "true for me" pairs: "I stopped eating meat / I stopped to eat lunch at a gas station."

L1 watch-outs
  • Infinitive after a preposition and as a subject ("before to leave," "interested in learn," "To swim is good," "Swim is good for you"): Spanish, Portuguese, Italian and French use the infinitive here (antes de salir, interesado en aprender). Very common. Keep saying: "After a preposition: -ing. Always."
  • "I want that you listen" / "She said me to wait": Spanish quiero que escuches, Portuguese, French, Russian, Arabic. Drill "want you to, told me to."
  • "He suggested to go" / "I suggest you to go": German vorschlagen zu, Spanish sugerir, French. Teach suggest + -ing (or suggest that we go for stretch).
  • No -ing form at all ("I enjoy cook," "I finish read"): Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai (no verb endings). The -ing is a small sound: say it clearly and write it in color.
  • "I enjoy the swimming": Arabic uses a verbal noun with al- (the). Link to C17: "general activity = no article."
  • "Looking forward to see you": everyone, including very fluent speakers and many native writers. Use the noun test every time.
Homework

Online lesson 6.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_17.html. Listen to the model sentences and the Lena and Marco dialogue with 🔊 and read them aloud. Do the three exercises and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 6.2: Ex 5 (my verb collection: 5 -ing verbs, 5 to verbs, one true sentence each), "My new words," and the journal (one meaning-change verb, both versions, and the difference in your own words).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B I enjoy swimming in the sea. · Q2 B stopped to have · Q3 (new) B seeing. Script 6.2-A (Ex 4): 1 b · 2 a · 3 a · 4 b · 5 a · 6 a · 7 b · 8 b.

6.3 · C19

Degree & Quantity — Complaints & Compliments

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use too + adjective (a problem) and adjective + enough / enough + noun, with a to-infinitive result (It's too cold to swim. She's old enough to drive.)
  • choose too many (countable) or too much (uncountable)
  • add emphasis with so + adjective / adverb and such (a) + (adjective +) noun, plus … that + result
  • feel the difference between a few / a little (some, positive 🙂) and few / little (not many / not much, negative 🙁)
  • use all / most / some / none, with of before the / my / them
  • make a polite complaint and give a compliment, saying exactly what is wrong and what is good
Materials
  • Workbook 6.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, the role-play plan)
  • Slides 14–20 · degree & quantity flashcards + the 🙂 / 🙁 cards · something heavy or high (a full bag, a top shelf) · Complaints & Compliments A/B cards (1 set per pair) · Community Center problem-solving sheet (1 per group, for early finishers or as the alternative task) · C18 meaning-change cards for the warmer
  • Listening scripts 6.3-A (a restaurant review) and 6.3-B (happy or sad?)

Left: too hot (😖 a problem) · very hot (a fact) · hot enough (✔ OK) · enough money · too many cars (count) · too much noise (no count). Arrow: too cold to swim · old enough to drive.

Center: Noun after it? NO → so good / so quickly · YES → such a good movie / such kind people. + that + result: It was so hot that we left early.

Right: a 2×2 box: 🙂 a few friends / a little time · 🙁 few friends / little time (count / no count). Below: all → most → some → none · Most people (general) / Most of the people here · none of them.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: meaning-change memory7S–SReview C18. Pairs turn the meaning-change sentence cards and meaning cards face down and play memory: turn two, keep a match only if you can read it aloud and explain it. Then 2–3 learners share one sentence from their verb collection.
Lead-in: too heavy!5T–STry to lift a heavy bag, or reach a high shelf. "I can't! It's too heavy. I'm not strong enough." Then slide 14: learners describe the pictures (too hot, too expensive, too small, big enough). Ask: "Is 'too' good or bad?" (Bad: a problem.) "Can I say 'This cake is too good'?" (Not as praise. Say "so good.")
too / enough / too much / too many13T–SSlide 15. Position: too before the adjective, enough after an adjective but before a noun. Then too many (count it) / too much (you can't count it). CCQs and drill (below). Contrast very and too: "It's very hot. We can swim!" / "It's too hot. We can't go outside."
Controlled practice 15S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1 alone, then pairs check. Quick board check.
so / such … that10T–S → S–SSlide 16. The noun test. Read the Priya and Dan dialogue (also on slide 16) in two halves of the room. Then pairs make so / such … that sentences from prompts on the board: "tired / fell asleep" · "good movie / watched it twice" · "kind people / cried when we left."
(a) few / (a) little · all / most / some / none10T–SSlide 17. The 2×2 box, then Script 6.3-B: learners hold up 🙂 or 🙁 for 8 sentences. Then the scale all → most → some → none and the of rule: "Most people…" / "Most of the people here…" CCQs.
Controlled practice 27S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2 and Ex 3 alone, then pairs check. Board-check Ex 3 Part B (the of items).
Listening: a restaurant review6T–SWorkbook Ex 4. Read Script 6.3-A twice. Learners note two good things and three problems, and the exact words (so delicious that, such a friendly person, too small, not enough space). This gives the language for the role-play.
Freer: Complaints & Compliments17S–S → T–SSlides 18–19. Model the front-desk dialogue with a strong learner. (1) Pairs take situation cards (A = customer, B = staff). A reads the problems; B reads what they can offer. 1 minute to plan: no writing the whole dialogue (2 min). (2) Role-play: A complains politely and precisely; B apologizes and offers a solution; when it's fixed, A gives a compliment (4 min). (3) Swap roles with a new card (4 min). (4) New partner, a third card (4 min). (5) Two pairs perform for the class; the class listens for one too / enough and one so / such (3 min). ICQs first. Groups who finish early, or classes who prefer it, do the Community Center problem-solving task.
Feedback + module check5T–SDelayed correction: "The service was too good!" (praise) · "The room isn't enough big." · "It was so a long wait." · "Most of people were kind." Learners fix them. Then learners tick the Module 6 "I can…" lists.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 6.3 and the review project (write a review of a meal, a trip, a store, or a movie: 8+ sentences). Preview C20: "Next class: agreeing and disagreeing. Is it too easy to disagree in English?"
Total907 + 5 + 13 + 5 + 10 + 10 + 7 + 6 + 17 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Four small grammar points in one class is a lot. all / most / some / none is the easiest to shorten: teach only the of rule with two examples and leave the rest to Ex 3 Part B at home. Keep the 🙂 / 🙁 listening: it's fast and memorable. Don't cut the role-play below 12 minutes; do two rounds instead of three.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: too / enough / very. too = more than you want: there's a problem or a result you don't like. enough = the right amount. very = strong, but no problem.

"The coffee is too hot." Can I drink it now? (No.) Is it a problem? (Yes.) · "The coffee is very hot." Can I drink it? (Maybe, carefully.) · "She's old enough to drive." Can she drive? (Yes.) · "We don't have enough chairs." Can everyone sit down? (No.)
"There are too many cars." Can we count cars? (Yes: one car, two cars.) So…? (too many.) · "There's too much noise." Can we count noise? (No.) So…? (too much.) · "You worry too much." Is there a noun? (No, it's after a verb: too much.)

Meaning: so / such. Same meaning: "really, very." The difference is grammar: what comes after.

"It was such a good movie that I watched it twice." Was the movie good? (Very good.) What happened because of that? (I watched it twice.) Is there a noun after "such a good"? (Yes, "movie.") · "It was so good that I watched it twice." Is there a noun after "so good"? (No.)

Meaning: a few / few, a little / little.

"I have a few friends here." Some friends or no friends? (Some.) Am I OK? (Yes 🙂.) · "I have few friends here." Many or not many? (Not many, almost none.) How do I feel? (A bit lonely 🙁.) · "We have a little time." Hurry, or relax? (Relax. There's some time.) · "We have little time." (Hurry!) · Friends: countable or not? (Countable: few.) Time? (Uncountable: little.)
"Most people like music." All people in the world, generally? (Yes, in general.) Is there of? (No.) · "Most of the people in this room like music." General, or a specific group? (Specific: this room.) So…? (Add of.) · "None of them came." How many came? (Zero.)

Form.

tootoo + adjective / adverb (+ to…)It's too cold to swim. · He drives too fast.
enoughadjective + enough · enough + noun (+ to…)She's old enough to drive. · We don't have enough chairs.
too many / muchtoo many + plural noun · too much + uncountable noun · verb + too muchtoo many meetings · too much sugar · You worry too much.
soso + adjective / adverb (+ that…)I was so tired that I fell asleep. · She spoke so quietly that…
such (a)such a / an (+ adj.) + singular noun · such (+ adj.) + plural / uncountablesuch a long show · such kind people · such nice weather
(a) few / (a) little🙂 a few + plural · a little + uncountable · 🙁 few / little (= not many / not much)a few minutes · a little money · few people came · little time
all / most / some / none+ noun (general) · + of + the / my / this / them (specific)Most people agree. · Some of my friends came. · None of them.

Pronunciation. too is always stressed and long /tuː/: "It's TOO hot." (Compare weak to /tə/.) enough /ɪˈnʌf/: gh = /f/, stress on the second syllable. so and such carry the emphasis, so they are stressed and can be stretched: "It was sooo good!" such /sʌtʃ/ ends in "ch" (Spanish and Portuguese speakers may say "such" like "sush"). a few /ə ˈfjuː/: the /ə/ is tiny but it changes the meaning; say "a‿few" as one word and let learners hear it several times. For complaints, intonation matters more than grammar: a soft, rising start ("Excuse me… I'm sorry to bother you, but…") sounds polite; a flat, loud "The room is too cold." can sound angry even with perfect grammar.

Drill script
  1. too or enough? T shows a card and says the adjective: "hot tea, hot" → S: "It's too hot to drink." · "15 years old, old, drive" → S: "She isn't old enough to drive." · "small apartment, big" → S: "It isn't big enough." · "10 people, 6 chairs" → S: "We don't have enough chairs."
  2. many or much? T: "cars" → S: "too many cars." T: "sugar" → "too much sugar." T: "people" → "too many people." T: "homework" → "too much homework." T: "noise" → "too much noise." Fast.
  3. so or such? T: "tired" → S: "I was so tired!" T: "a long day" → S: "It was such a long day!" T: "kind people" → "They're such kind people!" T: "quickly" → "The time went so quickly!"
  4. Complaint → compliment: T: "the room / cold" → S: "Excuse me, the room is too cold." T: "the staff / helpful" → S: "The staff were so helpful. Thank you!" Practice the polite intonation, choral then individual.
  5. of or no of? T: "most / people" → "Most people…" T: "most / the people here" → "Most of the people here…" T: "none / them" → "None of them…"
Complaints & Compliments — how to run it
  • Six situation cards: a hotel room, a restaurant, a store return, a phone and internet company, a delivery order, and a community class. A (customer) has 3 problems as prompts (🥶 room / warm → "The room isn't warm enough."). B (staff) has what they can and can't offer ("You have a few rooms free on the 5th floor. None of them have a view.").
  • The polite complaint shape (on slide 18): 1 Excuse me / I'm sorry to bother you, but… 2 the exact problem (too / not … enough / too many) 3 a request: Could you…? / Would it be possible to…? B: 4 I'm so sorry about that. I'll… / Let me see what I can do. A: 5 a compliment when it's fixed (so / such … that).
  • Precision is the point. "The room is bad" is not enough; "The room is too cold, and there aren't enough towels for three people" gets a solution. Tell learners: "In real life, a precise complaint gets a faster answer."
  • The compliment is not optional. Many learners have learned complaining is rude or dangerous; the round ends with thanks and praise, so it feels safe and positive. It also practices so / such in a friendly way.
  • These are roles. Nobody needs to share a real complaint. If a situation card is uncomfortable (for example, the apartment or the phone company), the pair takes a different one. Some learners prefer to solve the problem as a group: give them the Community Center sheet (below).
Community Center problem-solving — the alternative task
  • Groups of 3–4. The sheet lists 8 complaints from members (too few chairs, not enough parking, the room is too cold in winter, too many people at the Saturday class…) and a budget of $1,500 with 9 possible fixes and their prices.
  • The group chooses the fixes to buy (the total must be $1,500 or less), then reports: "Most of the complaints are about the big room. It's too cold, so we'll buy a heater. There aren't enough chairs, so we're buying 20. We have a little money left for…"
  • Use it for early finishers, as a 12-minute replacement for round 3 of the role-play, or as the main freer task in a class that prefers group discussion to role-play. It is also a good "discussion" preview for Module 7.
ICQs before the role-play
Is A the customer or the staff? (The customer.) · Do you write the whole dialogue first? (No, just plan for a minute.) · Is "The room is bad" enough? (No, say exactly what: too cold, not enough towels.) · What does A say at the end? (A compliment: "You've been so helpful.") · Must you use a real complaint? (No. They're roles.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on too + adjective, not … enough and so + adjective. Use the picture flashcards (hot, cold, small, expensive) as prompts. In the role-play, give the phrase strip (on the role-play page) and the hotel card only; the partner or teacher reads the situation aloud. Target: 2 complaints ("It's too cold. It isn't big enough.") + 1 request ("Could you help me, please?") + 1 compliment ("You're so kind. Thank you!").

Stretch

Play the staff role with a difficult customer: B can't fix everything and must offer an alternative ("None of the rooms with a view are free, but I can give you a free breakfast."). Use so … that and such … that in every compliment, and a few / little pair. Early finishers write the online review of the place in 8 sentences, including most of / none of.

L1 watch-outs
  • "too" as praise ("The food was too delicious!" "You're too kind" meaning very kind): Chinese tài hǎo le 太好了, Tagalog sobra, Spanish demasiado (in some countries), Arabic, Hindi/Urdu bahut. It can sound like a complaint. Teach "so / really / very" for praise. (Note: "You're too kind" is a fixed polite phrase in English and is fine.)
  • "enough big" / "enough old": Spanish suficientemente grande, Portuguese grande o suficiente (OK) but bastante grande, French assez grand, Russian, Chinese gòu dà 够大 (enough before the adjective). Very common. Chant: "big enough, old enough, warm enough."
  • "so a nice day" / "such nice" (no noun): Spanish tan and French si / tellement work before adjectives and nouns. Use the noun test.
  • "much people" / "too much cars": Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Vietnamese, Tagalog, Arabic (no countable/uncountable grammar). Link to C17: "Can you put a number in front?"
  • "the most of people" / "most of people": Spanish la mayoría de la gente, Portuguese a maioria das pessoas, French la plupart des gens. Teach: general → no of; specific → of the.
  • Direct complaints: in some cultures a complaint is very indirect (learners may only say "It's a little cold, maybe…"), in others very direct ("This is bad. Change it."). Teach the English middle: soft start + exact problem + polite request.
Homework

Online lesson 6.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b1/lesson_18.html. Listen to the model sentences and the Priya and Dan dialogue with 🔊 and say them aloud. Do the three exercises and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 6.3: Ex 5 and the project "Write a review" (8+ sentences about a meal, a trip, a store, a class or a movie: one too, one enough, one too much / many, one so … that, one such (a) … that, one a few / a little, one most / some / none of), "My new words" and the journal (a few / few and a little / little about your own life).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B She isn't old enough to drive. · Q2 B such an … that · Q3 (new) B little. Script 6.3-B (🙂 / 🙁): 1 🙂 · 2 🙁 · 3 🙁 · 4 🙂 · 5 🙂 · 6 🙁 · 7 🙂 · 8 🙁. Script 6.3-A (Ex 4): good: the food was so delicious that they stayed three hours; the waiter was such a friendly person (he remembered their names) · problems: too small; too many tables / not enough space; too much noise; most of the desserts were sold out (only a few left) · 3 stars · she'd go back on a quiet Tuesday.

Closing the Module

End of C19: Module 6 wrap-up

Recap on slide 20, the three precision questions: Which one? (a / the / no article) → Which form? (-ing / to, and the meaning-change verbs) → How much? (too / enough, so / such, a few / few). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C20 warmer.

Keep it alive. Precision grows slowly. For the rest of the course, use a 2-minute "Editor" warmer once a week: write one sentence from learners' homework with an article or verb-pattern slip and let the class fix it. Keep the C17 board question ("Does my listener know which one?") on a poster on the wall.

Watch for in Module 7: discussion language (I agree / I'm not sure I agree / That's a good point, but…) is a natural place to recycle this module: too vs very when giving opinions ("That's too expensive for most people"), most of / some of in summaries ("Most of us think…"), and verb patterns (I suggest waiting, I refuse to believe…). The Community Center task is a good bridge.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 7 — Real-World Communication

Three practical speaking classes about using English in the situations adults care about. First, a structured discussion: giving opinions, agreeing, disagreeing politely and taking turns on everyday community topics. Then complaints and getting things fixed with stores, landlords and utility companies, in person, on the phone and by email, with polite escalation to a manager. Last, a job-interview lab: describing experience with the present perfect, STAR-style answers, and mock interview panels with observer checklists.

3 lessons Lessons 7.1–7.3 Classes C20–C22 Weeks 10–11 90 min per class CEFR B1

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (groups, role-plays, panels) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes. In this module the freer stage is the biggest stage of every class (20–23 minutes). Protect it: if you run late, cut controlled practice and give the rest for homework.
  • Present from slides/module-7-real-world.html. Print materials/module-7/classroom-materials.html before C20. It holds the flashcards, discussion topic cards, discussion role cards and talking tokens, Discussion Phrase Bingo, the complaint role-play cards (in person, phone, email), the candidate profile cards, interviewer question cards, the observer checklist and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences and dialogues again at home.
  • This is a functional module, not a new tense. The language is mostly fixed chunks (I see your point, but…; Would it be possible to…?; I'm responsible for…). Teach them as whole phrases and drill them for rhythm and polite intonation. The grammar inside them is recycled: the present perfect and present perfect continuous (1.1–1.2) for experience, the passive (4.1) for describing a problem without blame, and -ing after prepositions (6.2) for good at / responsible for / mind. Treat these as extension and contrast, not re-teaching.
  • Building on A2. A2 Module 7 practiced simple returns ("There's a problem with this jacket. Can I have a refund?"). The new B1 work is register (how polite, how formal), three channels (in person, phone, email), and polite escalation.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use some British forms: favourite, colour, apologise, organisation, organising, practise (verb), parcel, mobile, CV. Teach favorite, color, apologize, organization, organizing, practice, package, cell phone, résumé. Accept British forms when learners use them. Note: in online lesson 7.2 Exercise 1, the gap checker accepts only apologise. Tell learners that apologize is also correct.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Speak up → stand up for yourself → present yourself. In C20 learners take part in a fair group discussion: they give an opinion, agree, disagree without offending anyone, and invite quieter people in. In C21 they use the same softening language to get something fixed: a broken sink, a double charge, a faulty jacket. They open politely, describe the problem, ask for action and, if needed, ask for a manager. In C22 they present their own experience to an employer with confidence and proof (STAR stories). The same idea runs through all three classes: calm, polite and clear gets results. Say this at the start of every class.

B1 speaking classes: fluency first, then accuracy

During the freer tasks, do not stop learners to correct. Walk around with a notepad split into two columns: Great language I heard and Fix it together. Correct on the spot only if a mistake blocks meaning (the listener can't understand). After the task, give feedback in this order: (1) content ("Which group changed their mind? What was the best argument?"), (2) great language (read 2–3 good phrases aloud, no names, and praise them), (3) fix it together: 3–4 sentences on the board, no names, learners correct in pairs. This keeps confidence high and makes feedback useful.

Dominant and quiet speakers: make turn-taking fair

Every B1 class has one or two people who talk a lot and a few who never start. This module gives you tools. (1) Talking tokens (materials Set C): each person has three. They put one in the middle each time they speak. When your tokens are gone, you wait until everyone has used theirs. (2) Roles: give the most talkative learner the chair or observer role. Their job is to bring others in, not to speak. (3) Think time: give 1 minute of silent planning before every discussion, so slower speakers are ready. (4) Round first: each person says one sentence before open discussion starts. (5) Praise listening out loud: "Great, you asked Mai what she thinks." Never shame a strong speaker. Say "Hold that thought. Let's hear from someone new first."

Trauma-informed and safe: topics, problems and work histories

C20 topics are everyday community topics (public transport, working from home, phones in schools, city vs country, online shopping). Do not use politics, religion, immigration, or any topic about a learner's country or identity. If a learner brings one up, redirect kindly: "That's a big topic. Let's keep to our card today." C21: problems with a landlord or a utility company can be very real and very stressful (housing, shut-off notices, fear of trouble). The role-plays use fictional situation cards. Nobody has to share a real problem. This class teaches communication, not law. If a learner has a real housing, billing or safety problem, do not give legal advice. Connect them with your program's case manager or navigator, or local legal aid or a tenant helpline, after class. C22: some learners have gaps in their work history, qualifications that are not recognized here, or jobs they don't want to talk about. Say it every time: "Use your real experience or a candidate profile card. Both are good." Caregiving, volunteering, farming, a family business and studying all count as experience.

Before the Module

  • Print the picture flashcards (one set for the board): topics (7.1), problems (7.2) and jobs (7.3), plus the word-only phrase cards. Card stock helps.
  • For C20: one set of discussion topic cards and one set of role cards (chair, timekeeper, observer, summarizer) per group of 4–5. Cut talking tokens (3 per learner), or use coins, paper clips or dried beans. One Discussion Phrase Bingo card per observer (cards A and B are different).
  • For C21: one set of complaint role-play cards per pair (in person and phone), the email task cards, and the escalation cards (the "I'm afraid I can't do that" staff cards). Set up the room as three stations: a "counter" (a desk), "phone" chairs back to back, and a writing table.
  • For C22: one candidate profile card set on a side table, interviewer question cards per trio, and three observer checklists per learner (one per round). Ask learners in C21 to bring a real job posting to C22 if they want (from a job board, a store window or your program's job developer). Bring 3–4 printed postings for people who don't have one.
  • If your program has a career services team, job developer or WIOA workforce partner, invite them to be a "guest interviewer" for 20 minutes in C22. Learners love a real interviewer, and it builds a link to services.
  • A toy or real phone for the C21 phone demo helps. Back-to-back chairs make phone role-plays realistic (no lip-reading, no gestures).
